# Manuscript Binding: Magl. A.6.36

->**Front Cover**<-

The exterior of this volume is bound in aged <term>vellum</term> <note>a high-quality writing surface or binding material made from specially prepared animal skin, typically calf, lamb, or kid</note>. The material has taken on a cream-colored hue over the centuries, though it now displays significant brown <term>foxing</term> <note>age-related spots and browning seen on old paper and parchment, often caused by humidity or metallic impurities in the material</note> and mottling across the entire surface.

The spine, visible along the right edge, is constructed from a darker shade of tan <term>parchment</term>. It bears several faint characters hand-inked in a vertical orientation, including the letters **G**, **T**, and what appears to be an **M** or **N**. The edges of the internal paper block are visible at the top, showing the thickness of the gathered leaves within.

<summary>This page displays the physical exterior of manuscript Magl. A.6.36, featuring a foxed vellum binding and a parchment spine with faint ink markings. It serves as the protective housing for the documents contained within the volume.</summary>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page describes a rare 1480 edition of Gaffurio's "Theorica Musice," noting its bibliographic rarity and physical characteristics.</meta>

Franchino Gaffurio of Lodi’s Theory of
Music: Naples, by Francesco
di Dino the Florentine, 1480, in quarto<gloss>a book size where the printer's sheet is folded twice to create four leaves</gloss>

This exceedingly rare edition is recorded
by Maittaire <note>Michael Maittaire (1668–1747), a famous bibliographer of early printed books</note> on page 155 of Volume I,
but it is entirely unknown to other bibliographers.
Sassi, on page 345 of his *Typographical 
History of Milan*, speaks extensively of 
Gaffurio and his works, but gives no 
indication of knowing this edition of 
Gaffurio. Nor does Malegulo, 
who was his student, mention it, whereby <unclear>it follows</unclear>
with a brief description.

The work begins without any preface 
or letter, starting with the heading or 
title in capital letters: **Chapter One: 
On the Emotional Effects and Commendation of Music.**
<margin>in which the author speaks in praise of music and lists many ancients who devoted themselves to it</margin>
The first initial is missing, 
but there is a blank space left so it could be supplied by hand <note>Early printers often left spaces for decorative initials to be painted in later by an artist</note>.
The typeface is Roman <note>original: "rotondo"</note> without diphthongs, 
featuring many abbreviations, simple 
punctuation, no page numbering, and no 
register <gloss>a list of the book's quires or sections provided for the bookbinder</gloss>. The work is divided into five books, 
and the books into chapters. On page 13 verso<gloss>the back side of a leaf</gloss>
is a curious woodcut depicting 
six people striking hammers on an 
anvil to indicate musical consonances <note>This refers to the legend of Pythagoras discovering the mathematical ratios of musical intervals by listening to the weight of hammers in a blacksmith's forge</note>. On the back 
of page 28 is another figure indicating 
arithmetic and geometric proportions; 
the same appears on the back of page 46, at page 
51, and at page 96, where there is an engraving of an organ 
with a figure playing it, including musical 
notes and the title **F. Gaffurio’s Introduction 
to Music** <note>original: "Introductorium Musice F. Gafuri"</note>; this woodblock 
is likewise found in the treatise *The Practice 
of Music* by the same author, 
printed in Brescia in 1502, in folio<gloss>a large book size where the sheet is folded only once</gloss>.

<summary>This page provides a detailed bibliographic description of a rare 1480 Neapolitan edition of Franchino Gaffurio's "Theorica Musice," highlighting its absence from major 18th-century histories and its specific woodcut illustrations.</summary>
<keywords>Franchino Gaffurio, music theory, incunabula, Francesco di Dino, musical woodcuts, Pythagoras, bibliography, pipe organ, early printing</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the bibliographical description of Franchino Gaffurio's 1480 Neapolitan edition of his treatise on music theory, focusing on the potential dedicatee and providing the text of the colophon.</meta>

I suspect <insert>rather</insert> that a formal dedication might be missing—not one addressed to Ludovico Sforza, to whom Gaffurio dedicated <insert>a great portion of his works</insert>, but perhaps to a certain Cardinal. This is suggested by the author’s own epigram found at the end of the treatise:

> O you, to whom it was permitted to reach the cardinal’s honor,
> And me, whom virtue drove to venerate you,
> Accept these gifts, small though they be,
> Worthy of such a man: in whose name the work rises.
> No reader will judge these small things of the soul
> If you yourself pluck the fruit of my study.
> Come then: embrace me and the book with a grateful face;
> Do not be reluctant to say: You shall be my Servant.
<note>original: "Occui cardineum licuit contingere honorem / Et me quem virtus impulit ut venerer / Accipe parva licet sint haec haud munera tanto / Digna viro: cuius nomine surgit opus / Ista et animi nullus censebit parvula Lector / Si Studii fructum carpseris ipse mei / En age: me et librum vultu complectere grato / Dicere ne pigeat: Tu mihi Servus eris."</note>

This Cardinal could be Carlo Pallavicino, then Bishop of Lodi, by whom Gaffurio was invited and with whom he stayed for three years. During this time, he taught music to the youth, and it was precisely then that he began to write his *Pratica di Musica* <note>The Practice of Music</note>.

The book ends with this subscription <note>a statement at the end of an early printed book giving details of its publication, also known as a colophon</note>:

> The theoretical work on the discipline of harmony by Franchino Gaffurio of Lodi, Professor of Music, ends. Printed in Naples by Master Francesco di Dino the Florentine. In the year of our Lord 1480. On the eighth day of October. During the reign of the most unconquerable King Ferdinand. In the twenty-third year of his reign.
<note>original: "Franchini Gafuri Laudensis Musice / Professoris theoricum opus armonie / discipline explicit. Impressum Neapoli / per Magistrum Franciscum di dino Flo- / rentinum. Anno Dni. M.CCCC.LXXX. / Die octavo octobris. Invictissimo Rege / Ferdinando regnante. Anno regni ejus vi- / gesimo tertio."</note>

I do not find this treatise printed anywhere else in the 15th century, not even in Milan, where in 1508 his Italian treatise on music was printed <insert>by Gotardo da Ponte</insert>; nor in Brescia, where in 1502 his *Pratica Musicæ* was printed <insert>by Bernardino Misinta of Pavia</insert>; nor likewise in Milan, where in 1518 <insert>by Filippo Giardo of Pavia</insert> was <unclear>printed</unclear> printed his *Treatise on the Harmony of Musical Instruments* <note>De Harmonia Musicorum</note>.

<summary>The author identifies a possible missing dedication to Cardinal Carlo Pallavicino and provides the details of the book's 1480 Neapolitan printing, noting its uniqueness compared to later editions from Milan and Brescia.</summary>
<keywords>Franchino Gaffurio, music theory, incunabula, Carlo Pallavicino, Naples printing, Francesco di Dino, Ludovico Sforza, Renaissance musicology</keywords>

--- Page 16 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical and bibliographical account of Franchino Gaffurio, moving from his early works mentioned on the previous page to details about his origins, death, and primary biographical sources.</meta>

Gaffurio was of Bergamasque origin <note>meaning his family hailed from Bergamo</note>, though he was born <gloss>in Lodi</gloss> in 1451. He was a priest; he died at an advanced age, but the precise year of his death cannot be determined. His *Life* <note>referring to a biographical account</note>, drawn from the writings of his pupil Pantaleone Melegulo, can be found printed at the end of his treatise *On the Harmony of Musical Instruments* <note>original title: "De Harmonia Musicorum"</note> in the aforementioned 1518 Milan edition. It is also treated extensively by Saxio on page 345 <note>original: "CCCXLV"</note> and the following pages of his *Typographical History of Milan* <note>original title: "Storia Tipografica Milanese"</note>.

<summary>This page provides biographical details for the music theorist Franchino Gaffurio, noting his birth in Lodi and identifying key historical sources for his life story.</summary>
<keywords>Franchino Gaffurio, Lodi, biography, Pantaleone Melegulo, On the Harmony of Musical Instruments, Saxio, Typographical History of Milan</keywords>

--- Page 22 ---
<meta>The transcription begins on page 2, following a title or introductory section. The text is a Renaissance-era treatise on the value and history of music, likely part of a larger humanist work. The handwriting "Benedicti varchi" suggests a connection to the Florentine humanist Benedetto Varchi.</meta>

<insert>Of Benedetto Varchi</insert>

# ON THE EFFECTS AND COMMENDATION
# OF MUSIC, Chapter One.

Through the reading of long-term study, I have discovered that in ancient times, the discipline of music was embraced with the greatest honor among the most learned seers <note>The author uses "vates," suggesting a connection between poets, prophets, and musicians</note>, while those who were unlearned were considered far from the Muses. <margin>⚬—</margin>**For a man was hardly called liberally educated** <margin>⚬—</margin>**who was ignorant of music.** Themistocles <note>A famous Athenian statesman</note>, <margin>⚬—</margin>when a lyre was offered to him after dinner at a banquet, blushed in great disgrace when he admitted he was unskilled in it. Furthermore, the sacred scriptures <note>original: "littere sacre," referring to the Bible</note> have taught me that Jubal <note>original: "tubalē," referring to the descendant of Cain mentioned in Genesis 4:21 as the father of all who play the harp and flute</note> first became famous for the kithara and the organ. Others have written that Zethus and Amphion <note>Twin brothers from Greek mythology; Amphion was said to have moved stones with his music to build the walls of Thebes</note> were the first musicians. The Greek commentaries agree that Pythagoras first grasped the courses and sounds of the stars through musical proportion <term>musica ratione: the concept of "Musica Universalis" or the Music of the Spheres</term>, by means of a certain divine and supernatural investigation. <margin>⚬—</margin>**Archytas and Aristoxenus stated that grammar is subordinate to music.** <margin>⚬—</margin>**Eupolis taught both music and letters. And Maricas** <note>A character in a lost play by Eupolis</note>, <margin>⚬—</margin>**who is Hyperbolus, confessed that he knew only letters among the musical arts.** <margin>⚬—</margin>**Aristophanes showed in more than one book that boys were educated in this way in ancient times.** <margin>⚬—</margin>**Socrates, though he had already been an old man for a long time, did not blush to be taught music.** <margin>⚬—</margin>**Poets composed their songs to the lyre.** Orato[rs]...

<image-desc>Circular library stamp of the "BIBLIOTECA NAZIONALE FIRENZE" (National Library of Florence) containing a central shield with a cross.</image-desc>

<summary>This page argues for the essential role of music in a classical education, citing its biblical origins with Jubal and its prestige among Greek philosophers and statesmen like Pythagoras and Socrates. It emphasizes that in antiquity, music was considered a foundational discipline superior even to grammar.</summary>
<keywords>music theory, liberal arts, classical education, Pythagoras, Socrates, Jubal, Greek philosophy, history of music</keywords>

--- Page 23 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of music's historical importance, moving from its origins and its relationship to the stars and grammar to its practical application in oratory, psychology, and medicine. It follows the previous page’s mention of Eupolis.</meta>

...care was given to the voice, which is so characteristic of music? <note>This sentence appears to conclude a thought from the previous page regarding the education of orators</note> When Gaius Gracchus <note>A famous Roman reformer and orator</note> was delivering an oration before the Roman people during his tribunate, he kept a servant behind him who was skilled in the musical art. This servant, using an ivory flute, would secretly set the tones for Gracchus's speech, either by rousing him when he was too subdued or calling him back when he was more agitated than was proper. This was necessary because the heat and impetus of the performance did not allow the orator himself to be a focused judge of this moderation.

Timotheus <note>A celebrated Greek musician and poet</note>, far surpassing all other musicians, whenever he wished, would inflame the minds of men with a vehement harmony <margin>at a banquet</margin> and then soothe them again with a soft and peaceful one. Indeed, when he played a Phrygian song <term>Phrygian mode: an ancient musical scale often associated with excitement, passion, and martial spirit</term> at a banquet for Alexander <note>Alexander the Great</note>, he is said to have stirred the king so much that he leaped up to seize his arms; yet, by changing the melody, he led him back again to the festivities and the feast. 

The Pythagoreans <note>Followers of the philosopher Pythagoras</note> calmed the mind of a raging youth with melodies while he was attempting to burn down the house of a rival in which a courtesan was being kept. <margin>he rescued</margin> David rescued Saul from an unclean spirit by the art of musical modulation <note>Referring to the biblical account in 1 Samuel 16:23, where David's harp-playing soothes King Saul</note>. Asclepiades the physician <note>A Greek physician who practiced in Rome and advocated for music as a medical treatment</note> often restored the minds of the frenzied, which had been disturbed by disease, back to health through symphony <term>symphonia: here used in the ancient sense of harmonious or concordant sounds used for healing</term>. <margin>Terpander</margin> Terpander and Arion of Lesbos, as well as the Ionians, rescued people from the most serious diseases through the protection of song. Aristotle teaches that music possesses a natural delight and is a friend to every age and to individual characters...

<summary>This page details the "power of music" (musica humana and instrumentalis) through historical anecdotes, showing how it was used to control oratory, influence the emotions of kings like Alexander, and heal physical or mental illnesses.</summary>
<keywords>Gaius Gracchus, Timotheus, Alexander the Great, Phrygian mode, Pythagoras, David and Saul, Asclepiades, music therapy, Terpander, Arion, Aristotle</keywords>

--- Page 24 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of music's psychological and physical power. Having just described how David used music to soothe King Saul, the text now expands on how music affects people of all ages, animals, and even inanimate objects, citing both philosophers and classical mythology.</meta>

[Music] [soothes] <note>The Latin text is fragmented here, likely "Mulcet" or "Placuit," meaning it soothes or pleases</note> the sorrowful, gladdens the joyful, and makes them rejoice more. As music pours its efforts <margin><unclear>notes of human speech</unclear></margin> through all things, it joins young and old so naturally by a certain spontaneous affection for musical modes that there is absolutely no age that is separate from the delight of sweet song. For we read that Chrysippus <note>A Greek Stoic philosopher (c. 279–206 BCE) who believed music was essential for moral education</note> assigned a specific song to the nurses who are employed for the suckling of infants. It is told as a fable that Orpheus <note>A legendary hero in Greek mythology whose music was so powerful it could charm even stones and rivers</note> and Amphion <note>The son of Zeus who was said to have built the walls of Thebes by playing his lyre so beautifully that the stones moved into place themselves</note> drew animals lacking reason, rocks, and forests with their songs. For we read of no heart so <margin>fierce</margin> fierce and so harsh that it is not moved by the affection of such delights. For perhaps the first [musicians] drew barbarian peoples—who were without the cultivation of reason or were like stone, softened by no affection—to the feeling of pleasure by singing. 

And it is no wonder if there is such great dominion of music among men when even birds—such as goldfinches <term>cardueles: a small, brightly colored bird prized for its song</term>, swans, and nightingales, and others—practice song as if by a certain discipline of art. Some land or water beasts also run into nets of their own accord when invited by song, which is shown by the authority of the wise man:
<note>original: "Fistula dulce canit uolucrē dum decipit auceps"</note>
"The pipe <term>fistula: an ancient shepherd's flute made of reeds</term> sings sweetly while the bird-catcher deceives the bird." 
Song also encourages rowers to endure whatever labors. And the modulation of the voice comforts the fatigue of each person's work and soothes the mind. Furthermore, [it draws] even the beasts themselves, as well as serpents, birds, and dolphins, to sweet modulation...

<summary>This page explores the universal power of music, arguing that it affects everyone from infants to the elderly and even extends to charming wild animals and laborers.</summary>
<keywords>Chrysippus, Orpheus, Amphion, animal behavior, bird song, manual labor, psychological effects of music, fables</keywords>

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of music's power over the natural and human world. The previous page ended with a description of how animals, like birds and beasts, are drawn to musical sounds; this page concludes that thought by referencing the myth of Arion before moving into the historical and social uses of music among the Romans and in military life.</meta>

...calls forth the [attention] of their hearing. <note>The word "tiois" is the end of a word like "attentionis," continuing from the previous page's mention of animals being drawn to sound.</note> When **Arion** <note>A legendary Greek poet and musician; according to myth, when he was forced overboard by sailors, his music drew a dolphin which carried him to safety.</note> was plunged into the waves, the dolphins—called by their very nature—brought the cithara-player <note>original: "Citharizantem Arioneʒ"</note> safely to the shore. Song delights the mind and excites the emotions; it stirs up passion, yet it also calms the wild, tames the fierce, and easily enables those who are weary or held back by laborious exercise to tolerate their toil.

I shall certainly not omit the elders among the **Romans**, who at banquets used to sing songs <margin>to imitate</margin> encompassing the outstanding deeds of their ancestors to the accompaniment of **pipes** <term>tibias: ancient Roman flutes or reed instruments</term>, so that they might make the virtue of the young more eager to imitate those deeds. A lyre or cithara was passed around at banquets, and for each of the guests reclining there, a specific type of convivial song was arranged—a practice which we see preserved even in our own times. What more can be said? In this current age of ours, nothing indeed excites and provokes the feelings of young men with bold love, nor almost all honest maidens and women, as much as music. 

Likewise, we read that even among the unrefined and the warlike, and even among the greatest leaders, a care for music was not lacking. The army of the **Lacedaemonians** <note>The Spartans, famous for their military prowess and their use of music to maintain order and courage in battle.</note> was stirred by musical modes. What else do the drums and trumpets do in our own legions? The <margin>the more</margin> <margin>the more</margin> more vehement their **harmony** <term>concentus: the blending of voices or instruments in unison or harmony</term> is, the stronger the spirit of the warriors becomes for the struggle.

<summary>This page explores music's diverse powers, from the mythical rescue of Arion by dolphins to its historical role in Roman banquets and Spartan warfare. It emphasizes music's ability to inspire virtue in youth, stir romantic passion, and embolden soldiers on the battlefield.</summary>

## On Worldly Music, Chapter Two

Several philosophers have written that music plays a role in the composition of the rotation of celestial bodies, of time, and of the elements. For the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of the Greek philosopher Pythagoras, who believed numbers and proportions were the foundation of the universe.</note> explained music as arising from the sounds and consonances <note>Harmonious musical intervals.</note> caused by the rotation of the celestial spheres. Indeed, the celestial bodies themselves, as well as the elements, mixed together through a certain proper fitting of harmony, constitute this whole world, which according to the philosophers is always in motion. 

How, therefore, can it be that such a swift "machine of the heavens" moves in a silent and quiet course? And although that sound does not reach our ears—which, according to the **Pythagoreans**, happens because it has been ingrained in us from the very beginning of our birth—it is similar to what is seen with blacksmiths <note>original: "maleatoribus"; literally "those who work with hammers."</note>, who, through habit, scarcely perceive the noise of their own work. 

**Aristotle** <note>The influential Greek philosopher (384–322 BC).</note> disputes their view in the second book of *On the Heavens* <note>original: "caeli & mundi"</note>, yet it is necessary for this [sound] to exist for many other reasons. For such a very swift motion of such great bodies cannot fail to stir any sounds at all, especially since the courses of the stars are joined with such a fitting connection that nothing else so perfectly joined, nothing so well-mixed, could [be found] in...

<summary>This page introduces the concept of "Musica Mundana" (Worldly Music or Music of the Spheres), arguing that the rapid movement of celestial bodies must produce a divine harmony, even if humans are too accustomed to the sound to hear it.</summary>

<keywords>Musica Mundana, Music of the Spheres, Pythagoras, Aristotle, celestial bodies, harmony, cosmology, elements</keywords>

...to be understood. For some spheres are carried higher, others lower; and thus they all revolve with an equal impulse, so that a fixed order of paths is guided through diverse inequalities. Therefore, a fixed order of modulation <note>original: "modulationis"; here referring to the mathematical arrangement or "tuning" of the heavens.</note> cannot be absent from this celestial rotation.

<margin><image-desc>A manicule (pointing hand) in the left margin draws attention to the mention of Cicero.</image-desc></margin>
Indeed, Cicero <note>original: "Tullius," referring to the Roman statesman Marcus Tullius Cicero.</note> says most learnedly that this modulation delighted the ears of Plato—reflecting the depth of Plato's own words. "What," I ask, "is this sound, so great and so sweet, that fills my ears?" <note>This is a famous quote from Cicero’s "Dream of Scipio," where the narrator hears the music of the spheres.</note> "This is it," he said, "which, separated by unequal intervals, yet distinguished by a fixed proportion of parts, is generated by the impulse and motion of those spheres." Furthermore, the diversities and contrary powers of the four elements <note>The ancient building blocks of the universe: Earth, Air, Fire, and Water.</note> would by no means come together into one body and machine unless a certain harmony <note>original: "armonia"</note> joined them.

Indeed, all this diversity produces such a variety of seasons and fruits that it forms the single body of one year. Hence, if you should <gloss>separately</gloss> **pluck away** <note>original: "decerpas"</note> in mind and thought any of these things which provide such great variety to reality, they would all perish, nor would they necessarily preserve—if I may say so—anything consonant <note>In musical agreement or harmony.</note>. And just as in low strings <note>original: "cordis"</note> and voices, a motion exists so that the low pitch does not descend all the way to silence, and in high pitches, that measure of sharpness is guarded so that the strings, stretched too far, do not break from the thinness of the sound,

but rather that the whole may be **consistent and fitting** with itself.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Musica Mundana" (Worldly Music), moving from the general concept of celestial sound to the specific mathematical and philosophical theories regarding the distances between heavenly bodies. So also in the music of the world, it is provided that nothing is so **excessive** that it dissolves another by its own particular excess. Truly, whatever that [force] is, it either brings forth its own fruits or assists others so that they may bring them forth; for that which winter constricts, spring relaxes, summer scorches, and autumn ripens. Therefore, the seasons in turn either bring forth their own fruits themselves or supply the means for others to bring them forth.

Thus the world is composed by a certain <term>harmony</term> <note>original: "armonía"; the author uses the term here to mean a mathematical and musical fitting-together of parts.</note>, and the heaven itself is revolved by the modulation of harmony. However, the intervals of the celestial bodies are estimated by **reason alone**, not by the senses. For **Archimedes** <note>The famous Greek mathematician and astronomer.</note>, as **Macrobius** <note>A late Roman author whose commentary on the "Dream of Scipio" was a primary source for medieval science and music theory.</note> testifies, believed that he had discovered the number of stadia <note>A "stadium" was an ancient unit of distance, roughly 600 feet.</note> by which the Moon is distant from the surface of the earth;

<margin><image-desc>manicule pointing right</image-desc></margin> from the Moon, Mercury; from Mercury, Venus; from Venus, the Sun; from the Sun, Mars; from Mars, Jupiter; and from Jupiter, Saturn. He even thought that he had measured by reason the entire space from the orb of Saturn all the way to the star-bearing heaven itself. Indeed, the **Platonists** <note>Followers of Plato's philosophy, who emphasized mathematical proportions in the universe.</note> repudiated this measurement of Archimedes, because it did not preserve the **double and triple** intervals <note>Musical ratios of 2:1 (the octave) and 3:1 (the octave plus a fifth), which Platonists believed governed the spacing of the planets.</note>. They established that another distance must be maintained, which **Porphyry** <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher and scholar.</note> inserted into his books.

**Pythagoras** <note>The semi-legendary philosopher who first related musical pitch to mathematical ratios.</note>, however, indicated how many stadia there were between the earth and each individual star through **musical comparison**. **Eratosthenes** <note>original: "therastothenes"; the Greek polymath who famously calculated the Earth's circumference.</note> gathered by geometric reason that the maximum circumference of the earth was 252,000 stadia. For in this measurement of the world, they understood a stadium to consist of 625 <note>The OCR reads ".ccccc. xxv." (525), but historical context and the source text of Macrobius indicate 625 feet.</note> feet. There are, besides, other long...

...[differing] in distance, such as the Olympic stadium, which is six hundred feet. Likewise, the Pythic stadium is one thousand feet. Therefore, **Pythagoras** <note>The influential Greek philosopher and mathematician who first linked musical intervals to mathematical ratios.</note> believed that from the Earth to the Moon is a distance of approximately one hundred and twenty-six thousand <term>stadia</term> <note>original: "stadiorum"; an ancient Greek unit of length.</note>, and that this constitutes the interval of a **tone** <note>original: "tonus"; the basic unit of distance in this celestial musical scale.</note>. From the Moon to the star of Mercury—which they used to call **Stilbon** <note>original: "stilbon," meaning "The Gleaming One."</note>—is a greater **semitone** <note>original: "semitonium"; literally a "half-tone," though in ancient tuning, semitones could vary in size.</note>. From there to the star of Venus, called **Phosphoros** <note>original: "phosphoron," meaning "The Light-Bringer."</note>, it is almost as much, specifically a minor semitone. From there further to the Sun, it is a little more than three times as much, namely a tone and a minor semitone. Thus, the star of the Sun is distant from the Earth by three tones and a minor semitone, 
<margin>a fifth</margin>
which is called a **diapente** <note>original: "diapente"; the Greek musical term for the interval of a perfect fifth.</note>. 
<margin>a fourth</margin>
From the Moon, however, it is two tones and a semitone, which is a **diatessaron** <note>original: "diatessaron"; the Greek musical term for the interval of a perfect fourth.</note>. From the Sun to the star of Mars, whose name is **Pyrois** <note>original: "pyrois," meaning "The Fiery One."</note>, there is the same amount of distance as from the Earth to the Moon, and that makes a tone. From here to the star of Jupiter,

## Chapter Three: On Human Music

<margin>
you may effect
</margin>
The **Pythagoreans** and **Platonists** <note>Followers of Pythagoras and Plato, who believed the universe and the human soul were governed by the same mathematical and musical ratios.</note> handed down the tradition that the conjunction of the immortal soul, the human body, and the elements was made by musical reason. For every man, descending into his own self, may comprehend this. For what is it that mixes that incorporeal vitality of reason with the body, if not a certain fitting-together and a tempering—as if of high and low voices—producing, as it were, a single **consonance**? What else is it that joins the parts of the soul itself together, which (as **Aristotle** <note>The Greek philosopher who, in his *De Anima*, discussed the division of the soul into rational and irrational faculties.</note> pleases) is joined from the rational and the irrational? Indeed, what is it that mixes the elements of the body...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Human Music" (musica humana), specifically exploring how the same musical ratios that govern the stars also bind the human soul to the physical body. It references the works of Boethius and Macrobius to explain that life itself is a form of "consonance" that ends when these mathematical proportions fail.</meta>

...mixes <note>The text continues from the previous page: "mixes that incorporeal vitality of reason with the body."</note> or holds the parts together in a fixed fitting-together. For just as **consonance** in sounds is a mixture of high and low sound reaching the ears sweetly and uniformly—as **Boethius** <note>original: "Boetii"; referring to Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 477–524 AD), whose "De institutione musica" was the standard music theory textbook of the Middle Ages.</note> states in the first book of his *Music*—so the proper fitting-together or agreement of the parts of any composite thing is called **consonance**. 

<margin>
What is this form?
</margin>
And this indeed has its place both in things that follow one another <note>original: "successivis"; referring to things that happen in sequence, like the notes of a melody.</note> and in things that endure <note>original: "permanentibus"; referring to things that exist all at once, like the proportions of a physical body.</note>. Therefore, when we receive that which is aptly and suitably joined in sounds, since it is joined and suitably fitted to us, we are delighted by it, recognizing that we ourselves are constructed with that same likeness. 

Truly, among the Greeks there was such great devotion to music that they believed it even held power over souls. Indeed, through its "touch," it has often repressed fits of anger and has performed many wonders in the passions of bodies and souls. 

When, indeed, souls are allied with bodies by a certain established ratio of **numbers** <note>original: "numerorum ratione"; the author refers to the Pythagorean idea that the soul's relationship to the body is defined by mathematical proportions.</note>—a point which **Macrobius** <note>A 5th-century Roman writer whose commentary on the "Dream of Scipio" was a primary source for Neoplatonic philosophy and music theory.</note> also maintains in the first book of the ***Dream of Scipio***. As long as these numbers remain, the body continues to be animated; but when they fail, that secret force by which the alliance itself stood is soon dissolved. This is what they used to call **Fate** and the **fated times of life**. 

The soul itself, to be sure, does not fail, since it is immortal and perpetual. But when the numbers are fulfilled, the body cracks open <note>original: "fatiscit"; can mean to gape, crack, or grow weak with age.</note>, yet the soul [is not] wearied...

<summary>This page explains "Human Music" as the harmonious numerical relationship between the soul and the body, citing Boethius and Macrobius. It argues that life continues as long as these mathematical ratios endure, and "Fate" is simply the completion of these musical numbers.</summary>

<keywords>Human Music, Boethius, Macrobius, soul and body, consonance, mathematical ratios, Fate, immortality of the soul</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of "Human Music" (musica humana), specifically how the mathematical ratios of the soul animate the physical body and what happens when those ratios fail.</meta>

...animating, but the soul deserts its duty to the body when the body can no longer be animated. From this comes that saying of the most learned poet <note>Likely referring to a classical poet like Virgil or Ovid, though the specific quote is a philosophical adaptation.</note>: "From **Phoebus** <note>original: "phœbo"; Phoebus Apollo, the god of light, music, and the sun, often symbolizing the source of life or the rational soul.</note> I return the number to the shadows." <note>This implies that when the "number" or musical ratio of life is lost, the soul departs the light of life for the darkness of death.</note> 

Thus, just as the soul is joined to the body by **numbers**, so also by musical **harmonies**—which are produced from those very numbers—we handle them by a natural disposition and a certain agreeable fitting-together. We do not doubt that not only is the soul mingled with the body, but also the physical elements and their impulses are joined in this way. 

From this, I believe, comes the saying of **Herophilus** <note>original: "Hierophili"; Herophilus of Chalcedon (c. 335–280 BC), an ancient Greek physician who was one of the first to measure the pulse using a water clock.</note>, the most famous master of the medical art: "When we speak, or are moved inwardly by the pulses of the veins, it is proven to be fostered by the virtues of harmony through musical rhythms." 

**Human Music** <term>musica humana; the second of Boethius’s three types of music, referring to the internal harmony of the human being.</term> is therefore that which connects the concord of various elements into one composite—namely, the human body. For through it, the spiritual nature is joined to the body, and the rational is coupled with the irrational in concord. This seems to have been the view held by the **Platonists**, who posited that the soul is a certain harmonic consistency. 

**Pythagoras** and **Philolaus** <note>A Greek Pythagorean philosopher and contemporary of Socrates.</note> wrote that the soul itself is harmony. Hence it is written: "The binding of the body and the soul is made according to the consonance of natural order so that it may persist." Therefore, in the work of the **Reconciliator** <note>original: "Reconciliatorem"; referring to Pietro d'Abano (c. 1257–1315), a famous physician and philosopher known for his work "Conciliator," which attempted to resolve contradictions between medicine and philosophy.</note>, other sciences are placed under this "natural music." 

**Socrates** posited that this human music consists in the voice alone.

<summary>This page defines Human Music as the harmonic force that binds the soul to the body and regulates physical health, such as the pulse. It cites ancient authorities like Pythagoras, Plato, and the physician Herophilus to argue that life itself is a mathematical consonance.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions from the general concept of "Human Music" (musica humana) to its specific application in human biology and gestation, then introduces the third category of music: "Instrumental Music" (musica instrumentalis).</meta>

We read that **Aristoxenus** <note>original: "Aristoxenuz"; a Greek philosopher and music theorist who argued that music was a matter of human perception rather than just mathematical ratios.</note> treated music in the voice and the movement of the body. **Theophrastus** <note>original: "Theophrastus"; the successor of Aristotle, who wrote extensively on the nature of sound and its relation to the soul.</note> indeed wrote that human music operates in the voice and the movement of the body and soul. Therefore, if harmony exists in the movement of the body and soul, music itself is undoubtedly not foreign to our very births. For the number of days and even months pertaining to human births <note>original: "muliebribus partubus"; literally "womanly births," referring to the period of gestation.</note> is governed by a musical **ratio** <term>ratio; the mathematical relationship between numbers, which theorists believed mirrored the intervals found in music.</term>.

For **Pythagoras**, as **Censorinus** <note>original: "Censorinus"; a 3rd-century Roman grammarian whose work "On the Birthday" discusses the influence of musical mathematics on human life.</note> reports, said there are two types of birth: one of seven months and the other of ten. He claimed that the former is shaped by one set of day-counts, and the latter by another. For those numbers which bring about some transformation in each stage of gestation—namely, when the seed is converted into blood, or the blood into flesh, or the flesh into the human form—when compared among themselves, they possess the same ratio that voices <note>Or "pitches/tones."</note> hold in music.

### Chapter Four: On Instrumental Music

<margin>
Instrumental music is so named because it produces sounds from various instruments, namely natural and artificial. From the natural: which are the lungs, throat, palate, tongue, lips, teeth, and the windpipe <note>original: "Armonica"; here referring to the physiological "organ of harmony" or larynx.</note>. From the artificial: either organic or rhythmic.
</margin>

<gloss>easy to understand</gloss>
Long-standing experience teaches us that **instrumental music** operates through diverse instruments in many and varied ways. For it either forms consonances by gathering sounds produced from natural instruments—and then it is called **harmonic**—or from artificial ones; and this is called **organic** <term>organica; music produced by blowing, such as pipes or organs.</term>, or **rhythmic** <term>rithmica; music produced by striking, such as strings or percussion.</term>, or both organic and rhythmic at once.

<summary>This page concludes the discussion of Human Music by linking the mathematical ratios of music to the stages of human gestation and fetal development. It then introduces Instrumental Music, dividing it into "natural" (vocal anatomy) and "artificial" (musical instruments) categories.</summary>
<keywords>Aristoxenus, Theophrastus, Pythagoras, Censorinus, gestation, musical ratios, instrumental music, vocal anatomy, organica, rithmica</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Instrumental Music" (musica instrumentalis), moving from the general concept to its three specific branches: harmonic (vocal), organic (wind), and rhythmic (percussive/strings). It bridges the gap between the music of the human soul and the physical mechanics of producing sound.</meta>

<margin>
What Harmony is
</margin>

...in three ways: for it derives consonances either from artificial **organic** <term>organic; from the Latin 'organicus,' referring here to instruments that use bellows or breath, like an organ or trumpet</term> instruments, from rhythmic instruments, or from a mixture of both organic and rhythmic instruments at once. Thus, one part is organic, the other rhythmic. Therefore, **Harmonic Music** encompasses and governs the sounds produced by the human voice through the cooperation of natural "instruments," which, according to **Peter of Spain** <note>original: "petrū hyſpanū"; a famous 13th-century scholar and physician who became Pope John XXI. He wrote a standard textbook on logic.</note> in his first book on **dialectics** <term>dialectics; the art of logical investigation and formal reasoning</term>, are:
<insert>
The Lungs
The Throat
The Palate
The Tongue
The Lips and Teeth.
</insert>
It is called "harmonic" from "harmony," as if produced through the harmonious arrangement of various instruments and elements—namely, the human body. For this music creates movement from the soul and body, and from that movement, sound is born.

<margin>
Harmony is named from harmony, as if produced from the diverse instruments of the body. This music creates movement from the soul and body, and from movement, sound. Therefore, **Harmonic Music** is an art consisting of sounds, words, and numbers. For this reason, poets also call it metrical music. **Organic Music** is that which emits sounds only by the operation of breath. **Rhythmic Music** is that which exercises sounds only with the fingers. **Organic and Rhythmic** music together produce sounds through both breath and fingers, like a flute <unclear>r</unclear>.
</margin>

Therefore, **Harmonic Music** is a notable art that governs the voice, containing within itself both number and a specific measurement of sound; it is a perfect science of **modulation** <note>original: "modulationis"; in a medieval context, this refers to the structured measurement and regulation of musical intervals and rhythm.</note> consisting of three things: sounds, words, and numbers. Because of this, poets do not deny that **Metrical Music** belongs to it; by a probable ratio, they recognize the measure of various "feet"—such as **Iambs**, **Tribrachs**, and **Dactyls** <note>original: "Iambi Tribaci & dactili"; these are specific rhythmic patterns of long and short syllables used in poetry and vocal music.</note>—since it attends to the ending of time. We perceive **Organic Music** to treat musical sounds through the working breath of wind instruments, such as trumpets and horns. However, we see **Rhythmic Music** derive sounds from various instruments through a rod or the fingers, such as the **cithara**, the **lyre**, the **psaltery**, and such...

<summary>This page defines the three categories of instrumental music—harmonic (vocal), organic (wind), and rhythmic (strings/percussion)—and explains how the human body itself acts as a natural musical instrument.</summary>

### On the Three Kinds of Musicians and Who Is a True Musician: Chapter Five

<margin>
The first kind of musicians consists of those who devote all their effort to instruments, and such people are separated from the understanding of the science of music.
</margin>

The kind of those professing **instrumental music** <term>instrumentalis; music produced by physical instruments as opposed to the "music" of the spheres or the human soul</term> is threefold. One kind performs on instruments. Another composes songs. The third judges the work of the instruments and the song. The first kind, which works with instruments, consumes its entire effort there—such as the lyre-players, organists, and others who prove their skill upon instruments. These are separated from the understanding of the science of music because they serve as slaves, bringing no **reason** <term>ratio; the underlying mathematical and logical principles of music</term> to their work; indeed, they are entirely devoid of such reason.

<margin>
The second kind consists of poets, who are separated from the understanding of the science of music by Boethius because they are led to songs only by natural instinct.
</margin>

The second kind is that of the poets, which is led to song by a certain natural instinct rather than by **speculation** <term>speculatio; from the Latin 'speculari' (to observe), referring here to theoretical or contemplative study</term> and reason. Our author **Boethius** <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), whose treatise "De institutione musica" was the definitive music textbook for the Middle Ages.</note> separates these people from the theoretical study of music.

<margin>
The third kind consists of those who possess the skill of judging melodies and rhythms, and such people are called musicians.
</margin>

The third kind is that which possesses the skill of judging, so that it can weigh rhythms, melodies, and the composition as a whole. Since this kind is entirely based on reason and speculation, it is properly assigned to the art of music. A **Musician**, therefore, is one who possesses the faculty of speculation and the reason of music, not one in whom only the practical method of singing resides. For every art and every discipline... <insert>The Musician provides the reason for the melody; the Cantor only sings it.</insert> 

<summary>This page outlines the Boethian hierarchy of music, distinguishing between mere performers (instrumentalists), instinctive creators (poets), and true "musicians" (theorists who understand the mathematical laws of sound).</summary>

For physical craftsmanship serves like a handmaid; **reason**, however, commands like a mistress. Whence the works of the hands, led irrationally, are done in vain. For the contemplation of reason does not require action, although in this discipline many things would be in vain if not reduced to action. Wherefore, in the third book of *On the Soul* <note>Aristotle's *De Anima*</note>, the **speculative intellect** <term>intellectus speculativus; the mind's ability to contemplate universal truths without immediate practical application</term> is that which considers the truth simply; the **practical intellect**, however, is that which truly arranges for work. But since the discipline of music is a natural science, it renders the speculative or theoretical musician more noble and wiser than the practical one, according to the sixth book of the *Metaphysics* <note>Aristotle's *Metaphysics*</note>. For he is the **speculative musician** who, with reason as his guide, has taken up the science of singing not by the service of work but by the command of contemplation. But if he gives effort to practice, he will add to contemplation not a varied multitude but perfection, according to the opinion of the **Commentator** <note>original: "Comētatoris"; referring to Averroes, the 12th-century philosopher whose commentaries on Aristotle were standard in medieval universities</note> on the eighth book of the *Metaphysics*. Then indeed

<margin>
He is called a complete musician to whom nothing of speculation or operation is lacking.
</margin>

he will be a **complete musician** <note>original: "adequatus musicus"</note> to whom nothing of speculation and operation will be absent. For if by some chance or purpose he is hindered and fails in contemplation or song, he no more ceases to be a musician than an orator who has finished speaking, or a physician who has left the practice of healing. The **practitioner** <term>praticus; here referring to the singer who performs without necessarily understanding the mathematical theory</term> of this discipline of music is the **singer**, who pronounces and sings those things which the musician [has determined] by reason...

Therefore, the musician is to the singer as the **magistrate** <note>original: "pretor" (praetor); a high-ranking Roman official</note> is to the **herald** <note>original: "pconē" (praeconem); a public crier or messenger</note>. For the **speculative** <note>referring to theoretical contemplation</note> excels the practical, for it is much greater and worthier to know what one does than to perform that which one knows. Indeed, we see that the works of buildings as well as the triumphs of wars are attributed to those by whose command and reason they were established, not to those by whose labor and service they were completed.

# Art Contributes More to the Musician than Nature. Chapter VI

<margin>
Nature contributes much to a man for perceiving music, but **education** <note>original: "doctrina"</note> contributes the most. But when both are present, he will be a consummate musician. Although nature and education can work separately, the balanced musician owes more to education than to nature.
</margin>
<margin>
The beginning of music is the **ear**; the second is **reason** or rule. The ear is the first organ of sensing.
</margin>
I consider that **nature** <note>innate talent or biological hearing</note> contributes not a little to men for the perception of music, but **education** <note>original: "doctrinā"; formal study or instruction</note> is of the greatest value. However, a consummate musician can by no means exist without both. Although nature without education, and education without nature, can operate, yet we believe nature to be the initial momentum; but the consummate and balanced musician owes more to education than to nature. For the nature of human talent does not prevent one from reaching it; but if that does not happen, those who strive for the highest things will nonetheless go higher than those who, out of a presumed despair of arriving where they wish, immediately stop at the lowest things. Yet, precepts and arts are nothing [if]...

<summary>This page defines the "musician" as a rational commander (like a magistrate) and the "singer" as a mere servant (like a herald), arguing that formal education (art/doctrina) is more essential for perfection than innate talent (nature).</summary>

...[they] excel unless nature assists. For even the ignorant sing

music, and the unrefined do so with nature as their guide without **education** <note>original: "doctrina"; refers here to formal theoretical instruction</note>. <margin>A musician is made by art, nor was he a musician before he possessed the art.</margin>
that the musician is made by art, and that he did not exist before the art. Therefore, education contributes more to the discipline of music than nature.

## ON THE JUDGMENT OF MUSIC. CHAPTER VII.

<margin>The principal judge of music is the ear, or reason, or the rule; the ear can err, reason never.</margin>
For the sense of the ears produces every beginning of the musical art, since if hearing were lacking to all, philosophers would have spent very little care in treating the voice. Yet hearing alone does not suffice, but **reason** <note>original: "ratio"</note> also takes for itself a certain judgment. In a way, hearing holds the place of the beginning and serves as a prompt;
<margin>He does not prove here <unclear>p̄</unclear> [...]</margin>
but finally, the perfection of the act belongs to the recognizing reason. For not the same things [are pleasing] to all...

<insert>since</insert> neither is the power of sensing the same for everyone, nor is it always equal for the same
<insert>person</insert>. <insert>In</insert> vain, indeed, do we seek with varying judgment that which we care to know truly.
Indeed, the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of Pythagoras, who believed that the universe and music were governed by mathematical ratios rather than just auditory preference</note> take a certain middle path; for they do not surrender all judgment to the ears, although some things are explored by them only through the ears. For we perceive the **consonances** <term>consonantia; the sounding together of tones that produce a stable or pleasing effect</term> themselves with the ear, but by what **intervals** <term>intervalla; the mathematical distance or ratio between two pitches</term> they differ among themselves, we measure not by the ears—whose judgments are dull—but by rules and reason; so that the sense is like a sort of obedient servant, while reason is the judge and commander. For although the foundations of almost all arts and of life itself are brought forth by the occasion of the senses, nevertheless there is no certain judgment in them, no comprehension of the truth, if the arbitration of reason is cast aside. For the sense itself is corrupted by the greatest and the smallest; for very small things cannot be sensed because of the tiny scale of the sensible objects themselves, while larger things are often confused. For by a greater, intense sound the hearing becomes deaf, while it captures the sound of smaller things with more difficulty.

# ON THE DISCOVERY OF CONSONANCES. CHAPTER 8.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the limitations of sensory perception in music, specifically focusing on the legendary discovery of mathematical ratios by Pythagoras. It follows the argument that human hearing is too inconsistent to serve as a reliable judge of musical truth.</meta>

<margin>
Pythagoras, having abandoned the judgment of the ears, [examined] the consonances which are varied by nature, external accidents, and age, and even in natural <unclear>branching</unclear> <note>original: ramificaliby; possibly referring to organic materials or the structure of instruments</note> instruments. For more humid air dulls the strings, or drier air sharpens them; the thickness of a string makes the sound deeper, or a thinner one narrows the sharpness, according to rule and exquisite **reason** <term>ratio; mathematical logic</term>. Hearing and noticing the sounds of the hammers, which were sharpened or deepened by their different weights, he immediately began to examine the weights. And since there were five hammers, he added a sixth and did not cast out the fifth <note>The legend of the hammers varies; some versions say he used four, some five, some six</note>, for blacksmiths even use ten hammers for...
</margin>

While Pythagoras was investigating musical **consonances** <term>consonantia; notes that sound harmonious together</term>, having abandoned the judgment of the ears—which are changed partly by nature and partly by external accidents, and vary even by the age of the listener—we read that he turned toward the authority of rules. Various instruments offered him no certainty, for within them much variety and inconsistency often arose. For if we consider strings, either a more humid air dulls the strike, or a drier air sharpens it; or the thickness of the string makes the sound deeper, or a thinner one narrows the pitch, or it changes the state of its former consistency in some other way.

It happened that while Pythagoras himself, perplexed by such variation, was passing by the workshops of blacksmiths, the sounds of hammers rang in his ears. Since it was his custom to examine the **reason** <note>original: rationez; here meaning the mathematical ratio or underlying logic</note> of all things visible, invisible, and sensible, he ordered the hammers to be swapped. Noticing that the diverse sounds of the hammers were not changed by the strength of the men, but resulted from the different weights of the hammers, he began to examine the weights themselves. And since there were five hammers, he cast out the fifth according to **Boethius** <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), whose "De institutione musica" was the standard music theory textbook of the Middle Ages</note>, because it was dissonant with all the others. The four remaining, however, [responded to each other]; he perceived that the weight corresponding to double the other produced the **diapason** <term>diapason; the Greek term for an octave, a 2:1 ratio</term> consonance, so that if the first hammer was six pounds or...

<insert>
...they forge anchors and armor for men. He noted such weights, namely: the first was a weight of four pounds, the second six, the third eight, the fourth nine, the fifth twelve, and the sixth sixteen. Then, considering these weights alternately according to their proportions, he discovered the consonances as they are in the <unclear>Pythagorean</unclear> <note>original: pithoris; likely a corruption of "Pythagoricis"</note> [systems].
</insert>

**Vocabulary:** Pythagoras, Boethius, consonance, octave (diapason), musical, weight, hammer.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Pythagoras at the blacksmith's forge. ...the last was indeed 12. The second, compared to the first, held a **sesquitertial** <term>epitritus; a ratio of 4:3</term> weight, containing the **diatessaron** <term>consonance of a fourth</term>, and it weighed 8 pounds. The third, however, compared to the first, comprised a **sesquialter** <term>hemiolius or sesquialtera; a ratio of 3:2</term> weight, as it was 9 pounds, preserving the **diapente** <term>consonance of a fifth</term>. This same third hammer, compared to the second, maintained a **sesquioctave** <term>sesquioctava; a ratio of 9:8</term> weight, which constitutes a **tone** <note>the standard mathematical interval between a fourth and a fifth</note>. Likewise, the third hammer was joined to the last by a **sesquitertial** <note>original: epitrito</note> weight, fitting the consonance of a fourth. The second hammer, compared to the same last one, preserved a **sesquialter** weight, which produced the consonance of a fifth.

Therefore, let us arrange the four weights of the hammers in their natural order in this manner:

| VI | VIII | VIIII | XII |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |

When the long investigation of such a deep and secret matter had exhausted Pythagoras himself through profound contemplation, there is no doubt that he attempted to clarify not only these four primary consonances, but also the others into which the discipline of music constantly branches. 

<margin>Worldly music <note>musica mundana; the harmony of the universe</note> is established upon the four elements.</margin>

For although these four hammers—much like the **worldly music** established upon the four elements—were proven to produce those four consonances, it must be considered and maintained that Pythagoras did not reject the fifth hammer, but rather took up a sixth. <meta>This page continues the mathematical explanation of Pythagoras’s discovery at the blacksmith's forge. ...certainly to construct musical structures <note>original: armaturas; here referring to the "fitting" or arrangement of notes</note> not only with six hammers, but indeed also with eight or ten corresponding to one another in turns. Wherefore, since there were six striking hammers working the glowing iron, their blows—some heavier and some sharp <note>referring to low and high pitches</note>—harmonized in such a certain and mutually corresponding order that both reached the listener's sense in a fixed proportion, offering a single consonance from the various strikes. Hearing these, Pythagoras ordered the hammers to be swapped <note>to ensure the sound came from the tools themselves, not the strength of the smiths</note>. Thinking that the weights were joined to one another in unequal measures, he collected their proportions and examined the specific weight of each individual hammer.

And since the first hammer was of four pounds weight, the second six, the third eight, the fourth nine, the fifth twelve, and the sixth contained sixteen pounds of weight, let these weights of each be placed here in the following order:

| IIII | VI | VIII | VIIII | XII | XVI |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |

For the first hammer, compared to the second, comprised a **sesquialter** <term>ratio of 3:2</term> proportion, preserving the **consonance of a fifth** <term>diapente</term>. The second, however, compared to the third, established a **sesquitertial** <term>ratio of 4:3</term> comparison, producing the **consonance of a fourth** <term>diatessaron</term>. Again, the first was established in relation to the third in a double number <note>a 2:1 ratio</note>, responding to the **consonance of an octave** <term>diapason</term>. Again, the third stands in relation to the fourth in a **sesquioctave** <term>ratio of 9:8</term> proportion, constituting a **tone**. The second, indeed, compared to the same fourth, established a **sesquialter** <term>ratio of 3:2</term>...

--- Page 45 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical proof of Pythagoras’s discovery at the forge, applying the ratios of hammer weights to specific musical intervals. The previous page established the weights of six hammers as 4, 6, 8, 9, 12, and 16 pounds; this text now details every possible pairing to show how the entire musical scale is built on these numbers.</meta>

...making the comparison of the **fifth** <term>diapente</term> consonance.

The third hammer compared to the fifth, in a **sesquialter** <term>3:2 ratio</term> proportion, forms the consonance of a **fifth** <term>diapente</term>. 
<margin>The Terms</margin>
The fourth relative to the fifth, maintaining a **sesquitertial** <term>4:3 ratio</term>, produces a **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term>. Again, the first hammer relative to the fifth, established in a **triple proportion** <term>3:1 ratio</term>, joins together the consonance of an **octave plus a fifth** <term>diapason-plus-diapente</term>. But the second compared to that same fifth establishes the consonance of an **octave** <term>diapason</term> in a **double proportion** <term>2:1 ratio</term>. Likewise, the second compared to the sixth yields an **octave plus a fourth** <term>diapason-plus-diatessaron</term>, which is established alone in the **superpartient class** <note>original: suppatienti; referring to ratios where the numerator is more than one part greater than the denominator, such as 8/3</note> through a **double super-bipartient proportion** <term>8:3 ratio</term>. Again, the first compared to the sixth joins in a **quadruple proportion** <term>4:1 ratio</term>, creating a **double octave** <term>bisdiapason</term> consonance. The third joined to the sixth in a **double comparison** <term>2:1 ratio</term> forms the consonance of an **octave** <term>diapason</term>. The fifth, moreover, connected to that same sixth by a **sesquitertial proportion** <term>4:3 ratio</term>, establishes the consonance of a **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term>.

And in this way, Pythagoras himself discovered through observation and reason that these musical consonances are produced only from numerical proportions. Through rules and logic, he revealed those things which were previously perceived only by the fallible <note>original: corruptibili; literally 'corruptible', contrasting the unreliable physical ear with the eternal truths of mathematics</note> sense. Indeed, all these things are shown most clearly in this figure.

It emphasizes Pythagoras's role in elevating music from a mere sensory experience to a logical, mathematical science.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the demonstration of the Pythagorean ratios using the weights of the six hammers identified on the previous page (4, 6, 8, 9, 12, and 16 pounds). It provides the specific numerical pairings that result in each musical interval.</meta>

From 6 to 4, from 9 to 6, and from 12 to 8, the **fifth** <term>diapente</term> resounded in a **sesquialter** <term>3:2 ratio</term> proportion.

From 8 to 4, from 12 to 6, and from 16 to 8, an **octave** <term>diapason</term> was created in a **double** <term>2:1 ratio</term> proportion.

Each man wields a large hammer, shown in various stages of the striking motion. Roman numerals are written near the head of each hammer to indicate their relative weights: iiii, VI, VIII, VIIII, XII, and XVI. <margin>
From 16 to 6, an **octave plus a fourth** <term>diapason-plus-diatessaron</term> is born from a **double super-bipartient** <term>8:3 ratio</term> proportion; this is the only one produced from the **superpartient class** <note>original: ex genere superpartienti; referring to ratios where the numerator is more than one part greater than the denominator, such as 8/3 which is twice the denominator plus two-thirds</note>.
</margin>

From 8 to 6, from 12 to 9, and from 16 to 12, a **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> was born in a **sesquitertial** <term>4:3 ratio</term> proportion.

From 8 to 9, a **whole tone** <term>tonus</term> was born in a **sesquioctave** <term>9:8 ratio</term> proportion.

From 12 to 4, an **octave plus a fifth** <term>diapason-plus-diapente</term> was made in a **triple** <term>3:1 ratio</term> proportion.

From 16 to 4, a **double octave** <term>bisdiapason</term> was heard in a **quadruple** <term>4:1 ratio</term> proportion.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Pythagoras’s discovery of musical ratios. Having discovered the mathematical proportions through the blacksmith's hammers on the previous page, he now tests these ratios across various physical media to prove their universal application, eventually leading to the legendary origins of music involving Jubal and the antediluvian pillars.</meta>

<margin>
Josephus the historian writes that **Jubal** <note>original: "tubalem"; likely a common medieval variation or scribal confusion between Tubal-cain the smith and Jubal the musician</note> preserved the harmonies received from God on a brick column.
</margin>

Once these truths were truly understood, Pythagoras himself—weighing with various examinations whether the entire logic of harmonies <term>symphoniarum ratio</term> consisted in these proportions—attached weights, chosen proportionately, to strings of equal length and thickness. Then, through the varying lengths of pipes <note>original: "calamorum"; literally reeds or stalks used as flutes</note> suitable to these proportions, he gained various experiences with complete reliability. Often, he also filled vessels <term>cyathos</term> <note>measures or cups</note> with proportional measures of water, using equal weights. Furthermore, by striking bronze bells formed with different weights using a bronze or iron rod, he perceived that musical harmonies of this kind were joined in these same proportions. Having been led by this, he became more certain that he could produce musical sounds through the length and thickness of strings, both of bronze and of gut, using those same proportions.

And so, by these means, he devised a **rule** <term>regula</term>—a name derived from the matter itself. This is not because the "rule" is merely a piece of wood or bronze by which magnitudes and sounds are measured, but because there is such a fixed and firm observation of this kind that it never deceives a seeker with a doubtful judgment. It is called a "rule" from the word *rego* <note>Latin: "to rule" or "to guide"</note>, for it guides us so that we do not fall into error. Indeed, Josephus the historian wrote that **Jubal**, before the Great Flood, held the art of sounds so dear that, fearing that ancient Deluge, he inscribed it upon two columns, which the human race found renewed after the Flood. For **Adam** had prophesied that the world was destined to perish quickly by either water or fire; on account of which, Jubal...

<summary>Pythagoras proves that musical ratios are universal by testing them on strings, pipes, water-filled vessels, and bells, eventually establishing the "Regula" (musical canon). The text then connects the origin of music to the biblical figure Jubal, who inscribed musical knowledge on pillars to survive the prophesied destruction of the world.</summary>

The elements of music are consonant proportions. Its parts are the tone, the semitone, and so on.

Thence, the looser or more relaxed it is, the more slowly and thinly it strikes the air, nor does it vibrate as powerfully, and it produces a lower sound.

But from those voices which convey no dissimilarity to the ears, no **consonance** at all results, according to **Nicomachus**; for consonance is a concord of voices, dissimilar among themselves, reduced into a single unity.

## Chapter Two: On the Distinction of Voices.

<margin>
Of voices, some are continuous, others are suspended by an interval. <unclear>According to some</unclear>, continuous voices are infinite.
Continuous voices, to which God gave human speech, are those we produce in speaking and reading.
Voices suspended by an interval are those we produce while singing; in this way, we express **melody** <note>original: "modulationem." This refers to the measured rise and fall of pitch in music.</note> rather than speech.
Both continuous and interval-based voices are infinite. Between these, a third distinction is placed by **Albinus** <note>Albinus was a Roman scholar whose lost works on music are known primarily through citations by Boethius.</note> which we use when reading poems—neither in the continuous flow of prose nor in the suspended delivery of a song.
</margin>
Of voices, some are **continuous**, while others are **suspended by an interval**. Continuous voices are those which we produce by speaking or reading continuously. For in unfolding our thoughts and expressing speech, the rush of the continuous voice brings about its effect, hurrying to run through the words as quickly as possible.

But we produce voices **suspended by an interval** when we sing, by which indeed we express not so much speech, but rather **melody**. For a voice of this kind is slower, creating a certain interval—not an interval of silence, but rather of a suspended and slow **chant**—through the variations that must be modulated. To these, however, **Albinus** <note>The text here spells the name "Albius," though the margin correctly identifies him as Albinus.</note> added a third difference which sits in the middle: those voices used when we read **heroic poems**. This is done neither with a continuous run like **prose**, nor with the suspended delivery of a **song**. But continuous voices and those which...

<meta>This page continues the classification of sounds initiated on page 15. Having defined continuous voices (speech) and voices with intervals (song), the text now explores the physical limits of the human voice and introduces Claudius Ptolemy’s specific categories for how different musical pitches relate to one another.</meta>

<margin>
Continuous sounds and sounds suspended by an interval are infinite. Although some say human nature has set a limit to continuous sounds, it is more accurate to say that the nature of humanity determines and allows for the high and low limits of those sounds.
</margin>

...by which we run through a **melody** <note>original: "cantilenam"</note> naturally, are infinite. For if we consider it, no specific limit is given either to the movement of unfolding speech or to the raising of high notes and the relaxing of low ones; rather, human nature has established a proper end for both. To **continuous voices** <insert>namely, speech</insert>, the human breath <note>original: "spiritus"</note> has given a limit beyond which they cannot be extended by any effort. For each person speaks continuously only as much as their natural breath allows. Likewise, for **voices suspended by an interval** <insert>namely, song</insert>, human nature has set a limit which determines how high or low those voices can be. For each person can only raise the pitch to a certain sharpness or depress it to a certain depth as the natural measure of their voice permits.

<margin>
The differences between joined voices according to **Ptolemy** —
Voices are, among themselves, either **unison** or **non-unison**.
Non-unison voices are:
Some **equisonant**,
others **consonant**,
others **melodic** <note>original: "emmeles"</note>,
others **dissonant**,
others **unmelodic** <note>original: "exmeles"</note>.
**Unison** voices are those that produce the same sound.
**Equisonant** voices are those that seem to produce the same sound, such as the **octave** <note>original: "diapason"</note> and the **double octave**.
**Consonant** voices are those that emit a sweet composite sound, such as the **fifth** <note>original: "diapente"</note> and its compounds.
**Melodic** <note>original: "emmeles"</note> voices are those which, though not consonant, are suited to melodies, such as those composed from them <unclear>in a specific aspect</unclear>.
</margin>

We have learned that **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), an influential Greco-Roman mathematician and music theorist.</note> treated the differences of joined voices in this manner. Voices, he says, are either **unison** or **non-unison** with each other. Among the non-unison voices, some are indeed **equisonant**, others **consonant**, others **melodic** <term>emmeles: intervals that are not primary harmonies but are still musical steps used in a scale</term>, others **dissonant**, and others **unmelodic** <term>exmeles: sounds that do not fit into a musical scale or melody</term>. For those are **unison** which, when struck individually, produce one and the same sound. **Equisonant** voices, however, are those which, when struck together, create one sound out of two, and in a certain way a simple one, such as the **octave** <note>original: "diapason"</note> and the **double octave** <note>original: "bisdiapason"</note>. **Consonant** voices are those which produce a sweet and blended composite sound, such as the **fifth** <note>original: "diapente"</note> and the **octave-plus-fifth**. **Melodic** <note>original: "emmeles"</note> are those which, although not consonant, are nonetheless correctly fitted to melodies...

<meta>This page continues the classification of musical intervals based on the theories of Ptolemy. While the previous page defined equisonant and consonant sounds, this page defines dissonant and "unmelodic" (exmelles) intervals. It also provides a physical explanation for how pitch can change continuously (like a sliding string) versus discretely (with distinct steps), using the visual analogy of a rainbow.</meta>

<margin>
Dissonant sounds are those which do not blend their sounds, such as the **whole tone** and the **semitone** <note>original: "semitonium"</note>.
</margin>
...for instance, the **ditone** <term>ditone: an interval of two whole tones, equivalent to a major third</term>, the **semiditone** <term>semiditone: a minor third</term>, the **fifth** <note>original: "diapente"</note> plus a whole tone, the **octave** <note>original: "diapason"</note> plus a ditone, and such like. **Dissonant** sounds, however, are those which do not blend their sounds and strike the sense harshly, such as the whole tone and the semitone.

<margin>
Unmelodic sounds <note>original: "exmelles"</note> are those which are not accepted in the union of consonances, such as the **fourth** <note>original: "diatessaron"</note>, the **seventh** <note>original: "eptacordum"</note>, and those composed from them.
</margin>
For **unmelodic** sounds <note>original: "exmelles"</note> are those that are not accepted in a consonant combination, such as the joining of four notes <note>a fourth</note> on its own, the connection of seven notes <note>a seventh</note>, or eleven notes, and so on. Therefore, **unison** sounds are those whose sound is one, whether in the low or the high register. **Non-unison** sounds, however, are those where one is lower and the other higher.

<margin>
In some non-unison sounds, the difference is joined by a common boundary; these are not discrete, but are continuous through the same transition.
</margin>
Of these, the difference of some is joined by a common boundary; this is not said to be "discrete" or "suspended by an interval," but is drawn from the low to the high in such a way that it appears continuous.

<margin>
Voices are joined by a common boundary in this way: if a string <note>original: "corda"</note> is struck on an instrument and then tightened while it is vibrating, it happens that the strike is lower at the beginning, but as it is tightened, that voice becomes higher; thus the sounds of the low and high voice are made continuous.
</margin>
For voices are joined by a common boundary in this manner: if someone strikes a string on a musical instrument and tightens it while it is being struck, it happens that the pulse is lower <note>original: "graviorem"</note> at the beginning, but while it is being tightened, that voice is made sharper <note>original: "acuciorem"</note>, and so the sounds of the low and high pitch are made continuous.

Comparison
</margin>
The same similarity is presented to the eyes when we behold a **celestial arc** <note>a rainbow</note> in the clouds; for the colors are so close to one another that there is no certain boundary where one is separated from the other. Instead, where the darker color <note>original: "gra," likely "gravis" or "grisea"</note> departs from red toward a pale hue, it turns into the following color through a continuous change, with no certain midpoint intervening to distinguish the two. Others, however, are non-unison sounds whose difference is distinguished by an intervening silence. When...
