<meta>This page continues the classification of sounds and intervals, transitioning from the definitions of unison and non-unison sounds to the physical nature of sound itself. It relies heavily on Boethian musical theory, particularly the distinction between "continuous" and "discrete" sound and the acoustic analogy of water ripples.</meta>

<margin>
Others are unison in such a way that the difference between them is distinguished by an intervening silence.
</margin>

Therefore, of voices, some are **continuous** and others are **discrete** <note>original: "disaggregate"</note>. Continuous sounds are those where the difference between them is joined by a common boundary, and they do not have a designated place where the high or low pitch might hold steady. **Discrete** sounds, however, have their own distinct places between them, like unmixed colors whose difference is established to the sight in its own specific place. Continuous sounds—which are not unison—are separated from the faculty of harmony, for they are dissimilar within themselves and do not sound as a single, stable thing <note>This refers to the "sliding" sound of speech or a glissando, which ancient theorists felt could not be measured by music theory</note>. Discrete sounds, however, are subject to the harmonic art; for the specific difference between distant and dissimilar sounds can be apprehended and measured.

### Chapter Three: On the Formation of Consonance

<margin>
A comparison
</margin>

Finally, what occurs in voices is the same as what happens in marshes or quiet waters when a stone is thrown from a distance. First, the water gathers into a **tiny** <unclear>pūissiz</unclear> circle; then it spreads the spheres of waves into larger circles until the motion, exhausted by the effort of drawing out the ripples, comes to rest. The later wave is always spread wider than the one before. But if something is placed in the way which can obstruct the growing waves, that motion is immediately turned back and is rounded again by the same ripples, as if returning to the center from which it had first departed. Thus, when struck air creates a sound, it pushes the next air near it and rouses a rounded wave of air. In this way, the sound is spread abroad and reaches the hearing of all those standing around at the same time.

<margin>
How sound, or the voice, is formed in struck air.
</margin>

<margin>
When air is struck, it makes a sound and pushes the next air, rousing a rounded wave of air. It is spread to the hearing of all those standing around.
</margin>

<summary>This page explains the difference between continuous sounds (like speech) and discrete sounds (musical intervals), and provides a famous physical analogy comparing sound waves to ripples in a pond.</summary>
<keywords>continuous voice, discrete voice, acoustics, sound waves, ripples, Boethius, pitch, resonance</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on acoustics, specifically how sound travels through the air as waves. It transitions from the physical analogy of water ripples to the theoretical debates between the Empiricist school (Aristoxenus) and the Mathematical school (Pythagoreans/Platonists) regarding how musical intervals are measured and perceived.</meta>

<margin>
It strikes; for those who are further away, the wave of struck air arrives more weakly.

Aristoxenus treated consonances by the judgment of the ears and said that the <term>diapason<gloss>octave</gloss></term> consists of six tones; this is false because two minor semitones cannot produce one full tone.
</margin>

...it strikes the hearing, and the voice is more obscure to whoever stands farther away, because the wave of struck air reaches them more weakly. **Aristoxenus**, for that reason, did not base his treatment of <term>consonance<gloss>agreement of sounds</gloss></term> on <term>ratio<gloss>mathematical proportion</gloss></term> at all, but left it to the judgment of the ears. He does not mark the sounds themselves with any numerical proportions, but instead takes the difference between them as his starting point, so that he places his investigation not in the sounds themselves, but in the distance that separates them. This was quite imprudent, as he believed he could judge the difference between sounds for which he established no defined image or measure. 

He, therefore, proposes that the <term>diatessaron<gloss>perfect fourth</gloss></term> consonance consists of two tones and a semitone; that the <term>diapente<gloss>perfect fifth</gloss></term> consists of three tones and a semitone; and the <term>diapason<gloss>octave</gloss></term> consists of six tones. But this is false, for we shall demonstrate <note>The author likely refers to the Pythagorean proof that six whole tones exceed an octave by a small interval called a comma</note> that two minor semitones can in no way complete a full tone. 

But consonance arises for the ears according to **Plato** in this way: when a higher sound, which is swifter, enters the ear quickly after it has been released, and having struck the furthest part of that same internal space, it returns with a repeated motion. However, it is now slower and not so swift as it ran when first sent out by the initial impulse. Therefore, that same higher sound, now returning lower <note>original: "gravior"</note>, meets the first part of the sound (which is similar to the incoming wave) and mixes with it, creating a single consonance. 

But **Nichomachus** <note>Nichomachus of Gerasa, a Pythagorean mathematician and music theorist</note> rebukes this Platonic consideration; for as was touched upon in the first chapter, this concord of singing is not a similarity...

<summary>This page critiques Aristoxenus for relying on the ears rather than math to define musical intervals, and explains Plato's physical theory of how high and low sounds mix in the ear to create harmony.</summary>
<keywords>Aristoxenus, Plato, Nichomachus, consonance, acoustics, octave, perfect fourth, perfect fifth, semitones, music theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the transition from the errors of Aristoxenus to the more mathematically grounded theories of Nicomachus and Boethius. It moves from discussing the distance between sounds to how the mind and ear perceive musical intervals as unified consonances through numerical proportions.</meta>

...it produces nothing but dissimilarity; hence, if one were to mix heavy [low] sounds, they would create no <term>consonance<gloss>agreement of sounds</gloss></term>. Consonance is, therefore, a mixture of a high and a low sound reaching the ears sweetly and uniformly. Wherefore, although dissimilarity exists in the individual voices, it is joined into unity when they are mixed. 

Here, therefore, **Nicomachus** <note>Nicomachus of Gerasa (c. 60–120 AD), a mathematician who wrote the influential Manual of Harmonics</note> himself, considering how consonance is made, says that it is not merely one strike that emits a simple mode of voice; rather, a string once struck hits the air more frequently and produces many sounds. But because the velocity of the percussion is such that one sound somehow overtakes the next, the distance between them is not perceived, and it reaches the ears as if it were a single voice. If, therefore, the strikes of low sounds are <term>commensurable<gloss>having a common numerical measure</gloss></term> with the strikes of high sounds—as seen in the proportions of the hammers <note>A reference to the legend of Pythagoras discovering musical ratios by listening to the weight of blacksmiths' hammers</note>—there is no doubt that if this commensuration is mixed within itself, it produces a single consonance of voices.

<margin>
Propose a number.
</margin>

<margin>
In the ear and in reason, because the soul rejoices in quantity, is the judgment of music. 

For when a number or a line is proposed, nothing is easier for the eyes or the soul than a judgment of the double, and then half and triple will follow—thus regarding Nicomachus, he thinks the <term>diapason<gloss>octave</gloss></term> comes from the double [2:1 ratio], the <term>diapente<gloss>perfect fifth</gloss></term> holds the middle harmony [the 3:2 ratio], the <term>diapason-diapente<gloss>octave plus a fifth</gloss></term> from the triple [3:1 ratio], and the others similarly, etc.
</margin>

But **Boethius**, as was more extensively declared in the first book, reports that among all the consonances we have mentioned, a judgment must be held in the ear as well as in reason as to which of these ought to be considered better. For in the same way the ear is affected by sounds, or the eyes by sight, the judgment of the soul is affected by numbers or continuous quantity. 

For if a number or a line is proposed, nothing is easier for the eye or the soul to behold than its double [2:1]. Likewise, after the judgment of the double follows the half; after the half, the triple [3:1]; after the triple, the third part. Since this is easier...

### Chapter 4: On Lowness, Highness, and the Interval

<margin>
A sound, which is called a <term>phtongos<gloss>original: "phtongus"</gloss></term> by the Greeks, is a fall of the voice that is <term>emellis<gloss>melodic; original: "emellis"</gloss></term>, that is, suitable for melodies in a single tension. An interval is the distance between a low and a high sound, according to what musicians say.
</margin>
Now it is necessary to explain the sound which the Greeks call a <term>phtongos<gloss>a musical tone</gloss></term> <note>original: "phtongos"</note> by analogy with speech. For a sound is a "fall" of the voice that is <margin>melodic</margin> <term>emellis<gloss>musical or melodious</gloss></term> <note>original: "emellis"</note>—that is, suitable for melodies in a single tension. Therefore, since a <term>consonance<gloss>the agreement of two sounds</gloss></term> is a mixture of a high and a low sound reaching the ears uniformly and sweetly, it encompasses a certain interval of sounds, which is defined thus: An **interval** is the distance between a high and a low sound. For whenever two dissimilar voices are joined, whether the interval is very large or very small—such as a <term>tone<gloss>whole step</gloss></term>, a <term>semitone<gloss>half step</gloss></term>, or any other discrete quantity such as a <term>diatessaron<gloss>perfect fourth</gloss></term>, a <term>diapente<gloss>perfect fifth</gloss></term>, or even <term>ditones<gloss>major thirds</gloss></term> and similar ones—then the lower sound is always assigned to <term>lowness<gloss>gravity</gloss></term> and the higher to <term>highness<gloss>acuteness</gloss></term>. As witness **Marcus Tullius [Cicero]** says in the sixth book of *The Republic* <note>Specifically the "Dream of Scipio," where Cicero describes the music of the celestial spheres</note>: "And nature brings it about that the extremes sound low from one side, and high from the other" <note>original: "Et natura fert ut extrema ex altera parte gravis ex altera vero acute sonent"</note>. From this it follows that highness and lowness have no proper or fixed place of their own that they occupy. For to whatever lower sound...

<summary>This page defines the fundamental components of music—sound (phtongos) and interval—using Pythagorean ratios and classical authority. It explains that pitch is relative, with "high" and "low" defined by their relationship to one another within a mathematical distance.</summary>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from defining sound (phtongos) and intervals to explaining the relative nature of pitch. The previous page established that an interval is the distance between high and low sounds; here, the author argues that "high" and "low" are not absolute categories but are defined by their relationship to one another.</meta>

<margin>
**Lowness** and **highness** have no fixed place of their own, for a low sound becomes high by the removal of another sound, and the same is true of high sounds. Every sound, by the addition of another, becomes either low or high. Therefore, the introduction of the **Hand** <note>The "Guidonian Hand," a mnemonic device used to teach the gamut of musical pitches</note>—which divides sounds into low, high, and super-high—is false for students.
</margin>

...if another sound is joined below a sound, then the first sound, which was previously the lower, becomes the higher in comparison to the sound placed beneath it, which now maintains the <term>gravitas<gloss>lowness or weight of pitch</gloss></term>. The same occurs if a higher sound is added above an already high sound: for the sound that was first "high" becomes "low." This does not happen by a relaxation <note>original: "remissio"; refers to the loosening of a string to lower its pitch</note> of the note itself—for it is held immobile—but by the addition of another sound above it which now maintains the <term>acumen<gloss>highness or sharpness of pitch</gloss></term>.

It follows that an interval is the distance between a high and low sound, and a <term>consonance<gloss>the agreement of sounds</gloss></term> is constituted from high and low sounds. Here, the explanation of those who introduce students to this discipline is proved false when they propose an introductory manual <note>original: "introductorium"; likely referring to the pedagogical "Hand"</note> divided into "low," "high," and "super-high" sounds. Such a division must necessarily border on falsehood, unless it was established simply as an easier way of distinguishing and perceiving the <term>notulae<gloss>little notes</gloss></term> and the <term>claves<gloss>keys or clefs</gloss></term>. 

For consonances are made from those very voices which they categorize as "low," and likewise from those they call "high." But a consonance cannot exist at all except from the mixing of low and high sounds. Although the ears judge the sound, it is <term>ratio<gloss>reason or mathematical proportion</gloss></term> that weighs it. It occurs in this way: whenever two strings are stretched—one lower than the other—and struck at once, they return a somewhat mixed and sweet sound. Two voices run together into one as if joined; then that is a consonance. <term>Euphonia<gloss>well-soundingness</gloss></term> <note>original: "Euphonia"</note> is also the same thing as consonance, for it is said...

<margin>
by <unclear>drit</unclear> Euphonia
</margin>

<meta>This page transitions from the general definition of pitch to the specific definitions of consonance and dissonance. It continues the author’s project of grounding musical theory in the authority of classical and medieval thinkers like Augustine, Hucbald, and Isidore of Seville, while preparing to explain the mathematical ratios (proportions) that create these sounds.</meta>

<margin>
**Consonance** is said to be **Symphony**, which is **Euphonia** <note>original: "euphonia"</note>, from *eu*—meaning good—and *phonia*—meaning sonority. **Dissonance** is called **Diaphonia**, which is a harsh and unpleasant mixing of sounds.
</margin>

<margin>
**Harmony**, according to the **Philosopher** <note>In a medieval context, "The Philosopher" almost always refers to Aristotle</note>, is the ratio of sounds in highness and lowness. **Hucbald** <note>original: "Vbaldus"; Hucbald of Saint-Amand (c. 840–930) was a monk and influential music theorist</note> says: **Harmony** is the fitting unification of diverse voices.
</margin>

...[consonance is derived] from *eu*, which is "good," and *phonia*, "sonority," as if it were a **"good sonority."** We understand this to be the same as **Symphony**; for according to **Augustine**, Symphony is the concord of voices. **Harmony** too, according to **Hucbald**, is the fitting unification of diverse voices. The **Philosopher**, however, established that Harmony is the ratio of sounds in highness and lowness. But when two strings struck together do not blend into a sweet sound to the ears, and do not form a single sound composed, as it were, of two, then that is what is called **dissonance**. For dissonance, according to **Isidore** in the *Etymologies* <note>original: "Isīdōrū ī ethīōlōgīīs"; referring to Isidore of Seville's 7th-century encyclopedia</note>, is a harsh and unpleasant mixing of two sounds reaching the ears after being mixed together. It is also called **diaphonia** <note>original: "Diāphōnīā"; literally "discordant sounding" in Greek</note>, a mixing of discordant sounds.

### In which proportions consonances consist: Chapter Five

For from the comparisons of highness and lowness which are <term>commensurate<gloss>measurable by the same standard or ratio</gloss></term> with each other—wherever a known common measure occurs—the musical **consonances** arise. For that is called a "common measure" which, being the difference between two numbers, measures both by dividing them, as appears in <term>multiple proportions<gloss>ratios where the larger number is an exact multiple of the smaller, such as 2:1 or 3:1</gloss></term>. For in that which is "double" <note>the 2:1 ratio, which creates the octave</note>, that part measures the whole which is the difference between the two numbers; for example, between two and four, the number **two** measures both. Between three and six, the same is true of the number **three**...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical intervals, specifically moving into the mathematical proofs of why certain ratios are considered "consonant" (pleasant) and others are not. It builds on the definitions of the previous page by applying Pythagorean number theory to sound.</meta>

<margin>
Consonances are found in **multiple** and **superparticular** <term>superparticular: a ratio where the larger number contains the whole of the smaller number plus one fractional part of it (e.g., 3:2 or 4:3)</term> proportions. <unclear>Wherefore</unclear> they are found through measurement; hence it <unclear>follows</unclear> <unclear>that</unclear> <unclear>when</unclear> there is a difference between two numbers, it measures both <unclear>parts</unclear>, just as between two and four the difference is 2, and it measures both. <unclear>Hence</unclear>, <unclear>from elsewhere</unclear>, <unclear>it shows</unclear> <unclear>two</unclear> numbers where the larger exceeds the remaining smaller one by a difference that <unclear>measures</unclear> both numbers <unclear>through</unclear> <unclear>their</unclear> <unclear>common</unclear> <unclear>measure</unclear>, <unclear>as</unclear> is <unclear>again</unclear> found in multiples and superparticulars.
</margin>

[...the number two] measures both. And between two and one, that same **unity** <note>original: "unitaś"; the number 1</note> is what measures both. Likewise, between nine and six, that same **number three** <note>original: "ternariuś"</note> measures both. 

Likewise, in **superparticulars**, if there is a **sesquialtera** <term>sesquialtera: the ratio 3:2 (literally "one and a half"). In music, this produces the interval of a Perfect Fifth</term> proportion—such as six and four—the **number two** <note>original: "biariuś"</note> is that which measures both, because it is the difference between them. 

But if it is a **sesquitertia** <term>sesquitertia: the ratio 4:3 (literally "one and a third"). In music, this produces the interval of a Perfect Fourth</term> proportion—such as eight to six—the same number two measures both. 

This, however, does not happen in other types of inequality, such as in the **superpartient** <term>superpartient: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number plus more than one part of it (e.g., 5:3)</term>. For if you compare five to three, the number two—which is their difference—measures neither; for compared once to three it is less, but when doubled it exceeds it. Likewise, compared twice to five it is less, but tripled it exceeds it. 

And for that reason, this type of inequality is separated from the nature of consonances, because in the first two types which form consonances, many things are similar, but in this superpartient and the other two composite types, there is very little [similarity]. 

For this is proven in this way: for a **double** is nothing other than twice a single. A **triple** is nothing other than thrice a single. A **quadruple** is the same as four times a single.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of musical intervals, specifically applying the "multiple" and "superparticular" ratios discussed previously to the standard musical consonances: the octave, fifth, and fourth. It begins mid-sentence, concluding the logic of the "double" ratio.</meta>

<margin>
¶ Take note of this:
Since equal-sounding consonances must consist of specific numbers, it is necessary that a ratio of inequality be joined to equal numbers, specifically that ratio which is closest to equality. In the mixing of numbers, that proportion of inequality called **double** <note>original: "dupla"; the ratio 2:1</note> is used, which exceeds by one that which is equal to unity. Therefore, the double ratio is rightly fitted to equal-sounding voices, namely the **diapason** <term>diapason: the interval of an octave</term>; and the double-double, namely the **quadruple**, to the **bisdiapason** <term>bisdiapason: the interval of two octaves</term>. Whence the proportions dividing the double ratio, such as the **sesquialtera** <term>sesquialtera: the ratio 3:2, which forms a Perfect Fifth</term>, are to be fitted to those consonances that divide the equal-sounding *diapason*; thus the **diapente** <term>diapente: the interval of a Perfect Fifth</term> is coupled to the *sesquialtera*, and the **diatessaron** <term>diatessaron: the interval of a Perfect Fourth</term> is coupled to the **sesquitertia** <term>sesquitertia: the ratio 4:3</term>. Consonances joined with equal-sounding ones produce other consonances, such as the **diapason-diapente** <term>diapason-diapente: an octave plus a fifth</term> in triple proportion. Only **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), an influential Greco-Roman mathematician and music theorist</note> proves the **diapason-diatessaron** <term>diapason-diatessaron: an octave plus a fourth (an 11th)</term>.
</margin>

...in equality, that proportion of inequality which is **double** [2:1] is the first species of the **multiple genus** <term>multiple genus: a category of ratios where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller (2:1, 3:1, 4:1, etc.)</term>. It is that in which the larger number exceeds the smaller by an amount equal to the smaller itself, just as two exceeds one by one, which is equal to that same unit. 

Rightly, therefore, the **double proportion** is fitted to equal-sounding voices, namely the **diapason**; while the double-double, namely the **quadruple** [4:1], is fitted to the **bisdiapason**. 

Those proportions that divide the double proportion must be fitted to those consonances that divide the equal-sounding **diapason**. From this it follows that the **diapente** is coupled with the **sesquialtera** [3:2] ratio, while the **diatessaron** is coupled with the **sesquitertia** [4:3] comparison. 

Furthermore, consonances joined with equal-sounding ones produce other consonances, such as the **diapason-diapente** in the **triple proportion** [3:1]. Therefore, the equal-sounding intervals are the **diapason** and **bisdiapason**, because through their tempering and mixture, a single and simple sound is produced in a certain way. 

And indeed, the **diapason** in double proportion is the first in the multiple genus; the **diapason-diapente** is the second in the triple proportion; and the **bisdiapason** is the third in the quadruple proportion of the same multiple genus. 

The **diapente** is proven to consist of the first ratio in the **superparticular genus** <term>superparticular genus: ratios where the larger number is the smaller plus one (3:2, 4:3, etc.)</term>, which is the **sesquialtera**; and the **diatessaron** is the second, in the **sesquitertia** proportion. 

Although the **diatessaron** consonance joined to the **diapason** [the interval of an eleventh] does not sound consonant to the ears, **Ptolemy** nevertheless proves it to be a consonance—not by the judgment of the ears, but by reason—in this way: 

Because the **diapason** consonance effects such a joining of voices that one and the same str— <note>the text breaks here; likely "string" (nervus)</note>

It also introduces Ptolemy's rational defense of the octave-plus-fourth as a consonance, despite how it might sound to the ear.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical ratios and consonances. It picks up from the previous page's explanation of why the "double" ratio (2:1) is the foundation of musical harmony, specifically focusing on the unique properties of the octave.</meta>

<margin>
The equal-sounding **diapason** <term>diapason: an octave</term>, whether by itself or joined with other consonances, always preserves a whole and inviolate sound—hence the **diatessaron** <term>diatessaron: a Perfect Fourth</term> is joined to the equal-sounding interval as if to a single string; and it belongs to the **superpartient** genus <term>superpartient: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number once, plus more than one part of it (e.g., 8:3)</term>, etc.
<note>Vertical note in margin:</note>
For it is measurable if it is sustained among others.
</margin>

<margin>
This occurs with the number ten <note>original: "denario numero"</note>; when five other numbers are added to it, it is always preserved whole—as in eleven, twelve, etc.—which does not happen with other numbers. For if you add two to three, they immediately become five, etc.
</margin>

...is seen to be [the first species], which the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of the philosopher Pythagoras, who believed the universe was governed by numerical ratios</note> also agree upon: if any consonance is added to it, it is preserved whole and inviolate. Therefore, the consonance of the **diatessaron** [fourth] is joined to the equal-sounding **diapason** [octave] as if to a single string. It is shown that this occurs because the *diapason* is almost like a single voice, and is such a consonance that it creates, in a certain way, one single sound.

And just as with the number ten, whatever is added to it is preserved whole and inviolate, while in other numbers this does not happen in the same way. For example, if you add two to three, they immediately become five, and the "species" (the identity) of the number is changed. However, if you add those same two to ten, you make twelve; the number two, joined to the ten, is preserved. Likewise for the number three and all others in the same way.

In the same way, this consonance of the *diapason* [octave] preserves whatever other consonance it receives; it neither changes them nor turns a consonance into a dissonance. Whence, just as if the consonance of the **diapente** <term>diapente: a Perfect Fifth</term> is added to the equal-sounding *diapason*, the mixture of the **diapason-diapente** <term>diapason-diapente: an octave plus a fifth (a 12th)</term> is created, consisting in **triple proportion** <note>the ratio 3:1</note>. So also the *diatessaron* [fourth] joined to the equal-sounding *diapason* will yield another consonance by the addition of one to the other. Although this does not arise from a **multiple** or **superparticular** ratio <note>These were the only ratios traditionally considered "pure" enough for consonances</note> (from which that quantity between two terms is the common measure of the difference of both), it nevertheless arises from the **superpartient genus**, from the ratio of **eight to three** [8:3] and sixteen...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical ratios and consonances, specifically addressing how certain intervals—like the octave-plus-fourth—relate to the simple octave discussed on the previous page. It transitions from the physics of sound (the "hammers" of Pythagoras) to the fundamental definition of number.</meta>

...to six, as was shown in the examination of the weights of the **second hammer** <note>This refers to the legend of Pythagoras, who allegedly discovered the mathematical ratios of musical intervals by listening to the different weights of hammers striking an anvil in a blacksmith's forge.</note> to the sixth. Thus, the **diapason-diatessaron** <term>diapason-diatessaron: an interval of an octave plus a fourth (an 11th)</term> does not harmonize for the ears in the same way, because it is of that same ratio [8:3]; the difference between the smaller terms is not a common measure of both. Nevertheless, it will be considered a consonance because very frequently and appropriately either a **tone** <term>tone: a whole step</term> or a **sesquioctave ratio** <term>sesquioctave: a 9:8 ratio, the mathematical definition of a whole tone</term> is joined to it, and then it becomes the **diapason-diapente** <term>diapason-diapente: an interval of an octave plus a fifth (a 12th)</term> consonance in a **triple ratio** <note>3:1 ratio</note>, which clings sweetly to the ears. 

Moreover, let there also be an addition of a **diapente** <term>diapente: a Perfect Fifth</term> most appropriately to that same *diapason-diatessaron*, and then the **bisdiapason** <term>bisdiapason: a double octave (a 15th)</term> corresponds to the ears in a **quadruple ratio** <note>4:1 ratio</note>. Nor should it be a wonder that the *diapason-diatessaron*, although perceived by the ears, is [not always] included in the count of consonances, since the **diatessaron** <term>diatessaron: a Perfect Fourth</term> consonance does not harmonize simply for the ears by itself, but rather creates a dissonance. But because either a tone or a *diapente* is very easily joined to it—by whose connection the other is made into a consonance—it is included in the number of consonances by both reason and the judgment of the ears, especially since we are led by the authority of so great and famous a man as **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), an influential Greco-Roman mathematician and music theorist who argued that the octave-plus-fourth should be considered a consonance.</note>.

### What a Number Is. Chapter VI.

<margin>
**Isidore** <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636), an archbishop and encyclopedist whose works were standard textbooks in the Middle Ages.</note>: A number is a multitude made up of units.

### Chapter VII: On the Distinction of Numbers

<margin>
No number is equal to another; hence they are also unequal and diverse among themselves. Individual numbers are finite in themselves, but infinite in relation to all things; therefore they are subject to discrete quantity.
</margin>
Of numbers, one is **even**, another is **odd**. Again, among even numbers, one is **evenly even** <term>pariter par: a number that can be halved repeatedly until reaching 1 (i.e., a power of 2 like 4, 8, 16)</term>, another is **evenly odd** <term>pariter impar: an even number whose half is an odd number (e.g., 6, 10, 14)</term>, and another is **unevenly even**. Among the odd numbers, one is **primary and simple**, another is **secondary and composite**, and another is **intermediate**. An even number is that which can be divided into two equal parts, such as two, four, eight, and ten.
<margin>
Of numbers, some are even, others odd. **Even** is that which can be divided into two equal parts. **Odd** is that which cannot.
</margin>
An odd number, however, is one that cannot be divided into two equal parts, because one unit in the middle is either missing or remains over, such as three, five, seven, and nine.
<margin>
Of the even numbers, some are **evenly even**, others **evenly odd**, and others **unevenly even**.
</margin>
An **evenly even** number is one that is divided equally according to an even number until it reaches indivisible **unity** <note>original: *unitatem*; in medieval mathematics, 1 was often considered the source of numbers rather than a number itself</note>, such as sixty-four, of which the half is thirty-two, the half of these is sixteen, and of those, eight, the half of which makes four; again, the half of these is the **dyad** <note>original: *binarium*; the number two</note>; of the dyad, however, unity—which is indivisible—holds the half. An **evenly odd** number is one that receives a division into equal parts, and [those] parts...
<margin>
An evenly even number is one that can be divided equally according to an even number until it reaches unity, as in:
64, 32, 16, 8, 4, 2
</margin>

<insert>
An **evenly odd** number is one that is divided into two equal parts by a single cut, and from there the parts are equally indivisible <note>Meaning the resulting halves are odd numbers and cannot be halved again into whole integers.</note>, such as six, ten, etc.
</insert>

This is because the number three clearly does not accept a halved division; indeed, we encounter the number three itself before reaching unity, and we cannot cut it.

An **unevenly uneven** number is one that is measured unevenly by an odd number, such as 25 and 49. Since these are odd numbers, they are also divided by odd parts, such as seven times seven and five times five.

For these, there is only one aliquot part.

<meta>This page continues the classification of numbers, moving from the relationship between odd numbers (relatively prime vs. composite) to the classification of even numbers based on the sum of their factors. It builds directly on the previous page's discussion of "simple" and "composite" odd numbers.</meta>

...[seen to be] intermediate and in another way composite, as for example when the number **nine** <note>original: "nouenarius"</note> is compared to **twenty-five**. It is primary and incomposite <note>this refers to numbers being "relatively prime" to each other</note> because it has no common number between them except only the monadic, that is, **unity** <note>the number 1</note>. If indeed the same nine is compared to **fifteen**, it is secondary and composite because there is between them a common number beyond unity, namely the number **three**. If three is taken three times, it precisely constitutes nine, but taken five times, it completes fifteen.

Again, of even numbers, some are **superfluous** <term>superfluus: today known as an "abundant number," where the sum of its divisors is greater than the number itself</term>, others are **diminutive** <term>diminutivus: today known as a "deficient number," where the sum of its divisors is less than the number itself</term>, and others are **perfect**. 

Superfluous numbers are those whose aliquot parts <term>aliquot part: a divisor that goes into a number exactly, such as 3 being an aliquot part of 12</term>, when brought together, exceed the total sum, such as the number **twelve**. 

<margin>
**Aliquot** [part] is so called because it can be stated exactly how many times [it goes into the whole].
</margin>

An aliquot part is so called because, when taken a certain number of times, it returns its whole, so that it can be said which part it is—such as the first, or second, third, fourth, and so on. 

<margin>
Of even numbers, some are superfluous, some diminutive, others perfect. Superfluous are those whose aliquot parts joined together exceed themselves, as with twelve, whose aliquot parts return sixteen.
</margin>

Wherefore, the aliquot parts of the number twelve are: **unity**, which is its twelfth part; **two**, which is its sixth part; **three**, which is its fourth part; **four** <note>The Latin text repeats "ternarius" (three) here, but the mathematical context and the "third part" (tertia) clearly indicate the number four</note>, which is its third part; and **six**, which is its half part. All of these, when led into a sum, exceed the total sum of twelve; for one, two, three, four, and six, when led together, exceed twelve and complete the number sixteen. 

Moreover, diminutive numbers are those whose aliquot parts, when led into a sum, do not complete the whole itself, but fall short.

<margin>
Diminutive [numbers] are those whose aliquot parts led together fall short of the sum, as with eight and nine; the parts of the former return seven, the parts of the latter return four, etc.
</margin>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of number types, concluding the section on diminutive (deficient) numbers and beginning the explanation of perfect numbers, including their theological significance in the works of St. Augustine.</meta>

<margin>
**Perfect [numbers]** <unclear>are those</unclear> whose all aliquot parts <term>aliquot part: a divisor that fits into a larger number a whole number of times</term>, considered together, return the numbers themselves—such as **six** <note>original: "senary"</note> within ten, and **twenty-eight** within a hundred, and **four hundred ninety-six** within a thousand. **Six** is the first perfect number, in which God perfected all His works.
</margin>

...from the total sum, such as the number **eight** <note>original: "octonarius"</note> and the number **nine** <note>original: "nouenarius"</note>. For **eight** has an eighth part, namely **unity** <note>the number 1</note>; a fourth part, namely **two** <note>original: "binarius"</note>; and a half, namely **four** <note>original: "quatnarius"</note>. If these parts are joined together, they do not complete the whole sum, since they only combine to make the number **seven**. Likewise, the aliquot parts of **nine**, when brought into a sum, are far from the whole sum; for its ninth part is **unity** and its third part is **three**, which when added together constitute the number **four**. However, **six** is not called an aliquot part of the number **nine**, because it cannot be stated exactly "how many" times it goes into it <note>this refers to the definition of a proper divisor: 6 does not divide 9 evenly</note>.

Truly **perfect numbers** are those whose aliquot parts, when brought into a sum, return precisely that whole, as the number **six** demonstrates. Its sixth part, namely **unity**, and its third part, namely **two**, and its half, namely **three**, when placed together, complete the whole itself, namely **six**. For nothing is left over, and nothing is lacking, but they produce the six itself precisely. 

Indeed, within the number ten, the perfect number is **six**; within a hundred, it is **twenty-eight**. Within a thousand, truly, it is **four hundred ninety-six**. The perfection of the number **six** is certainly commended by **Augustine** in the eleventh book of *The City of God*, in the chapter "This, moreover" <note>original: "hec autem," referring to De Civitate Dei, Book 11, Chapter 30</note>. For there we read: "This [the creation], moreover, is narrated as being perfected in six days on account of the perfection of the number six, with the same day repeated six times..."

**N**ow this also must be noted: that those things which are naturally simple are suitable for comparison. And since **heaviness** <note>original: "gravitas"; likely referring here to low musical pitch or weight</note> and **sharpness** <note>original: "acumen"; referring to high musical pitch or acute angles</note> consist in quantity, those things which maintain the property of **discrete quantity** <term>discrete quantity: things that can be counted as individual units, like apples or integers</term> seem to preserve the harmony of nature most greatly. For while one kind of quantity is **discrete** and the other is **continuous** <term>continuous quantity: things that are measured rather than counted, like a line, time, or space</term>, that which is discrete is not at all limited, but proceeds toward the infinite through larger values. For in discrete quantity, **unity** <note>the number 1</note> is the smallest and most finite thing, but truly the **plurality** <note>the total count</note> increases toward infinity; thus **number**, because it begins from a finite unity, has no end as it grows. 

But **continuous quantity** is indeed finite as a whole, yet it is diminished through infinite [divisions]. For a **line**, which is continuous, is always diminished by infinite division, even though its total sum might be a foot in length or some other defined **measure**. ...since it grows to infinity. But **continuous quantity** <term>continuous quantity: that which can be divided indefinitely, such as a line, where you can always find a smaller point</term> is diminished toward infinity. **Multiplicity** <note>original: "multiplicitas"; referring to ratios like 2:1 or 3:1</note>, because it has no end of growing, preserves the nature of number most of all. **Superparticularity** <term>superparticular ratio: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller plus one fractional part of it, such as 3:2 (a whole plus a half) or 4:3 (a whole plus a third)</term>, however, since it diminishes the property toward an infinite smaller degree, preserves the nature of **continuous quantity**; for it diminishes the smaller [value] since it always contains it plus either its **half part**, or **third**, or **fourth**, or **fifth**.

For a **part** is always named from a larger number. The **fourth** decreases from four itself; for since the **third** is named from three, and the **fourth** from four, and since four exceeds three, the fourth is found to be smaller than the third. The **superpartient** <term>superpartient ratio: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller plus several fractional parts, such as 5:3 (a whole plus two-thirds)</term>, however, already departs in a certain way from simplicity. For it has two or three or four parts in addition, and departing from simplicity, it overflows into a certain plurality of parts.

Therefore, every **multiplicity** contains itself in its wholeness. For the **double** <note>the 2:1 ratio, the octave</note> has the whole smaller quantity twice. The **triple** <note>the 3:1 ratio</note> truly contains the whole smaller quantity three times, and others in the same way. But **superparticularity** preserves nothing whole, but exceeds either by a half part, or a third part, or a fourth, or a fifth; yet it operates by division into single and simple parts. But **superpartient inequality** neither preserves a whole nor takes [it] away as single parts; and for that reason, the **Pythagoreans** <note>followers of the philosopher Pythagoras who prioritized simple mathematical ratios in music</note> do not admit this class to musical consonances at all. # ON THE DEFINITION AND DISTINCTION OF RATIOS. CHAPTER I

<margin>
Let this be your study, whoever you are, who seeks the truth of ratios.
</margin>

It is necessary to open the consideration of **ratios** <note>original: "proportionum"; in this context, it refers to both mathematical ratios and the philosophical concept of proportions</note> through which, as soon as possible, young students being introduced to the summit of music may be able to investigate musical consonances <term>consonance: a combination of notes that sound pleasant or stable together</term>.

**In two ways**, according to Albert <note>likely referring to Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280), a philosopher who wrote on the logic of music and mathematics</note>, ratio is considered: for one is spoken of "commonly," and the other "properly." A **ratio commonly so-called** is the relationship of two things compared to each other in some third, univocal <term>univocal: when a word is used with the exact same meaning in both cases, like "animal" applied to a human and a cat</term> category. It is notably called "univocal" because, although a stylus <note>a pointed tool for writing on wax or clay</note> may be called "sharp" and a voice may be called "sharp," nevertheless, because "sharpness" is not used univocally of the sharpness of a voice and the sharpness of a stylus, the voice and the stylus are not compared to each other in sharpness. For this reason, we are not accustomed to saying that a stylus is "sharper" than a voice, nor "as sharp," nor vice versa. Similarly, although honey is "sweet" and a voice is "sweet," yet because "sweetness" is not used univocally of the sweetness of honey and the sweetness of a voice, we do not compare honey and the voice to each other in sweetness.

<summary>This page marks the start of Book III, introducing the study of ratios as the foundation for musical harmony. It distinguishes between general comparisons of quality (common ratios) and mathematical relationships, emphasizing that valid comparisons require terms to be used in the same sense.</summary>
<keywords>ratio, proportion, Albertus Magnus, univocal, sharpness, sweetness, music theory, logic</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Book III, specifically Chapter I, which was established on the previous page. It moves from the general philosophical definition of a "ratio" (comparison in a third category) to the specific mathematical and musical definitions of rational, irrational, equal, and unequal ratios.</meta>

But **ratio** <note>original: "proportio"</note> properly accepted is the relationship of two quantities of the same kind to each other. Likewise, of ratios, one is **rational**, another is **irrational**. A **rational ratio** is the relationship of commensurable <term>commensurable: quantities that can be measured by the exact same unit, like two lengths that can both be measured in whole inches</term> quantities to each other. Or thus: a rational ratio is one that is immediately named <note>original: "denominatur"; in medieval mathematics, a ratio is "named" by the number or fraction that describes it</note> by some number.

Quantities are called **commensurable** when there is some common measure that yields each of them precisely, such as **six to four**: two <term>binarius: the number two</term>, which is their difference, measures both of them; for taken twice it constitutes four <term>quaternarius: the number four</term>, and joined three times it completes six <term>senarius: the number six</term>, as was touched upon in the fifth [chapter] of the second [book].

Indeed, quantities are called **incommensurable** when there is no common measure <note>In this specific logical framework, the author may be excluding "one" as a common measure, as "one" was often considered the source of numbers rather than a number itself</note> yielding each of them precisely, such as **five to three**; for their difference is two, which measures neither of them. Indeed, if it is taken twice, it exceeds three <term>ternarius: the number three</term>, and if taken three times, it transcends five <term>quinarius: the number five</term>; since that two, connected only twice, does not complete that five.

An **irrational ratio** is the relationship of incommensurable quantities to each other. Or thus: an irrational ratio is one that cannot be immediately named by some number, but is immediately named by some ratio that is itself immediately named by some number <note>This describes what we would call a "root"—for example, the "half of a double ratio" represents the ratio of a square's diagonal to its side ($\sqrt{2}$)</note>.

Likewise, of ratios, one is of **equality**, another is of **inequality**. A **ratio of equality** is the relationship of two equal terms to each other, such as **two to two**

<summary>This page defines the mathematical foundations of ratios, distinguishing between rational and irrational proportions based on whether they share a common divisor. It sets the stage for calculating musical intervals by defining how numbers relate to one another as "terms."</summary>
<keywords>ratio, proportion, rational ratio, irrational ratio, commensurable, incommensurable, equality, inequality, Boethian mathematics</keywords>

--- Page 74 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page regarding mathematical ratios. While the previous page focused on commensurability (how numbers can be measured by a common unit), this page defines "terms," categorizes ratios of inequality, and explains why music relies on inequality rather than equality, citing the authority of Boethius.</meta>

For a **term** <note>original: "Terminus"</note> in this consideration of ratio is understood as a number. Truly, a **ratio of inequality** is the relationship of two unequal terms to each other, such as four to three, or two to one, and vice versa.

Of unequal ratios, one is of **greater inequality**, the other of **lesser inequality**. A **ratio of greater inequality** is the relationship of a larger term to a smaller one, such as two to one and four to three. But a **ratio of lesser inequality** is the relationship of a smaller term to a larger one, such as two to three and three to four.

In these ratios of greater and lesser inequality, there is a certain excess or **difference**, and it is that number by which the greater exceeds the smaller. For example, if we compare six to four, the number **two** <term>binarius: the number two</term> is called the difference; for the number **six** <term>senarius: the number six</term> exceeds the number **four** <term>quaternarius: the number four</term> by two.

For the discipline of music encompasses these ratios of inequality, but not those of equality; because, as was shown more extensively in the first and third chapters of the second book, **consonances** <term>consonantie: musical intervals that sound harmonious together</term> are made from dissimilar and unequal sounds. Therefore, they are explained by unequal ratios.

Nevertheless, it is worth knowing that just as **unity** <note>original: "unitas"; the number one</note>, which is not considered a "number" itself but the source of all numbers, is the principle of number, so also **equality** provides the origins for all unequal ratios. This is shown by **Boethius** <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 480–524 AD), whose "De institutione musica" was the standard music theory textbook of the Middle Ages</note> when he treats the three types of proportionality in the second [book] of his *Music*.

<summary>This page defines the mathematical "term" as a number and explains the distinction between ratios of greater and lesser inequality. It argues that musical harmony is derived from these unequal ratios, as equality of pitch cannot produce consonance.</summary>
<keywords>ratio of inequality, greater inequality, lesser inequality, difference, consonance, Boethius, unity, musical proportions</keywords>

### On proportionality and how ratios of inequality are born from equality. Chapter II.

**Proportionality** <note>original: "Proportionalitas"</note> is not defined as the relationship of only two terms to each other, but rather as a collection of equal ratios. For this reason, it is established that a proportionality consists of at least three terms, though it often contains more, such as four, five, six, or even more. It is indeed considered in three ways: namely, **arithmetically**, **geometrically**, and **harmonically** <note>original: "arythmetice geometrice & armonice"</note>. 

For when only three terms are established, and the first term maintains the same ratio to the second as the second does to the third, it is then called a proportionality. In this arrangement, there is a second term which is called the **middle** <term>medius: the central number in a mathematical sequence</term>. The ratios of the extremes and the middle term relate to one another through a threefold participation.

For either the **difference** <term>differentia: the quantity found by subtracting the smaller number from the larger</term> between the smaller term and the middle is equal to the difference between the middle and the largest, but the ratio is not equal. This is seen in these numbers: **1, 2, 3**. Here, there is a difference of only one unit between each number, yet the ratio is not equal; for two to one is a **double ratio** <note>original: "proportio dupla"; a 2:1 ratio</note>, but three to two is a **sesquialter ratio** <term>sexqualtera: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller once plus half of it, such as 3:2</term>.

Or, the ratio is equal in both pairs, but they are not established by equal differences. This is seen in these numbers: **1, 2, 4**. For two compared to one constitutes a double ratio in the same way that four relates to two. But between the number four <term>quaturnarium: the number four</term> and

<summary>This page defines proportionality as a series of equal ratios requiring at least three terms and introduces the three types: arithmetic, geometric, and harmonic. It illustrates the difference between arithmetic sequences (equal differences) and geometric sequences (equal ratios) using simple numerical examples.</summary>
<keywords>proportionality, arithmetic proportion, geometric proportion, middle term, double ratio, sesquialter ratio, mathematical terms</keywords>

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical definitions of proportionality, completing the example of arithmetic progression (1, 2, 3) from the previous page and introducing the harmonic progression, which is essential for understanding musical intervals.</meta>

the number two. <note>original: "binarium binarius"</note> Between unity and two, however, unity makes the difference. The third kind of **proportionality** <note>original: "proportionalitatis genus"</note> is that which consists neither of the same ratios nor the same differences; instead, as the greatest is to the least (or extreme to extreme), so the **difference** <term>differentia: the numerical distance between two values</term> of the larger terms is to the difference of the smaller terms, as is evident in these numbers: **3, 4, 6**.

For six compared to three makes a **double relationship** <note>original: "duplam collationem"; a 2:1 ratio</note>. Between 6 and 4, the number two brings the difference, but between 4 and 3, a unit (1) is the difference. If two is compared to one, a **double ratio** <note>original: "dupla proportio"</note> is created, just as results from six compared to three.

Therefore, just as the greatest **term** <term>terminus: a single number within a mathematical series</term> is to the least, so is the difference of the larger terms to the difference of the smaller terms. Thus, according to **Albert** <note>likely Albertus Magnus, a prominent medieval scholar</note> and **Boethius** <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius, whose 6th-century works on music and math were the standard textbooks for centuries</note>, that proportionality in which the differences are equal is called **arithmetic**. However, that in which the ratios are equal is called **geometric**. But that in which there is a similarity between the ratio of the extreme numbers and the ratio of the differences to each other is called **harmonic** <note>original: "armonica"</note>, as appears in these figures.

A continuous proportionality is when only one middle term is established, which is then placed after the smaller value and before the larger one, as is shown in the previously written ratios and figures. A disjoint proportionality, however, is when two middle terms are established, as in this geometric example: 1, 2, 3, 6. When three equal terms are set down, there are two ways by which <term>arithmetic proportionality</term> is produced: 

1. Let the first term be equal to the first; 
2. Let the second be equal to the first and second combined; 
3. Let the third be equal to the first, second, and third combined. 

This is demonstrated by this example: 1, 1, 1. These are three units. For the first unit, another unit is substituted which is equal to it. - Let the second number be equal to the first and two second unities, which is **3**.

In this case, unity creates the difference of the terms. For between the binary and unity, as well as between the ternary and binary, unity is the difference, and no natural number is interposed. For immediately after unity is the binary, and after the binary, the ternary is established, and let it be...

- The second (1+1) is **2**.
- The third (1+2+1) is **4**.

### On the Contrast between Continuous and Discrete Quantity: Chapter Three

As was already stated in the final chapter of the second book, there are two types of quantity: one is **continuous** and the other is **discrete**. Continuous quantity is called **magnitude** <note>original: "magnitudo"</note>; it begins from a finite quantity and is able to be multiplied toward infinity so that the limits of its ending...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of mathematical quantities, specifically transitioning from the derivation of proportions to the philosophical distinction between continuous magnitude and discrete multitude. ...[a limit] it does not admit <note>original: "terminus non admittat"; the text begins mid-sentence, likely completing a thought on the lack of boundaries in certain divisions</note>, and indeed in the quantity of its measure it admits a limit when it grows, yet it decreases by multiplying its divisions. For if there were a **two-foot line** <term>two-foot line: a "bipedalis linea," a standard unit of measurement used in geometry to illustrate divisibility</term> or a line of any other measure, it can be divided into two equal parts, and its half can be cut into another half, and that half again into another half; and no end to the cutting of the **magnitude** <term>magnitude: a continuous quantity, like a line or a shape, that can be divided infinitely</term> ever occurs. Therefore, magnitude is finite as far as its larger mode is concerned, but it becomes infinite when it begins to decrease.

**Discrete quantity**, however, is called **multitude** <term>multitude: a collection of distinct units, like the sequence of numbers 1, 2, 3...</term>. This begins from **unity** <term>unity: the number one, which the ancients viewed as the indivisible source of all numbers</term> (than which nothing is smaller). Being limited or finite in that unity, it is interminable toward the greater. For no end to its growth occurs; even as they appear finite, they reach toward infinity by growing. 

The properties of these quantities are therefore contrary to one another. For one <note>magnitude</note>, when multiplied by division, decreases in quantity; the other <note>multitude</note> receives an increase of quantity. Since numbers are the subjects of discrete quantity because of their infinity (as has been seen), sounds themselves—which are generated from the proportions of those very numbers—are subject to the same principles; for they are both discrete (that is, distinct from one another) and infinite. 

Indeed, I say that sounds are known to be infinite. For although it is said in the **Second of the Second** <note>original: "Secundo Secundi"; likely a reference to a specific section of a classical philosophical or logical text, such as Aristotle's *Physics* or a commentary</note> that the nature of man has established a limit for the human voice beyond which no intensity can exceed, this happens only because of the weakness of the physical organs and the human breath. Yet if these sounds and strings are added in the sharper part of the instrument—because their proportions become so much smaller and more diminished the further they proceed toward infinity—they seem to preserve the property of **continuous quantity** <term>continuous quantity: a magnitude like a line that can be divided forever, unlike a number which is made of indivisible units</term>.

Since, therefore, both continuous and discrete quantity are infinite, **philosophy** néanmoins treats them as if they were finite things. For within infinite things, something bounded is found, to which the sharpness of proper speculation can rightly be applied.

For of **magnitude**, some things are immobile, like the earth; others are mobile, like the sphere of the world and whatever revolves within it with <unclear>right</unclear> speed...

For some **discrete quantities** <term>discrete quantity: "discreta quantitas," numbers that are distinct units, like individual pebbles, as opposed to "continuous" things like a line</term> are **by themselves** <term>by themselves: "per se," referring to numbers studied as independent integers (1, 2, 3...)</term>, such as two or three or four and individual numbers. Others, however, are **related to something** <term>related to something: "ad aliquid," numbers that only have meaning when compared to another, such as "double" or "triple"</term>, such as the double, triple, and quadruple, and others which arise from comparison.

Indeed, **geometry** holds the study of immobile magnitude <note>original: "immobilis magnitudinis," referring to shapes and measures that do not change or move</note>. However, the science of **astronomy** pursues the study of mobile magnitude <note>original: "mobilis [magnitudinis]," referring to the movement of celestial bodies</note>. **Arithmetic** is the authority on discrete quantity by itself. But the discipline of **music** is proven to hold the mastery of quantity related to something—and this indeed will be the scope of our present consideration.

### On the five types of proportions. Chapter 4.

There are five types of **unequal proportions** <note>original: "proportionum inequalium," ratios where the two numbers being compared are not the same</note>. One is indeed called **multiplex** <term>multiplex: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number multiple times exactly, such as 2:1 (double) or 3:1 (triple)</term>; another is **superparticular** <term>superparticular: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number once, plus one "part" or fraction of it, such as 3:2 or 4:3</term>; the third is **superpartient** <term>superpartient: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number once, plus several "parts" or fractions of it, such as 5:3</term>. These three are **simple** <note>original: "simplicia," the basic categories from which others are built</note>. The fourth is **multiple-superparticular** and the fifth and last is **multiple-superpartient**; these are **composite**, formed from the first simple types. 

Indeed, in these categories consist all proportions of **greater inequality** <note>original: "maioris inequalitatis," where the first number in the ratio is larger than the second</note>. And opposite to these are another five types of **lesser inequality** <note>original: "minoris inequalitatis," where the first number is smaller than the second</note>, called by the same names but with the prefix **sub-** added to their names, such as **sub-multiple**, **sub-superparticular**, **sub-superpartient**, **sub-multiple-superparticular**, and **sub-multiple-superpartient**. Therefore, the **multiplex** type is when a larger number...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "multiplex" genus of proportions, which was introduced at the end of the previous page. It defines the specific species of the multiplex genus—double, triple, and quadruple—and then introduces the "superparticular" genus, which is the second major category of unequal ratios used in both mathematics and musical tuning.</meta>

...compared to the smaller, contains that smaller one within itself multiple times—such as twice, or thrice, or four times—so that nothing is missing and nothing is superfluous <note>original: "nihil desit nihilque superfluat," meaning the larger number is a perfect multiple of the smaller with no remainder</note>. There are indeed infinite species of this type. The first is called the **double proportion** <note>original: "proportio dupla," the 2:1 ratio, which in music creates the interval of an Octave</note>, which occurs when the larger term, compared to the smaller, contains it twice: such as two to one, four to two, six to three, eight to four, and so on. For two contains **unity** <term>unity: "unitatem," the number one, considered the building block of all numbers</term> within itself twice. Similarly, the **number four** <term>number four: "quaternarius," a Latin term for a group of four or the digit 4</term> contains the **number two** <term>number two: "binarius," the number 2</term>; the number six contains the number three; the number eight contains the number four; nothing is missing from them and nothing exceeds.

The **triple proportion**, which is the second species of the multiplex genus, occurs when the larger term, compared to the smaller, includes it three times within itself: such as three to one, six to two, nine to three, and twelve to four.

The **quadruple proportion**, which is called the third species of this genus, is when the larger number, compared to the smaller, contains it four times within itself: such as four to one, eight to two, twelve to three, and so on; the remaining species acquire their names from their numbers <note>for example, "quintuple" from five, "sextuple" from six, etc.</note>.

The **superparticular genus** is when the larger term, compared to the smaller, contains it within itself only once and no more, and in addition, one **aliquot part** <term>aliquot part: "aliquotam partem," a part of a number that divides it exactly without a remainder, such as 1 being a part of 2 (a half) or 1 being a part of 3 (a third)</term> of that smaller number; such as three to two. For the number three has within itself the whole of the number two once, and in addition its half part, namely unity. Also, four compared to three has the whole smaller number in itself and its third part, namely unity. Of this type also there are infinite [species]...

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general "multiplex" genus to the "superparticular" genus, focusing on ratios where the larger number contains the smaller once plus one aliquot part. It specifically defines sesquialter, sesquitertial, and sesquiquartal ratios, which correspond to the primary musical intervals of the fifth and fourth.</meta>

The species are as follows: the first is indeed called **sesquialter** <note>original: "sexquialtera," the 3:2 ratio, which in music produces the interval of a perfect Fifth</note>, the second **sesquitertial** <note>original: "sexquitertia," the 4:3 ratio, producing a perfect Fourth</note>, the third **sesquiquartal**, the fourth **sesquiquintal**, and they proceed in this order to infinity. 

Therefore, if that **aliquot part** <term>aliquot part: a part of a number that divides it exactly, like 2 is a part of 6</term> by which the larger surpasses the smaller is half of the smaller, it is called a sesquialter proportion. 

<margin>
In "sesquialter," *sesqui* means "half." In others, it means "the whole."
</margin>

This is named from *sesqui*, which means "half," and *altera*, which is "middle" <note>original: "a sexquialtera dicta a sexq quod est semis & altera quae est media"</note>, because a half part of the other number is added equally to the whole number, such as three to two, and six to four. 

If, however, the excess—or the difference, or that aliquot part by which the larger term exceeds the smaller—is a third part of the smaller, it will be called a **sesquitertial proportion**. It is named from *sesqui*, which here represents the whole, and "third part," because the larger number encompasses the whole of the smaller and, in addition, a third part of that smaller: such as four to three and eight to six. Regarding what an aliquot part is, this was more broadly explained in the second [book] of the seventh [of Euclid's Elements] <note>Refers to Euclidean geometry/number theory commonly taught alongside music in the Quadrivium</note>. 

If, indeed, that part by which the larger term exceeds the smaller is a fourth part of the smaller, it will be called a **sesquiquartal proportion**, such as five to four and ten to eight. In a similar way, the remaining species of this genus are determined.

<margin>
In the superparticular genus, the proportion is named from the larger number, not from the smaller.
</margin>

From these examples we must note that every proportion is named after the larger number, and not the smaller. 

The **superpartient** genus <term>superpartient: "superpartiens," a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller once, plus multiple fractional parts (like 5:3, which is 1 and 2/3)</term>, however, occurs when the larger term contains the whole of the smaller within itself once, and furthermore some part of that smaller that is not a single aliquot part, but is composed of two or more aliquot parts of the smaller itself: such as five to three.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical and mathematical ratios. For five contains three within itself only once, and furthermore [contains] the number two, which is not called an **aliquot part** <term>aliquot part: a divisor that goes into a larger number perfectly without a remainder; for example, 1 is an aliquot part of 3, but 2 is not</term> of three, but is composed of two aliquot parts of that same three: that is, from two units, each of which is called a third part of three.

There are also infinite species of this genus. Therefore, if that **non-aliquot part** <note>original: "pars non aliquota," referring to a remainder that cannot divide the base number into equal whole units</note> of the smaller number, by which the larger term exceeds the smaller, contains two aliquot parts of said smaller number, it shall be called a **superbipartient proportion** <note>original: "proportio superbipartiens," a ratio of 1 + 2/n, such as 5:3</note>, such as five to three. 

If, however, that part contains three aliquot parts of the smaller number, it shall be called **supertripartient** <note>original: "suptripartiens," such as 8:5</note>, such as eight to five; and if it contains four, it shall be called **superquadripartient**, such as nine to five. Therefore, the first species of this genus is the superbipartient proportion, the second is the supertripartient, and the third is the superquadripartient; following this order—after applying diligent observation—one proceeds to infinity. 

To determine a more specific name for these proportions, one must examine how those aliquot parts relate to the smaller term: whether they are thirds, fourths, or fifths of the smaller number. If they are thirds, it shall be called a **superpartient third** proportion; if fourths, it is called **superpartient fourths**, and so on to infinity. 

Let us say it this way: when the larger number contains the whole of the smaller and, in addition, a non-aliquot part of it containing two aliquot parts of the smaller, each of which is a third part of that smaller...

If, however, the larger contains the smaller twice and beyond that its third part, it shall be called a **double sesquitertial** <note>original: "dupla sesquitertia," a ratio of 2 and 1/3, or 7:3</note> proportion, such as seven to three; and thus this genus is multiplied through infinite species from the "multiple" and the "superparticular." 

The fifth and last genus is called **multiple superpartient** <note>original: "multiplex superpartiens"</note>. For it occurs when the larger number contains the smaller in its entirety multiple times, and in addition a non-aliquot part of that smaller number comprising several aliquot parts of said smaller number. And if the larger term should have within itself the whole of the smaller twice, and in addition to this a **non-aliquot part** <term>non-aliquot part: a remainder that is not a single, even divisor of the whole (e.g., 2 is not an aliquot part of 3)</term> containing two aliquot parts of that same smaller number, it shall be called a **double superbipartient proportion** <note>original: "proportio dupla superbipartiens"</note>, such as eight to three. And if each of those aliquot parts is a third part of the smaller number itself, it is called **double superbipartient thirds** <note>original: "dupla superbipartiens tertias"</note>, as in the previously mentioned example. In this same way, we shall speak proportionally of other species of **multiple superpartient** <note>original: "multiplicis suppartientis"</note> ratios by mixing multiplicity with the superpartient, following what has been said regarding multiple and superpartient proportions.

Now it must also be understood that the five genera of **lesser inequality** <note>original: "minoris inequalitatis"</note>, which are the opposites of those previously mentioned, maintain the same rule and nature; they differ only by being their contrary. Specifically, in these, the smaller number is contained by the larger. In the genera and species of **greater inequality** <note>original: "maioris inequalitatis"</note>, however, the larger contains and precedes the smaller, whereas in the genera of lesser inequality, the smaller preceding the larger is contained by it. Therefore, the genera of greater and lesser inequality are established by the same rules and measures, but their effects are contrary. For when proportions of greater inequality are established in **figured songs** <term>figured songs: "cantus figuratus," referring to polyphony or measured music where different voices have different rhythms</term>, they diminish the value of the small notes. Conversely, when proportions of lesser inequality are established, the value of the figures is increased and grows according to the arrangement of the established proportion.

A collection of the five aforementioned genera can thus be briefly summarized: every larger term compared to a smaller—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of mathematical proportions, moving from the definition of the five general types (genera) to the specific varieties (species) found within them. It focuses on how to derive these proportions using the sequence of natural numbers.</meta>

# On the formation of the species of any
## Genus, Chapter five

The species of the previously mentioned types of **inequality** <term>inequality: a relationship between two different numbers</term> are infinite within the natural numbers, and they behave in this way: If we compare each natural number to **unity** <note>original: "unitati"; the number 1, which was considered the source of all numbers but not a number itself</note>, we will discover the ordered species of the **multiple genus**. 

For example, if the number 2 is compared to 1, the first species will be the multiple—namely, the **double proportion** <note>original: "proportio dupla"; the ratio 2:1</note>. If 3 is compared to 1, it immediately becomes the second species—namely, the **triple**. If indeed the number 4 is compared to that same 1, it will be the third species—namely, the **quadruple**, and so on, as shown in this sequence: 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8.

When, however, you wish to have the species of the **superparticular genus** <note>original: "genus supparticularis"; where the larger number contains the smaller once plus one aliquot part</note>, let the natural numbers be arranged beginning from 2 (that is, with 1 removed), and let each one be compared to the smaller number closest to it. 

For instance, if we compare 3 to 2, it immediately becomes the first species of the superparticular genus—namely, the **sesquialtera proportion** <note>original: "proportio sesquialtera"; the ratio 3:2, where 3 contains 2 once, plus 1 (half of 2)</note>. But if 4 is compared to 3, it will become the second species of the superparticular genus—namely, the **sesquitertia proportion** <note>original: "proportio sesquitertia"; the ratio 4:3, where 4 contains 3 once, plus 1 (a third of 3)</note>. 

When, however, 5 is compared to 4, it creates the third species of this same genus—namely, the **sesquiquarta proportion** <note>original: "proportio sesquiquarta"; the ratio 5:4</note>. From 6 compared to 5, the fourth species is made—namely, the **sesquiquinta proportion** <note>original: "proportio sesquiquinta"; the ratio 6:5</note>. The number 7...

The number eight compared to seven, however, yields the sixth species—namely, the **sesquiseptimal proportion** <note>8:7</note>. Then the number nine compared to eight produces the seventh species—namely, the **sesquioctave proportion** <note>9:8</note>; and thus the process continues in that same order unto infinity, as shown in this figure:

->3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10<-

But when you wish to produce the infinite species of the **superpartient** 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9 genus <term>superpartient: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller once, plus more than one aliquot part remaining (e.g., 1 and 2/3)</term>, let the natural numbers be arranged not beginning from unity, nor from two, but from the number **three**. When a larger term is compared to this three, let one natural term in the middle be skipped. 

For when we compare **five** to **three**, the number four—which naturally falls between them—is skipped; and from the comparison of five to three, the first species of the superpartient genus is made, which is called the **superbipartient proportion** <note>original: "proportio supbiptiẽns"; the ratio 5:3. It is "bipartient" because two parts (2/3) remain</note>. 

The second species, however, is formed from the next smaller term—namely, **four**—compared to a larger one, with the skipping of two natural numbers (which are 5 and 6). Indeed, the larger term is **seven**, which compared to four creates the second species of this genus, namely the **supertripartient proportion** <note>original: "suptripartiẽtez"; the ratio 7:4. It is "tripartient" because three parts (3/4) remain</note>. 

The third species is formed from the next smaller term—namely, **five**—compared to the larger term **nine**, with the interposition of three natural numbers (which are 6, 7, and 8). And this comparison of nine to five is called the **superquadripartient** <note>original: "supq̃tri"; likely an abbreviation for "superquadripartiens," the ratio 9:5</note>...
