...**tripartient proportion**. But if you proceed in that same order, skipping four natural numbers, the **superquinquepartient proportion** <note>original: "superqncuptiēs"; the ratio 11:6, where 11 contains 6 once, plus five-sixths (5 parts) remaining</note> will be created, which is the fourth species of the aforementioned genus, such as 11 to 6. And in this same way, the process continues unto infinity by increasing the number of skipped terms, as shown in the following figure:

->5, 7, 9, 11, 13<-

In this same order, the diligent observer <term>speculator: in this context, a student or philosopher who meditates on the nature of numbers</term> will unravel all the species of the **multiple**, **superparticular**, and **multiple superpartient** <term>multiple superpartient: a complex ratio where the larger number contains the smaller multiple times, plus more than one part remaining (e.g., 8:3, which is 2 and 2/3)</term> genera, by always joining multiplicity with superparticularity or with superpartience as the work requires, as has already been stated in the preceding chapter.

For from the aforesaid, it is gathered that the **multiple genus of inequality** is far more ancient, worthy, and abundant than the two remaining simple genera, because in the natural arrangement of numbers, it is compared to **unity** <note>the number 1</note>, which is first. 

The **superparticular**, however, is not perfected by a comparison to unity, but by those numbers arranged immediately after unity, such as the ratio of the **ternary** to the **binary** <note>the ratio 3:2, which in music defines the interval of a perfect fifth</note>. 

The **superpartient**, on the other hand, is formed much further within, for it is compared neither by continuous numbers but by skipped ones, nor always by an equal skipping. Rather, it proceeds now by skipping one, now two, now also three, now even four, and thus it increases unto infinity. Furthermore, multiplicity takes its beginning from unity; superparticularity from the binary; and superpartience from the ternary. For these ratios possess a greater power over **musical consonances** <term>consonance: a combination of notes that sounds pleasant and stable; medieval theorists believed these were governed by simple mathematical ratios</term>.

<margin>
The **multiple proportion** <note>original: "multiplex proportio"; ratios like 2:1 or 3:1</note> has greater power toward musical consonances. Following that are the **superparticulars** <note>original: "supparticularij"; ratios like 3:2 or 4:3 where the larger number contains the smaller plus one part</note>.
</margin>

...**multiple proportion**. Next, however, is the **superparticular**. But the **superpartient** <term>superpartient: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller once, plus more than one part remaining (e.g., 5:3)</term> is separated from harmonic concord, as it seems to **Aristotle** and the **Pythagoreans**. But **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the influential 2nd-century astronomer and music theorist</note> dissents from this, as was more extensively evident in the fifth chapter of the second book. However, since Ptolemy himself reasonably sought from that genus only one single consonance—namely, that which is made of the **octave and fourth** <note>original: "ex diapaso & diatessarõ"; the interval of an eleventh</note>—the genus itself is for that reason cast aside from the other harmonies; thus, we shall have no further concern for it from now on. The two composite genera themselves are also to be left behind for the same reason. Therefore, the whole consideration in this process will be of the two simple genera: namely, the **multiple** and the **superparticular**.

---

<margin>
The **superpartient proportion** is separated from harmonic concord, as it appears to Aristotle and the Pythagoreans; but Ptolemy dissents, since from that genus he sought the consonance which is made from the **octave** and **fourth**—that is, the **eleventh**—which is made as an equisonant fourth.
</margin>

### Chapter 6: On the production of the superparticular genus from the multiple, and the superpartient from the superparticular.

Meanwhile, we must speak a little about the **square number**. For it is that which increases in equal dimension, as twice two, thrice three, four times four, five times five, and the like. However, the natural number is called the **side** <note>original: "latus"; what we now call the square root</note> of that square, as it remains simple as it was before the square generation. Therefore, let the continuous natural square numbers be established, under which their sides are placed—namely, the natural numbers, having cast aside **unity** <note>the number 1</note>, which is not a number but the principle of number, in this manner:

| 4 | 9 | 16 | 25 | 36 | 49 | 64 | 81 | 100 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 | 6 | 7 | 8 | 9 | 10 |

<margin>
Unity is not a number, but the principle of number.
</margin>

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of proportions, moving from the general classification of ratios (multiple, superparticular, etc.) to a specific exploration of the properties of square numbers and their relationship to their roots.</meta>

<warning>Bleed-through/ghosting from the facing page is visible on the left side of the leaf.</warning>

I have notably said that **square numbers** <term>square numbers: the product of a number multiplied by itself, such as 4 (2×2), 9 (3×3), or 16 (4×4)</term> are naturally continuous because no other square number is intercepted between them. Therefore, in this figure <note>referring to a diagram likely appearing on the original manuscript page</note>, the upper numbers are squares—or "squaring numbers"—while the lower ones are the **sides** <term>sides: what modern mathematicians call the "square root"</term> of those squares. Wherefore, if you compare each square number to its side, you will produce **multiple proportions** <note>original: "multiplices proportiones"; a ratio where the larger number is a perfect multiple of the smaller</note>. 

For if you compare the first square to the number two—which is its side—you will produce the first species of the multiple genus, namely the **double** <note>original: "duplam"; the ratio of 4 to 2 (4:2), which simplifies to 2:1</note>. If, however, you compare the second square number—namely nine—to its side, which is three, you will find the second species of the multiple, namely the **triple proportion** <note>original: "tripla proportio"; the ratio of 9 to 3 (9:3), which simplifies to 3:1</note>. When the third square number is compared to its side, soon the third species of the multiple arises, namely the **quadruple proportion** <note>original: "quadrupla proportio"</note>, for they are sixteen to four. In this way, the establishment of the multiple proportions has been made in an orderly fashion.

Now, however, let us open the investigation of these squares themselves. If we subtract a smaller continuous square from the next larger continuous square, that part of the larger square which is left over will be exactly as much as the sides of those squares constitute [together]. For example, if we subtract four from nine, five remains; and two and three—which are the sides of both squares—are joined together <note>2 + 3 = 5, which is the difference between the squares 4 and 9</note>. Likewise, if we subtract nine from sixteen, seven remains, which [comes] from three... <note>The text cuts off, but implies 3 + 4 = 7</note>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration from page 37. While the previous page established how to find multiple proportions by comparing a square number to its side (its root), this page explores the relationship between the sum of two "sides" and the difference between their corresponding square numbers.</meta>

...and they are joined by the number **four** <note>original: "quaternario"</note>; that is, from the **sides** <term>sides: what we now call a square root; the number that, when multiplied by itself, produces the square</term> of both squares. The same is also true in other cases. 

But if the **square numbers** <term>square numbers: the product of a number multiplied by itself, such as 4, 9, 16, or 25</term> are not continuous but one has been skipped between them, then their sides together make up only **half** of the remaining part of the larger square <note>meaning the difference between the two squares</note>. 

For instance, if you take **four** away from **sixteen**, **twelve** remains. The half of this is **six** <note>original: "senarius"</note>, which is joined from both of the sides of those squares; for the sides themselves are **two** <note>original: "binarius"</note> and **four** <note>original: "quaternarius"</note> <note>2 + 4 = 6, which is half of the difference (12) between the squares 4 and 16</note>. And in other cases, the same method holds, so long as one square number has always been skipped.

But if **two** square numbers have been skipped, then that which remains from the larger square—after the subtraction of the smaller square—will be such that it constitutes **three times** the sum of the sides; or rather, the joined sides will equal a **third part** of what remains. 

For instance, if we take **four** from **twenty-five**, **twenty-one** remains. Their sides indeed are **two** and **five**, which make **seven**; and this **seven** <note>original: "septenarius"</note> is the third part of **twenty-one**. 

And this is the rule: if **three** square numbers have been skipped, the sides of those squares joined together equal the **fourth part** of that portion which remains from the larger square after the removal of the smaller square from the larger. If, however, **four** square numbers are skipped, their sides will comprise the **fifth part** of what remains. And thus, the fractional parts are named by a number **one greater** than the count of the skipped squares.

*   Let the **third** be equal to the **first**, plus **two** of the **second**, and the **third**.

Now, however, we must consider how much these observations are of use. If the **difference** <term>differentia: the result of subtracting the smaller number from the larger</term> between two numbers has measured them both exactly <note>meaning the difference is a common divisor that goes into both numbers without a remainder</note>, then those numbers are in the same proportion as the numbers that represent how many times that difference fits into them.

For example, let the numbers be **50** and **55**. These, therefore, have a **sesquidecima** <term>sesquidecima: an 11:10 ratio, where the larger number contains the smaller plus one-tenth of it</term> relationship to each other. For their difference is **five**, which is the tenth part of the number 50. This five, therefore, taken ten times, measures 50; but taken eleven times, it measures 55. Indeed, according to the factors of **10** and **11**, the proper difference (that is, the number five) measures the numbers 50 and 55; and indeed, 11 to 10 are joined in a sesquidecima relationship. 

Therefore, the numbers that the proper difference has measured exactly are in the same proportion as the numbers that represent how many times that difference was measured. 

But if any difference between numbers measures those numbers in such a way that the count of the numbers exceeds that same measure <note>i.e., there is a remainder</note>, and the same excess is found in both, and the measure of the difference is smaller than the count of the numbers, then those numbers will possess a **greater** proportion to each other if that which remains after the measurement is [lesser] than when their own difference [measured them as] wholes... <note>The sentence concludes on the following page. This section discusses how ratios change when numbers are not perfect multiples of their difference.</note>

...was measuring. For instance, let there be these two numbers: **53** and **58**. 

Therefore, let **five** <term>quinarius: the number five</term>, which is their **difference** <term>differentia: the numerical gap between two values</term>, measure them. For five measures the number 53 ten times, reaching as far as 50, but **three** <term>ternarius: the number three</term> remains. Again, the same five measures the number 58 eleven times, reaching as far as 55, and in that, **three** <term>trinarius: another form of the number three</term> again remains. 

Therefore, let three be subtracted from both, and they will become **50** and **55**. In this case, it is manifest that the **proportion** <term>proportio: the ratio or relationship between two numbers</term> between 50 and 55 is greater than between 53 and 58. For in smaller numbers a greater proportion is always found, and in larger numbers, a smaller one. <note>In early arithmetic, a "greater" proportion refers to a ratio that is further from 1:1. For example, 55/50 = 1.1, while 58/53 is approximately 1.094; thus 1.1 is the "greater" ratio.</note>

But if that measurement of the difference exceeds the quantity of the numbers, and passes beyond both by the same amount, the proportions of the numbers measured above—with the addition of that sum by which the measuring difference exceeds them—will be smaller than they were before when their own difference was measuring them. 

For example, let the numbers be **48** and **54**. <note>The mathematical logic suggests the second number should be 53, as the following steps involve adding 2 to reach a multiple of 5.</note> Let five be their difference. <note>original: "quinarius differentia est"</note> 

Therefore, let five measure the number 48; ten times makes **50**. Thus, the number 50 exceeds 48 by **two** <term>binarius: the number two</term>. Furthermore, let that same five measure eleven times, which makes **55**; this, in turn, exceeds the number 54 <note>Again, 55 exceeds 53 by two; the author likely meant 53 to keep the excess consistent.</note> by two. 

Let two be added to both and arranged in this way: 

->**48 . 54 . 50 . 55**<-

Therefore, the proportions of 50 to 55 are smaller when compared [to the previous numbers] with the addition of the number two, by which the difference [measures] them...

<summary>The author explains how the ratio between two numbers changes when they are adjusted to become exact multiples of their difference, noting that proportions are larger in smaller numerical sets.</summary>
<keywords>arithmetic, ratio, proportion, difference, multiples, Boethian mathematics</keywords>

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration from the previous discussion regarding how numerical differences and "measuring" (dividing) numbers affect their ratios. It specifically concludes the example of the numbers 48 and 53 and transitions into a general rule about proportions in music and arithmetic.</meta>

...measuring, it passes beyond the numbers **48** and **53**, which were measured by that same difference of **five** <term>quinarius: the number five</term> which exceeded them. For larger and smaller **proportions** <term>proportio: the ratio or relationship between two values</term> are understood in this way: a half part is larger than a third part. A third part is larger than a fourth, a fourth is larger than a fifth, and so on in the same manner. From this it follows that a **sesquialtera** proportion <term>sesquialtera: literally "the whole and a half," representing a 3:2 ratio</term> is greater than a **sesquitertia** <term>sesquitertia: literally "the whole and a third," representing a 4:3 ratio</term>, and the sesquitertia surpasses the **sesquiquarta** <term>sesquiquarta: literally "the whole and a fourth," representing a 5:4 ratio</term>, and the same applies to the rest. <note>In this context, "greater" means the ratio is further from 1:1. A 3:2 ratio (1.5) is mathematically "larger" than a 4:3 ratio (approx 1.33).</note>

Here it happens that among smaller numbers, the proportion of **superparticular** <term>superparticularis: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number plus one equal part of it, such as 3:2 or 4:3</term> numbers is always seen to be greater. This is apparent in the arrangement of the natural number series in this way: **1, 2, 3, 4, 5**. 

For **two** <term>binarius</term> is **double** <term>duplus: a 2:1 ratio</term> compared to **one** <term>unitas</term>. **Three** <term>ternarius</term> is sesquialtera compared to two. **Four** <term>quaternarius</term> is sesquitertia compared to three. The larger numbers are three and four, while the smaller ones are two and one. Therefore, a smaller proportion is contained in larger numbers, and a greater proportion is always contained in smaller ones. 

Hence it is clearly evident that if an equal **quantity** <term>pluralitas: literally "a plurality" or "an amount"</term> is added to any numbers containing a superparticular proportion, the proportion is greater *before* the addition of that equal quantity than it is after the equal quantity has been added to them. <note>For example, 2:1 (a ratio of 2) is greater than 3:2 (a ratio of 1.5), even though 3:2 was formed by adding 1 to both sides of 2:1.</note>

## On the formation of the proportions 
## that produce musical consonances. Chapter 8

**Vocabulary:** proportions, music, consonances (harmonies), the number two, the number three, the number four, sesquialtera (3:2 ratio), sesquitertia (4:3 ratio), natural number.

<summary>This page explains a fundamental rule of Greek and Medieval mathematics: as numbers increase in a natural series, the ratios between consecutive numbers become smaller. It also demonstrates that adding a constant to both terms of a ratio decreases the value of that ratio.</summary>
<keywords>arithmetic, musical theory, superparticular ratios, sesquialtera, sesquitertia, numerical proportions, consonances</keywords>

--- Page 108 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical and mathematical ratios. Having previously established that smaller superparticular ratios are "greater" (meaning they represent larger intervals), the author now begins a systematic categorization of the ratios that create musical consonances, focusing on how the "double" ratio (the octave) can be subdivided into smaller parts.</meta>

# On the proportions that form consonances

<margin>+</margin>
Let us reveal them according to their nature. For in the **multiple** category <term>multiplex: a ratio where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller, such as 2:1 or 3:1</term> there are the **double** <term>dupla: 2:1 ratio</term>, **triple** <term>tripla: 3:1 ratio</term>, and **quadruple** <term>quadrupla: 4:1 ratio</term>. In the **superparticular** category <term>superparticularis: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number plus one equal part of it, such as 3:2</term>, there is the **sesquialtera** <term>literally "the whole and a half," a 3:2 ratio</term>, the **sesquitertia** <term>literally "the whole and a third," a 4:3 ratio</term>, and the **sesquioctave** <term>literally "the whole and an eighth," a 9:8 ratio, which forms the whole tone in music</term>. Indeed, following the principle of equality, that which is **double** is the first species of the multiple category; it is by its nature indivisible into two equal [ratios]. <note>In mathematical music theory, a 2:1 ratio (an octave) cannot be split into two identical rational ratios. For example, the square root of 2 is irrational.</note>

For the double is composed of the first two superparticulars—namely, from the **sesquialtera** and the **sesquitertia**—which are unequal, for these first ones divide the double ratio itself. <note>In musical terms, a perfect fifth (3:2) and a perfect fourth (4:3) added together equal an octave (2:1).</note> Nevertheless, the root of double proportions is formed simply: it is the first one occurring in the natural number series, specifically from **two** <term>binarius: the number two</term> to **one** <term>unitas: the number one</term> by itself, and not through the joining of the sesquialtera and sesquitertia. 

However, through the combination of the sesquialtera and sesquitertia, it is formed in this way: **2, 3, 4**. For **three** <term>ternarius: the number three</term> compared to **two** creates a sesquialtera; **four** <term>quaternarius: the number four</term> compared to **three** creates a sesquitertia; likewise, **four** compared to **two** creates a double ratio. A double proportion also encompasses other superparticular proportions beyond the sesquialtera and sesquitertia, as seen in these numbers: **4, 5, 6, 7, 8**.

For when **six** <term>senarius: the number six</term> is compared to **four** <term>quaternarius</term>, it constitutes a sesquialtera <note>The ratio 6:4 simplifies to 3:2, the musical fifth.</note>. **Eight** <term>octonarius: the number eight</term> compared to **six** creates a sesquitertia <note>The ratio 8:6 simplifies to 4:3, the musical fourth.</note>; likewise, **eight** compared to **four** creates a double ratio. Again, **five** <term>quinarius: the number five</term> compared to **four** is a **sesquiquarta** <term>5:4 ratio</term>; **six** to **five** is a **sesquiquinta** <term>6:5 ratio</term>; **seven** <term>septenarius: the number seven</term> to **six** is a **sesquisexta** <term>7:6 ratio</term>; and **eight** to **seven**...

<summary>This page classifies mathematical ratios into "multiple" and "superparticular" categories and demonstrates how the 2:1 (octave) ratio is composed of the 3:2 (fifth) and 4:3 (fourth) ratios using number series.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of how the "double" ratio (2:1, the musical octave) is subdivided. The previous page ended by noting that the double is composed of the first two superparticular ratios: the sesquialtera (3:2) and the sesquitertia (4:3).</meta>

...the ternary <note>original: "ternarium"; the number three</note> and the sesquiseptima <note>original: "sesquiseptimam"; the ratio 8:7</note>. Here it is clear that, besides the **sesquialtera** <term>3:2 ratio; the musical perfect fifth</term> and **sesquitertia** <term>4:3 ratio; the musical perfect fourth</term>, a **double** ratio <term>2:1 ratio; the musical octave</term> can also intercept other **superparticular** relationships <term>ratios of the form (n+1):n</term>. Furthermore, it can also include **superpartient** proportions <term>superpartiens: a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller plus more than one part of it, such as 5:3 or 7:4</term>, as is clearly seen by any observer in the numbers from seven to four <note>7:4 ratio</note>, seven to five <note>7:5 ratio</note>, and eight to five <note>8:5 ratio</note>.

However, let it be known that it is these two primary superparticular proportions themselves that always divide the **double**. For the first double found in natural numbers—apart from the "root" of doubles <note>the 2:1 ratio expressed as the numbers 2 and 1</note> which, due to its simplicity, intercepts no natural number (namely, between the number two and unity)—is composed and divided without the intervention of any other term. 

Therefore, beyond the root of doubles, the first double consists of the numbers **2, 3, 4**. For the number **three** compared to **two** holds the **sesquialtera** ratio; the number **four** compared to **three** constitutes the **sesquitertia**. Likewise, the number **four** compared to **two** completes the **double** ratio. Indeed, no other natural number falls between two, three, and four.

And because the **sesquialtera** is greater than the **sesquitertia**, it exceeds the middle <note>original: "rectum" or "medium"; referring to the arithmetic midpoint</note> of the double proportion. The **sesquitertia**, however, does not fill a complete half. Nature brings it about that whenever something is cut so that it is not divided into equal parts, the amount by which the smaller part is less than the half is exactly the amount by which the larger, increased part exceeds the half. Therefore, by as much as the **sesquialtera** is greater than the half of the double, the **sesquitertia** is smaller than that same half of the double proportion. For this reason, if you were to propose two continuous **sesquialtera** proportions...

<summary>This page explains that while many ratios exist, the "double" (octave) is most naturally divided by the sesquialtera (3:2) and sesquitertia (4:3) using the number sequence 2:3:4. It also describes the mathematical symmetry where the larger ratio exceeds the midpoint of the octave by the same amount the smaller ratio falls short of it.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical and musical exploration of how ratios combine. It specifically addresses how the sesquialtera (3:2) and sesquitertia (4:3) ratios relate to the double (2:1, the octave) and the resulting "leftover" ratio known as the sesquioctave (9:8, the whole tone).</meta>

...that which will result from the extreme terms will not only constitute a **double** proportion <term>2:1 ratio; in music, the octave</term> but will exceed it further, as is evident here: **4, 6, 9**. The **six** <note>original: "senarius"</note> compared to the **four** <note>original: "quaternarium"</note> is a **sesquialtera** <term>3:2 ratio; the perfect fifth</term>. The **nine** <note>original: "novenarius"</note> compared to that same **six** offers another **sesquialtera**. Likewise, the **nine** compared to the **four** does not constitute a **double** but goes beyond it. For there is an intervening **eight** <note>original: "octonarius"</note> which shows the **double** proportion to the **four** itself; and if we compare the **nine** to the **eight**, a **sesquioctave** <term>9:8 ratio; the musical whole tone</term> proportion is created. Therefore, two **sesquialtera** proportions exceed the **double** by one **sesquioctave** proportion.

Since, indeed, the **sesquitertia** <term>4:3 ratio; the perfect fourth</term> proportion is smaller than half of a **double**, two **sesquitertias** therefore will not complete a **double**. This is proven in this way: let us establish two **sesquitertia** proportions as in these numbers: **9, 12, 16**. Thus, **twelve** compared to **nine** makes a **sesquitertia**. **Sixteen** also compared to **twelve** proves another **sesquitertia**. Yet, **sixteen** compared to **nine** does not complete a **double**. However, if **two** <note>original: "binarius"</note> is added—which is an eighth part of **sixteen**—then it will become a true **double** proportion <note>18:9 is a 2:1 ratio</note>, as in these numbers: **9, 12, 16, 18**. You see, therefore, that **eighteen** compared to **sixteen** makes a **sesquioctave** proportion, and the same **eighteen** joins in a **double** relationship to **nine**. Thus, two **sesquialtera** proportions exceed the **double** by one **sesquioctave**, while two **sesquitertias** are smaller than a **double** by that same **sesquioctave**. From this, the **sesquialtera** surpasses the **sesquitertia** by one **sesquioctave**, as is evident in these numbers: **6, 8, 9**. For **eight** compared to **six** creates a **sesquitertia**, while **nine** compared to **eight** [creates] a **sesquioctave**...

<meta>This page continues the technical demonstration of how musical intervals, expressed as mathematical ratios, are composed and divided. It specifically explores the mathematical impossibility of dividing a whole tone into two equal halves using whole numbers.</meta>

Likewise, **nine** and **six** join to form a **sesquialtera** <term>3:2 ratio; the musical perfect fifth</term>. Therefore, a **sesquitertia** <term>4:3 ratio; the perfect fourth</term> is smaller than a **sesquialtera** by exactly one **sesquioctave** <term>9:8 ratio; the musical whole tone</term>.

The **sesquioctave** proportion is naturally indivisible into two equal parts <note>The author is explaining that a musical "whole tone" cannot be split into two identical "half tones" using integers, a fundamental problem in Pythagorean tuning</note>. For the evidence of this, let us arrange the terms that constitute it. We shall bypass the basic **sesquioctave** root which is made from **nine** <note>original: "novenario"</note> and **eight** <note>original: "octonario"</note>, since no natural number falls between those terms. Instead, let us arrange them in this manner: **16, 17, 18**.

For here, **18** compared to **16** constitutes a **sesquioctave** proportion <note>18:16 simplifies to 9:8</note>. But because the middle term—namely **17**—compared to **16** joins in a **sesquisextodecimal** proportion <term>17:16 ratio; a type of large semitone</term>, while **18** compared to that same middle term, **17**, makes a **sesquiseptimadecimal** proportion <term>18:17 ratio; a type of small semitone</term>, the **sesquioctave** itself can in no way acquire an equal division. For from the extreme and middle terms, unequal proportions arise.

Indeed, the **sesquisextodecimal** proportion <note>17:16</note> is larger than the **sesquiseptimadecimal** <note>18:17</note>, because, as was made clear in the preceding chapter, a larger proportion always results from smaller numbers, and a smaller proportion from larger numbers <note>The author means that as the numbers increase, the distance between them (when the difference is 1) becomes a smaller fraction of the whole</note>. The numbers **18** to **17** are indeed larger, while **17** to **16** are smaller.

If a **sesquialtera** <term>3:2 ratio</term> is added to a **double** proportion <term>2:1 ratio; the octave</term>, it becomes the second species of the multiple: namely, the **triple** <term>3:1 ratio; an octave plus a fifth</term>. This is what results from the extreme terms, as seen here: **3, 6, 9**. The **six** <note>original: "senarius"</note> compared to the **three** <note>original: "ternarium"</note> makes a **double**; the **nine** <note>original: "novenarius"</note> compared to the **six** makes a **sesquialtera**; likewise, the **nine** compared to the **three** completes a **triple** proportion. Hence, since the **sesquialtera** from...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical ratios. The sentence fragment from the previous page "f..." completes here as "excedit," clarifying that a sesquialtera (3:2) exceeds a sesquitertia (4:3) by exactly one sesquioctave (9:8).</meta>

If the **sesquitertia** itself were added to a **double** proportion <term>2:1 ratio; the musical octave</term>, it would not reach the perfection of a **triple** proportion <term>3:1 ratio; an octave plus a perfect fifth</term>, as shown here: **3, 6, 8**. 

For the **six** <note>original: "senarius"</note> compared to the **three** <note>original: "ternarius"</note> creates a **double** proportion. However, the **eight** <note>original: "octonarius"</note> compared to the **six** makes a **sesquitertia**. That same **eight** does not complete a **triple** proportion when compared back to the **three**. For the **nine** <note>original: "novenarius"</note> has added a **sesquioctave** to the **eight**, and that same **nine** completes a **triple** comparison to the **three**. 

But if, instead of adding the **nine**, a **sesquialtera** <term>3:2 ratio; the perfect fifth</term> is added, immediately the third species of the multiple <note>In Boethian mathematics, "multiple" is a class of ratios like 2:1, 3:1, 4:1</note>—namely the **quadruple** proportion <term>4:1 ratio; two octaves</term>—is found, as appears in these numbers: **3, 6, 8, 12**. 

For you see that the **twelve** <note>original: "duodenarium"</note> joined over the **eight** corresponds to a **sesquialtera** proportion; that same **twelve** retains a **double** proportion to the **six**, and the same **twelve** also retains a **quadruple** proportion to the **three**. 

But if you were to add a **sesquitertia** to the **triple** proportion which had previously been produced from a **double** and a **sesquialtera**—as here: **3, 6, 9**—you will note that the same **quadruple** is immediately formed, as is evident here: **3, 6, 9, 12**. 

For the **twelve** proves to be a **sesquitertia** to the **nine**, and you perceive that the same **twelve** completes a **quadruple** proportion to the **three**. Concerning all these things, let a figure of this kind be placed below:

<note>This diagram represents the "Perfect System" of Pythagorean tuning. The numbers correspond to string lengths or pipe proportions. By comparing these numbers, we find every consonant interval: for example, the ratio between 12 and 6 is 2:1 (an octave), while 12 to 8 is 3:2 (a fifth).</note>

| Proportion Name | Numerical Ratio | Musical Interval |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Double** <note>original: "dupla"</note> | 2:1 (e.g., 2 to 1, 4 to 2, 8 to 4) | **Octave** <note>original: "diapason"</note> |
| **Triple** <note>original: "tripla"</note> | 3:1 (e.g., 3 to 1, 9 to 3) | **Twelfth** <note>original: "octave plus a fifth"</note> |
| **Quadruple** <note>original: "quadrupla"</note> | 4:1 (e.g., 12 to 3) | **Double Octave** <note>original: "bis diapason"</note> |
| **Sesquialtera** | 3:2 (e.g., 3 to 2, 6 to 4, 12 to 8) | **Perfect Fifth** <note>original: "diapente"</note> |
| **Sesquitertia** | 4:3 (e.g., 4 to 3, 8 to 6, 12 to 9) | **Perfect Fourth** <note>original: "diatessaron"</note> |
| **Sesquioctave** | 9:8 (e.g., 9 to 8) | **Whole Tone** <note>original: "tonus"</note> |

However, the **eight** <note>original: "octonarius"</note> compared to the **six** makes a **sesquitertia**. <meta>This page continues the technical discussion of ratios (proportions) and their mathematical properties, likely within a treatise on music theory or arithmetic (such as Boethius’s De Institutione Arithmetica). It describes how different classes of ratios interact when combined.</meta>

Therefore, the diligent researcher will seek out the logic of the aforementioned ratios for themselves in the figure. This also must be noted from the preceding points: if a <term>multiple ratio</term> <note>original: "multiplex pportio"; a ratio where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller, such as 2:1 or 3:1</note> is multiplied twice, then that which is born from that multiplication is called a multiple ratio. But if that which results from the multiplication itself is not a multiple ratio, then that which was multiplied was not a multiple ratio before it was multiplied.

Likewise, if a <term>superparticular ratio</term> <note>original: "supparticularis"; a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller once plus one aliquot part of it, such as 3:2 or 4:3</note> is multiplied by two, that which results from the multiplication itself will be neither a multiple ratio nor a superparticular ratio. Whence, if that which is created from such a multiplication is not a multiple nor a superparticular ratio, then that which was multiplied before this kind of multiplication was either a superparticular ratio or of another kind, but not a multiple.

Here it also became clear that the first two types of superparticular ratios—namely the <term>sesquialter</term> <note>original: "sexquialtera"; the ratio 3:2, which in music forms a perfect fifth</note> and the <term>sesquitertial</term> <note>original: "sexquitertia"; the ratio 4:3, which in music forms a perfect fourth</note>—produce the first multiple ratio, namely the <term>double</term> <note>original: "dupla"; the ratio 2:1, which forms an octave</note>. Likewise, the first superparticular ratio combined with the first multiple ratio completes the second multiple ratio, namely the <term>triple</term> <note>original: "tripla"; the ratio 3:1, an octave plus a fifth</note>. Furthermore, the second superparticular ratio (the sesquitertial) added to the second multiple ratio (which is the triple ratio) forms the third multiple ratio, namely the <term>quadruple</term> <note>original: "quadrupla"; the ratio 4:1, or two octaves</note>. Or conversely, the second multiple ratio combined with the second superparticular ratio reaches that same third multiple ratio. In the same order...

<meta>This page concludes the mathematical discussion of ratios from the third book and transitions into the musical application of these ratios in Book Four. A <term>quadruple ratio</term> <note>4:1</note> with a <term>sesquiquarth</term> <note>original: "sexquiquarta"; the ratio 5:4</note> added to it completes a <term>quintuple ratio</term> <note>5:1</note>. Or, a sesquiquarth of a quadruple constitutes a quintuple in the same way. And in this manner, by joining <term>superparticular ratios</term> <note>ratios where the larger number contains the smaller once plus one part, e.g., 3:2 or 4:3</note> with <term>multiple ratios</term>, multiple proportions are produced infinitely. 

Now, as we are about to treat musical <term>consonances</term> <note>musical intervals that sound harmonious to the ear</note>, it must be known that what is called a <term>double proportion</term> <note>2:1</note> in numbers is called the **diapason** consonance in sounds <note>the octave</note>; and that which in numbers is called <term>sesquialter</term> or <term>hemiolia</term> <note>3:2</note> is named the **diapente** consonance in sounds <note>the perfect fifth</note>. Whereas that which is <term>sesquitertial</term> or <term>epitrite</term> <note>4:3</note> in numbers is called the **diatessaron** consonance in sounds <note>the perfect fourth</note>. Moreover, that which is <term>sesquioctave</term> <note>9:8</note> in proportions becomes the **tone** <note>a whole step</note> in consonances. That which is <term>triple</term> <note>3:1</note> joins the **diapason** and **diapente** consonances in sounds <note>an octave plus a fifth</note>. Indeed, a quadruple proportion in numbers forms the **bisdiapason** in sounds <note>a double octave</note>. But we must treat these things more broadly in the fourth book which follows.

<meta>This page continues the transition from general arithmetic to the specific mathematical ratios used in music theory. It begins mid-sentence, following the previous page's introduction to how multiple and superparticular ratios form the basis of musical consonances.</meta>

...that the consonances of the **musical discipline** might agree among themselves. Indeed, we know that three **multiple** <note>Ratios where the larger number is a clean multiple of the smaller (e.g., 2:1, 3:1).</note> and three **superparticular** <note>Ratios where the larger number contains the smaller once, plus one part of the smaller (e.g., 3:2, 4:3).</note> ratios of those numbers have been clearly explained. 

For these are the **multiples**: namely, the **double**, **triple**, and **quadruple**. Whereas the **superparticulars** are the **hemiolic** (or <term>sesquialter</term> <note>The 3:2 ratio, which forms the musical fifth.</note>), the **epitrite** (or <term>sesquitertial</term> <note>The 4:3 ratio, which forms the musical fourth.</note>), and the **epogdoic** (or <term>sesquioctave</term> <note>The 9:8 ratio, which forms a whole tone.</note>). 

A **double number** is that which, when compared to a smaller one, contains that whole smaller one twice, and it is called a <term>double proportion</term> <note>2:1</note>, such as four to two. 

A **triple number**, however, is that which, when compared to a smaller one, contains that smaller one within itself three times, such as six to two. 

Moreover, a **quadruple** is that which, when compared to a smaller one, contains it within itself four times, such as eight to two; and it is called a <term>quadruple proportion</term> <note>4:1</note> or "double-double." 

A **hemiolic number** is that which, when compared to a smaller one, contains the whole thing itself once and additionally a half part of that smaller one, such as three to two. For it is called "hemiolic" from *hemi* <note>original: "emi"; Greek for "half"</note>, which is half, and *holon*, "whole," as if containing the whole smaller number and also a half part of it. And this is properly called a **sesquialter proportion**, from *sesqui* which is "a half more" and *altera* ("another"), because a half part of the other number, equal [to the half], is added to the whole number. 

An **epitrite number** is that which, when it is compared...

An **epogdoic number** is that which, when compared to a smaller one, contains it whole and additionally its **eighth part**, such as nine to eight; and it is called a <term>sesquioctave proportion</term> <note>The 9:8 ratio, which forms the "whole tone" in Pythagorean tuning.</note>. The <term>Diapente</term> <note>The interval of a perfect fifth.</note> harmony consists in a **sesquialter** proportion (3:2). 
The <term>Diatessaron</term> <note>The interval of a perfect fourth.</note> harmony in a **sesquitertial** proportion (4:3).

The <term>Bisdiapason</term> <note>The interval of two octaves (a fifteenth).</note> in a **quadruple** proportion (4:1).

Now, therefore, we commend what we propose to the reader’s faith, so that it may be judged that the diapason consists in a double, the diapente in a sesquialter, the diatessaron in a sesquitertial, the tone in a sesquioctave, the diapasondiapente in a triple, and the bisdiapason in a quadruple proportion. Since these are indeed the principles of the discipline of music, they are not to be proven by necessity but are to be <term>supposed</term> <note>Accepted as foundational axioms. ### On the nature and formation of harmonies from ratios. Chapter 2. <gloss>To be understood</gloss>

Therefore, since the <term>Diapason</term> harmony <note>The interval of an octave.</note> consists in a **double** ratio (2:1), it partakes in the nature of that ratio. So also, each harmony retains the nature of the ratio in which it consists. From this it follows that the <term>Diatessaron</term> <note>The interval of a perfect fourth.</note> contains a **sesquitertial** nature (4:3); the <term>Diapente</term> <note>The interval of a perfect fifth.</note> preserves the property of the **sesquialter** (3:2); the **Tone** that of the **sesquioctave** (9:8); the <term>Diapason-Diapente</term> <note>An octave plus a fifth, known today as a perfect twelfth.</note> of the **triple** (3:1); and the <term>Bisdiapason</term> <note>A double octave.</note> of the **quadruple** (4:1). <meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of musical theory, specifically focusing on how multiplying the base terms of the harmonic proportion (3, 4, and 6) generates the ratios for every major musical interval, including the whole tone, fourth, fifth, and octaves.</meta>

The number four <note>original: "quaternarius"</note> taken four times produces 16. If 18 is compared to 16, it fulfills a <term>sesquioctave</term> proportion <note>original: "sesquioctavam." A ratio where the larger number is 1 and 1/8 times the smaller (9:8). In Pythagorean tuning, this defines the whole tone.</note>, which produces a sounding **whole tone**. Likewise, if we multiply the smallest term—namely three <note>original: "ternarium"</note>—by itself, the result is 9. But the larger term, the six <note>original: "senarius"</note>, multiplied by itself produces 36. These, when 36 is compared to 9, result in a **quadruple** proportion, confirming the <term>bisdiapason</term> <note>The interval of a double octave (a 4:1 ratio).</note>.

And if we inspect these things more diligently, this will be the complete multiplication of either the terms or the differences among themselves. For if the smallest term [3] is multiplied by the middle [4], it becomes 12. Likewise, if we multiply that same smallest [3] by the largest [6], it becomes 18. If, however, the middle term [4] is increased by the magnitude of the largest [6], it will constitute 24. Again, if the smallest term [3] grows by its own value, it becomes 9. Likewise, the middle term [4] increasing by itself makes 16. Finally, the six [6], which is the largest, if it multiplies itself, it yields 36.

From these, they prove the <term>Diatessaron</term> harmony <note>The interval of a perfect fourth.</note> through the comparison of 18 to 24 in a <term>sesquitertial</term> proportion <note>A 4:3 ratio.</note>; in the same way, 12 to 9. They derive the <term>Diapente</term> <note>The interval of a perfect fifth.</note> from 18 to 12 and 24 to 16 in a <term>sesquialter</term> proportion <note>A 3:2 ratio.</note>. However, they maintain the <term>Diapason</term> <note>The interval of an octave.</note> from 36 to 18 and 18 to 9 in a **double** proportion [2:1]. They preserve the **octave-plus-fifth** <note>original: "Diapasondiapenten." An interval of a perfect twelfth.</note> from 36 to 12 in a **triple** proportion [3:1]. They form the <term>bisdiapason</term> [double octave] from 36 to 9 in a **quadruple** proportion [4:1]. The **whole tone**, indeed, is produced from the comparison of 18 to 16 in a **sesquioctave** ratio. All of these things are indeed arranged in order in this figure:

Therefore, it is understood from the aforementioned things that the <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"</note> and the <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"</note> form the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"</note> consonance, just as the <term>sesquitertia</term> <note>literally "one and a third," the ratio 4:3</note> proportion and the <term>sesquialtera</term> <note>literally "one and a half," the ratio 3:2</note> complete the <term>double</term> <note>the ratio 2:1</note> proportion. And from the fourth and a <term>tone</term> <note>original: "tono," the whole step or 9:8 ratio</note>, the fifth is constructed in the same way that a <term>sesquialtera</term> proportion is composed from a <term>sesquitertia</term> and a <term>sesquioctava</term> <note>the ratio 9:8</note>. In the same way, if the fourth is taken twice with the addition of a tone, an octave is produced, whereby two <term>sesquitertia</term> proportions with the addition of a <term>sesquioctava</term> perfect the double proportion.

It is likewise clear that the octave and the fourth, with the addition of a tone, relate to the formation of the <term>octave-plus-fifth</term> <note>original: "diapasondiapente," an interval of a twelfth</note> consonance, just as the double proportion and the <term>sesquitertia</term> proportion, with the addition of the <term>sesquioctava</term>, perfect the <term>triple</term> <note>the ratio 3:1</note> proportion. By the same rule, the <term>double octave</term> <note>original: "bisdiapason," the interval of a fifteenth</note> is created from the octave-plus-fifth with the addition of a fourth, whereby a <term>quadruple</term> <note>the ratio 4:1</note> ratio is formed from the triple and <term>sesquitertia</term> proportions. Thus, finally, the consonances relate to one another in their formation and composition just as proportions do in their increase and conjunction—the properties of which were more extensively treated at the end of the third <note>Likely referring to the third book of a foundational text like Boethius's *Fundamentals of Music*.</note>. But if a fourth is added to the octave consonance, it will not, according to the <term>Pythagoreans</term>, result in a consonance, because the order of <term>multiplicity</term> <note>ratios like 2:1 or 3:1 where one number is a simple multiple of the other</note> is not preserved, nor is the order of <term>superparticularity</term> <note>ratios like 3:2 or 4:3 where the larger number is exactly one unit greater than the smaller</note>. <summary>This page explains the mathematical foundations of musical intervals, demonstrating how basic ratios (like 4:3 and 3:2) combine to form complex consonances such as the octave and the twelfth. --- Page 124 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical discussion of musical intervals, specifically addressing why the combination of an octave and a fourth was considered dissonant in classical music theory. It then transitions into Chapter 4, which examines the division of the whole tone into unequal semitones.</meta>

nor in these proportions does the larger number contain the smaller several times simply, nor just once with one <term>aliquot part</term> <note>A part that divides the whole exactly, such as 1/2 or 1/3. Here, it refers to the classification of musical ratios.</note> of that smaller number, as has been declared many times. If a <term>tone</term> <note>original: "tonus"; the interval of a whole step, mathematically a 9:8 ratio.</note> is added to these, it becomes an <term>octave-plus-fifth</term> <note>original: "diapason diapente"; an interval of a twelfth.</note> consisting in a <term>triple ratio</term> <note>A 3:1 ratio.</note>, as has been shown. But since the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"</note> consists in a <term>double ratio</term> <note>A 2:1 ratio.</note>, whatever other consonance it receives, it always remains within the order of <term>multiplicity</term> <note>A category of ratios where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller (2:1, 3:1, 4:1).</note>. From this it follows that while the octave itself exists in a double ratio and the octave-plus-fifth in a triple ratio, the <term>octave-plus-fourth</term> <note>original: "diapason diatessaron"; an interval of an eleventh.</note> can in no way be held within the order of multiplicity. This is because between a double (2:1) and a triple (3:1) ratio, no other multiple ratio can naturally be intercepted. Therefore, the octave-plus-fourth is rendered <term>dissonant</term> <note>original: "inconsonans." In Pythagorean theory, for an interval to be harmonious, its ratio had to be "multiple" or "superparticular" (like 3:2). The 8:3 ratio of the 11th fit neither category.</note>.

<margin>Points to note</margin>
### On the nature of the tone and semitones. Chapter 4.

<margin>
A tone derived from the <term>sesquioctava</term> <note>The 9:8 ratio.</note> is divided into two unequal parts: into two unequal semitones, the major and the minor. They are not named from "half" <note>original: "semis"</note> because the division would not be made perfectly; for that minor semitone is less than half a tone, and the major semitone exceeds it.
</margin>
When we consider that a tone is derived from the <term>sesquioctava</term> proportion, it therefore shares the nature of that proportion. On that account, it must necessarily be divided into two unequal parts. For it contains two unequal semitones, one of which is called "minor" and the other "major." For they are not properly called semitones <note>The author is arguing that "semitone" should not be taken literally as "exactly half a tone" because, mathematically, a 9:8 ratio cannot be split into two equal rational ratios.</note>

<summary>This page explains why the interval of an eleventh (octave plus a fourth) is considered dissonant based on its mathematical ratio, and begins a new chapter explaining that a whole tone is composed of two unequal semitones rather than two equal halves.</summary>
<keywords>octave-plus-fourth, dissonance, multiple ratio, triple ratio, whole tone, major semitone, minor semitone, sesquioctava, Pythagorean tuning</keywords>

--- Page 125 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical intervals from the previous page, specifically focusing on the division of the whole tone. It explains that a "semitone" is not a perfect mathematical half of a tone, but rather a name for the unequal parts that remain when tones are subtracted from a fourth.</meta>

...as if holding a half-tone. But because they do not reach the measure of a full <term>tone</term> <note>original: "tonus"; the 9:8 ratio interval of a whole step.</note>, we do not accept them as such; for even in grammar, we do not consider a "semivowel" among the letters to be exactly half of a vowel. 

The <term>minor semitone</term> <note>original: "semitonium minus"; the smaller of the two intervals that make up a whole tone in Pythagorean tuning.</note> does not reach a true half of a tone. The <term>major semitone</term> <note>original: "maius uero semitonium"</note>, however, exceeds the half of a tone by that same small portion by which the minor semitone is exceeded by that same half-tone. 

<insert><image-desc>A hand-drawn pen box encloses the following text section, with a decorative bracket on the right margin.</image-desc>And indeed, the followers of <term>Pythagoras</term> <note>A 6th-century BCE philosopher whose theories linked musical harmony to mathematical ratios.</note> called the minor semitone a <term>diesis</term> <note>original: "diesim"; in this context, it refers to the small Pythagorean semitone (256:243).</note>. <term>Plato</term>, however, called it a <term>limma</term> <note>original: "limam"; the Greek word for "remnant" or "leftover."</note>. Later writers decreed it should be called a "semitone," naming half of it a *diesis*, and established half of that diesis as a <term>comma</term> <note>original: "coma"; the tiny mathematical difference between two nearly identical intervals.</note>; and this is the small part by which the major semitone exceeds the minor. The <term>apotome</term> <note>original: "apothome"; the Pythagorean major semitone (2187:2048).</note> is indeed called the major semitone by everyone.</insert> 

Therefore, it is the minor semitone that properly holds a place in musical consonances. For when we subtract two whole tones from the consonance of a <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"</note>, that which remains is the minor semitone itself. <margin><image-desc>A marginal mark consisting of a horizontal line pointing to the start of the paragraph, terminating in a crescent-shaped flourish.</image-desc></margin>Likewise, if we subtract two <term>sesquioctave ratios</term> <note>original: "sexquioctauas"; the 9:8 ratio representing a whole tone.</note> from a <term>sesquitertial ratio</term> <note>original: "sexquitertia"; the 4:3 ratio representing a perfect fourth.</note>, that which remains will constitute a minor semitone. 

Let us therefore establish two tones, or two sesquioctave ratios, written in a continuous arrangement. However, we cannot have two continuous sesquioctave ratios unless a <term>multiple</term> <note>A common base number.</note> is found from which they can be derived. Let the first number be unity, and its first eight-fold multiple be eight.

For to this [number], only one <term>sesquioctave</term> <note>original: "sesquioctauus"; a ratio of 9:8, which defines the interval of a whole tone.</note> can be compared, as was discussed more extensively in the seventh [chapter] of the third [book] <note>Likely referring to Boethius’s *Fundamentals of Music*, Book III, Chapter 7, a standard medieval music theory text.</note>. Now it remains for us to apply to the number 64 its <term>sesquitertial</term> <note>original: "sesquitertius"; a ratio of 4:3, which defines the musical interval of a perfect fourth.</note> [ratio]; but since 64 does not have a third part <note>A whole number divisible by three is needed to calculate a 4:3 ratio.</note>, if all these numbers are multiplied by three, a third part will soon be available to the smallest number, and all will remain in the same proportion as they were before they were multiplied by three.

Therefore, the number 64 taken three times constitutes 192. Its third part—namely 64—added to itself completes 256; for this will be the sesquitertial proportion holding the <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"; the Greek term for a perfect fourth.</note> consonance, from 192 to 256. Let us now arrange the two sesquioctave proportions starting from 192, containing themselves in two numbers in the proper order. For the number 72 taken three times produces the number 216. The number 81 indeed, taken three times, completes the number 243, which [lie] between the two aforementioned numbers...

## The Sesquitertial Proportion.

In these numbers, the first [192] to the last [256] makes a sesquitertial proportion, producing the <term>diatessaron</term> <note>A perfect fourth</note> consonance. Furthermore, the first [number] compared to the second [216] forms a <term>sesquioctave</term> <note>A ratio of 9:8</note> and a <term>tone</term> <note>A musical whole step</note>. Likewise, the second compared to the third [243] makes another sesquioctave and another tone. There remains now the space that falls between 243 and 256, in which the first form of the <term>minor semitone</term> <note>original: "semitonii minoris"; in Pythagorean tuning, this small interval is called the "limma."</note> consists of these smallest [integer] values.

Because we have truly stated before that this semitone does not fill a proper half of a tone, let us now come to proving that point. For since the <term>difference</term> <note>original: "differentia"</note> between 243 and 256 stands at only 13 units, if that same difference is taken eighteen times <note>This is a mathematical proof common in medieval music theory; if the interval were a true half of a 9:8 tone, the math would align differently. 13 multiplied by 18 is 234.</note>, it will not return the whole smaller [number], but rather will fall short of the total sum of that smaller one. For it will become 234, which is less than 243.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical proof from page 53, which investigated the "difference" of 13 units between the numbers 243 and 256. The previous page established that multiplying 13 by 18 yields 234, which is less than the smaller number (243).</meta>

If, however, you multiply that same difference <note>The value of 13.</note> nineteen times, it will exceed the smaller number itself; for it will become 247. Therefore, that difference contains less than an eighteenth part of the smaller number, but more than a nineteenth part. From this, it follows that every <term>semitone</term> <note>A musical half-step</note>—if it is to preserve a complete half of a tone—must be placed between a <term>sesquisextadecima</term> <note>A ratio of 17:16</note> and a <term>sesquidecimeseptima</term> <note>A ratio of 18:17</note> proportion, which is impossible.

For as we said at the end of the third [section], the <term>sesquioctave</term> <note>The 9:8 ratio that defines a whole tone</note> proportion is divided into two unequal parts. Just as between the numbers 18 and 16, the number 17 naturally falls between them, the number 17 does not retain the same proportion to the number 16 as the number 18 makes to 17. And since 17 to 16, because they are smaller numbers, hold a larger proportion than 18 to 17 <note>In ratio calculations, smaller integer gaps (like 17:16) represent larger musical intervals than larger integer gaps (like 18:17) when the difference is 1.</note>, that sesquioctave [9:8] cannot be divided into two equal parts. If that were to happen, it would be necessary to join some small part of the sesquisextadecima proportion to the sesquidecimeseptima proportion (which is smaller) so that they would become equal, which can in no way be done.

This is because the sesquisextadecima proportion has no smaller part within itself than the [difference of the] sesquisextadecima, which is the <term>unity</term> <note>The number 1, the basic building block of integer mathematics</note>, which is entirely indivisible. It is clear, therefore, from these things that a tone can by no means be divided into two equal parts. Likewise, since the <term>minor semitone</term> <note>original: "semitoniũ minus"</note> does not reach a true half of a tone, if it is doubled, it will not complete a whole tone. <meta>This page continues the mathematical and musical proof from the previous page regarding the division of the whole tone. It moves from establishing the general principles of ratios to the specific definitions of the "minor semitone" (limma) and the "major semitone" (apothome).</meta>

[...semitones] do not make a whole tone. For whatever is the half of any thing, if that [half] is doubled, it completes that whole of which it is said to be the half. If, however, that doubled part cannot fill the whole, it follows that before the doubling, that part was smaller than the half-part of the whole itself. But if the doubled part should exceed its whole, then before it was doubled, it was greater than the half-part. 

Therefore, that which is truly called a <term>semitone</term> <note>From the Latin *semitonium*. While the name implies "half a tone," the author is proving it is mathematically smaller than a true 1/2 division of the 9:8 ratio.</note> in musical harmonies is less than a true half of a tone. Moreover, that which is left over from the whole tone is greater than the half-part of the tone itself. For this, the Greeks have used the name <term>apothome</term> <note>original: *apothomē*, meaning "a cutting off" or "remainder." This refers to the larger interval that remains when a minor semitone is subtracted from a whole tone.</note>; by us, however, it can be called a <term>decisio</term> <note>Latin: "a cutting away" or "diminution."</note>. 

Thus, by as much as the semitone is less than a true half of a tone, by that much the <term>apothome</term> exceeds the complete half of the tone. And since it has been demonstrated that the semitone primarily stands between [the numbers] 256 and 243 <note>The ratio 256:243 is the Pythagorean "limma," the mathematical value of a minor semitone.</note>, it must now be revealed in which smallest numbers the <term>apothome</term> consists. 

For if 243 could receive an eighth part so that its <term>sesquioctave</term> <note>A ratio of 9:8. To find the sesquioctave of a number, you must be able to divide it by 8 and add that part back to the whole.</note> could be compared to it, then the relationship of 256 compared to the <term>sesquioctave</term> of that smaller number would prove the <term>apothome</term> by necessary reasoning. But since the number 243 itself cannot have an eighth part <note>243 is an odd number and not divisible by 8.</note>, let both numbers be multiplied eight times...

<summary>The author explains that a "semitone" (256:243) is mathematically less than half a tone, while the remaining portion, called the apothome, is greater than half. He begins a new calculation to find the exact numerical value of the apothome by multiplying the base numbers by eight.</summary>

This is the larger remainder of a tone after a minor semitone is removed.</note>. And in these very numbers, the proportion of the apothome consists. Since the space of the minor semitone is contained in the smallest numbers 256 and 243, it follows that 1,944 and 2,048 are in that same proportion as 243 and 256, because 243 and 256 have both been multiplied by eight. For if one [and the same] number multiplies any two numbers, the numbers that arise from that multiplication will remain in the same proportion as those original numbers were.

...which the multiplier number has multiplied.

But Philolaus the Pythagorean handed down the division of the <term>tone</term><note>The whole tone, defined by the ratio 9:8.</note> in another way, establishing the first odd number—namely, three—along with its square, which is called nine. He also multiplies this by three, and they become 27. This number 27, when compared to 24, holds a tone in a <term>sesquioctave proportion</term><note>The ratio 9:8. Since 27 is 9x3 and 24 is 8x3, the ratio 27:24 is equal to 9:8.</note>. For three is their difference; when counted eight times, it makes 24, but counted nine times, it fills 27. For he divides the tone into a minor semitone and an <term>apothome</term><note>The "major semitone." Pythagorean tuning results in two unequal semitones; the apothome is the larger of the two.</note>.

Indeed, Philolaus said that the difference between these two is a <term>comma</term><note>A very small musical interval representing the difference between a major and minor semitone.</note>, asserting that the minor semitone consists of 13 units. This is because 13 is the difference seen between 243 and 256 <note>original: "ccliii" and "cclvi". The OCR likely misread 243 (ccxliii) as 253 (ccliii), as 256 minus 243 equals the 13 units mentioned.</note>. And because this same number 13 consists of a nine, a three, and the unit <note>the number 1</note> which holds the place of origin for all numbers.

<margin>
Three is the first odd number in the natural sequence of numbers.
Nine is the first odd square number.
</margin>

For this reason, when he established the <term>minor semitone</term> as the number 13, he judged that the remaining part of the number 27—which is 14—must contain the apothome. But since 13 and 14 have a difference of one, he thought that the "unit" should be held in the place of the comma. For he places the whole tone in 27 units, because between 216 and 243—which are distant from each other by a tone—the number 27 itself is the difference. From these...

and a semitone, and the semitone differs from the
apothome by a comma, the apothome is nothing other
than a minor semitone plus a comma. Whence,
if two minor semitones are subtracted from a tone,
a comma is left over. This is also shown by the following 
proof: if the <term>octave</term><note>original: "diapason"</note> is contained by five tones

two minor semitones are left;
but when five tones are removed from six tones, one 
tone is left, which exceeds those two semitones by a 
comma. If, however, the comma is added back to those two semitones, 
the tone is completed. Therefore, it is established that a tone consists 
of two semitones and one comma. 

For <term>Philolaus</term><note>A 5th-century BCE Pythagorean philosopher who was among the first to describe musical scales mathematically.</note> himself called the minor semitone a <term>diesis</term>. 
But later writers, as <term>Boethius</term><note>The 6th-century Roman scholar Boethius, whose "Fundamentals of Music" became the standard textbook for the Middle Ages.</note> asserts, called the 
diesis the half of a minor semitone. 
Therefore, a minor semitone contains two dieses within itself, 
whereas the apothome (or major semitone) contains two 
dieses and one comma. From this it follows that a tone 
is completed by four dieses and a comma. For the

For a <term>comma</term><note>The smallest interval recognized in Pythagorean tuning, representing the difference between twelve perfect fifths and seven octaves.</note>, as it pleases some, is half of a <term>diesis</term><note>In this context, the "diesis" refers to the Pythagorean minor semitone (limma), though the author notes that definitions vary.</note>. But our <term>Boethius</term><note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose work *De institutione musica* transmitted Greek music theory to the Middle Ages.</note> reasonably shows in the third book of his *Music* that the comma itself exceeds half of a diesis. And indeed, <term>Philolaus</term><note>A Pythagorean philosopher from the 5th century BCE who was the first to document the mathematical ratios of the scale.</note> himself measured even smaller particles. For some divide the comma into two <term>schismas</term>, and one schisma into two <term>dyachismata</term>. However, I do not care to reveal the nature of the diesis, the comma, and of other minute particles in numbers because of their difficulty, especially since they are hardly necessary for an easy consideration of the consonances. Instead, it suffices to have shown the nature of <term>semitones</term>, which are indeed the smallest parts established within the <term>consonances</term><note>Intervals that sound "stable" or "pleasing" to the ear, such as the fifth or fourth.</note> and are the first to occur in the division of <term>tones</term>, as appears more clearly in the division of the <term>monochord</term><note>A scientific instrument with a single string and a movable bridge, used to demonstrate the relationship between mathematical ratios and musical pitches.</note>.

A musical interval encompassing four notes of the scale, defined by the 4:3 ratio.</note> <term>consonance</term><note>Original Latin: *simphonia*. This number, compared to that same number 256, will hold a sesquioctave proportion and a tone. This very number 288, when compared to 192, proves a sesquialter proportion, containing the consonance of a fifth. Therefore, a fifth is made of a fourth and a tone—that is, from three tones and a semitone.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical proof from the previous page, detailing how the combination of musical intervals (the fourth and the fifth) creates the octave. It specifically addresses a long-standing debate in music theory regarding whether an octave contains exactly six whole tones.</meta>

It follows that from the **fourth** and **fifth** joined together, the **octave** consonance is produced. And since it has been demonstrated that the **fourth** <note>Original Latin: *diatessaron*</note> consists of two tones and a semitone, while the **fifth** <note>Original Latin: *diapente*</note> consists of three tones and a semitone, the **octave** <note>Original Latin: *diapason*</note> which is formed from them will necessarily consist of five tones and two semitones. 

However, since those two semitones do not complete a full whole tone (as was seen more extensively in the previous chapter), but rather exceed the middle of a tone, the **octave** consonance cannot in any way contain six whole tones. Instead, it falls short of six tones by one **comma** <term>comma</term><note>A tiny musical interval. In the Pythagorean system used here, six whole tones actually exceed an octave by a small amount; therefore, the perfect octave is "short" of six tones by this tiny mathematical remainder.</note>. If this comma were added to those two semitones, it would complete a full tone, and thus the octave would be made of six tones. Therefore, the octave does not consist of six tones, as **Aristoxenus** <note>A Greek philosopher who argued that musical intervals should be measured by the ear's perception rather than strictly by mathematical ratios.</note> believed. 

<margin>3</margin> **Macrobius** <note>A 5th-century Roman writer; his commentary on Cicero’s *Dream of Scipio* was a foundational text for medieval science and music theory.</note> reaches this same false conclusion in the second book of his *Commentary on the Dream of Scipio*. He states that the fifth consists of three tones and a semitone, and the fourth consists of two tones and a semitone, and that two semitones do not fill a whole tone. Yet, he then claims that the octave is composed of six tones; thus, he presents true premises but reaches a false conclusion. 

The "Lord Reconciler of Medicine" <note>Pietro d'Abano (c. 1257–1315), a famous Italian physician and philosopher known as the "Conciliator" after his famous book *Conciliator Differentiarum*.</note> was also deceived by this same error in his commentary on the 13th problem of **Aristotle’s** *Musical Problems*. He concluded that a **double octave** <note>Original Latin: *bisdiapason*</note> is completed by twelve tones, from which it would follow...

<summary>This page argues that a perfect octave consists of five tones and two semitones rather than six equal tones, correcting perceived errors in the works of Aristoxenus, Macrobius, and Pietro d'Abano.</summary>
<keywords>octave, diapason, fourth, diatessaron, fifth, diapente, comma, Aristoxenus, Macrobius, Pietro d'Abano, music theory, mathematical ratios</keywords>

...that the **octave** <note>Original Latin: *diapason*</note> has six tones. For the **double octave** <note>Original Latin: *bisdiapason*</note> is nothing other than a doubled octave. Likewise, twelve tones are nothing other than six tones doubled.

Therefore, since the **octave** consists in a **double ratio** <term>double ratio</term><note>The 2:1 mathematical ratio. In music theory, doubling the frequency of a note produces its octave.</note>, which is divided into two unequal parts—namely, into a **sesquialter ratio** <term>sesquialter</term><note>The 3:2 ratio, which defines the perfect fifth.</note> and a **sesquitertial ratio** <term>sesquitertial</term><note>The 4:3 ratio, which defines the perfect fourth.</note>—it is divided into two unequal parts: the **fifth** <note>Original Latin: *diapente*</note> and the **fourth** <note>Original Latin: *diatessaron*</note>. This was touched upon in the fifth chapter of the second book. We shall declare the nature of this more clearly in the division of the **monochord** <term>monochord</term><note>A historical scientific instrument consisting of a single string stretched over a soundbox with a movable bridge, used to demonstrate the relationship between mathematical ratios and musical intervals.</note>.

And indeed, if we add the consonance of the **fifth** to the **octave** consonance, a consonance named from both terms is produced, namely the **octave-plus-fifth** <note>Original Latin: *diapason-diapente*; in modern terms, this interval is a twelfth.</note>, consisting in a **triple ratio** <note>The 3:1 ratio.</note>, as has already become clear above. This is because the octave consists in a double ratio and the fifth in a sesquialter ratio, which when joined together complete a triple ratio, as appears more clearly in the second chapter.

But if the **fourth** <note>Original Latin: *diatessaron*</note> is added to the **octave-plus-fifth** consonance, the **double octave** <note>Original Latin: *bisdiapason*</note> consonance is soon produced, consisting in a **quadruple ratio** <note>The 4:1 ratio.</note>. For if we add a sesquitertial ratio to a triple ratio, it will produce a quadruple ratio, as appears in the aforementioned chapter and the last chapter of the third book.

For it is established that the **octave-plus-fifth** consists of eight tones and three **minor semitones** <note>In the Pythagorean tuning system used by the author, semitones are not all equal. A "minor semitones" (or limma) is slightly smaller than half a whole tone.</note>. The **double octave**, however, consists of ten tones and four minor semitones.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of musical intervals using ratios. ...as is clear to one looking carefully. If, however, a **sesquialter ratio** <term>sesquialter</term><note>The 3:2 ratio, which defines the musical fifth.</note> is added to a **quadruple ratio** <note>The 4:1 ratio, representing a double octave.</note>, it will soon become a **sextuple ratio** <note>The 6:1 ratio.</note>, and not without merit. For just as a **double ratio** <note>The 2:1 ratio (octave).</note> doubled makes a quadruple ratio, it is also necessary that a sextuple ratio be produced from a **triple ratio** <note>The 3:1 ratio (octave-plus-fifth).</note> doubled. 

Thus, therefore, when the **fifth** <note>Original Latin: *diapente*</note> is added to the consonance of the **double octave** <note>Original Latin: *bisdiapason*</note>, it will produce the consonance of the **double-octave-plus-fifth** <note>Original Latin: *bisdiapason diapentes*; in modern terms, a nineteenth.</note>. This interval has been least established by musicians in the order of consonances because of the very great distance of the extreme sounds from one another. 

And since between the quadruple and sextuple ratio one is naturally intercepted—namely, the quintuple—it must be seen whether the **sesquitertial ratio** <term>sesquitertial</term><note>The 4:3 ratio, which defines the musical fourth.</note> (which is smaller than the sesquialter), if joined to the quadruple ratio, can be contained in the order of **multiplicity** <term>multiple ratio</term><note>A ratio where the larger number is a precise whole-number multiple of the smaller (e.g., 2:1, 3:1, 4:1). Ancient music theory prioritized these "pure" ratios.</note>. 

For let the quadruple ratio be arranged in this way: **3** to **12**. To this let us add the sesquitertial, namely **16**. Let these all be ordered consequently: **3, 12, 16**. For the number twelve to three completes a quadruple ratio. Sixteen to twelve, however, completes a sesquitertial ratio. Likewise, 16 to 3 does not only make a quintuple ratio, but exceeds it by one unit, which is the third part of that 3 <note>16 divided by 3 is 5 with a remainder of 1. Because it is not a clean multiple, this indicates that adding a fourth to a double octave does not result in a "perfect" multiple ratio.</note>.

And because between that quintuple ratio <note>The 5:1 ratio.</note> and the sextuple ratio <note>The 6:1 ratio.</note>—which was produced earlier from the **quadruple** <note>4:1 ratio; the double octave.</note> and **sesquialter** <note>3:2 ratio; the fifth.</note>—no **multiple ratio** <term>multiple ratio</term><note>A ratio where the larger number is a precise multiple of the smaller (e.g., 2:1, 3:1, 4:1).</note> can naturally be intercepted; therefore, the **sesquitertial ratio** <term>sesquitertial</term><note>The 4:3 ratio, which defines the musical fourth.</note> is not added to the quadruple ratio in a way that stays within the multiple genus. Although this interval is seen to be formed from the ratio of 16 to 3—which is called a **quintuple-sesquitertial** <note>A ratio of 5 1/3 to 1. In Latin: *quincupla sexquitertia*.</note> in the **multiple superparticular** <term>multiple superparticular</term><note>A complex ratio category where the larger number contains the smaller number multiple times plus one fractional part (e.g., 16:3 contains 3 five times, plus 1/3 of 3).</note> genus—it is nonetheless proved not to consist in the multiple genus, but in the multiple superparticular. For in all quadruples, the order of multiplicity and superparticularity is preserved when that sesquitertial ratio is joined to it, as is demonstrated by these numbers: 6, 24, 32. 

Indeed, 24 compared to 6 is a **quadruple ratio** <note>4:1</note>; 32 compared to 24 is a **sesquitertial** <note>4:3</note>; likewise, 32 compared to 6 is a **quintuple-sesquitertial** <note>5 1/3 to 1</note>. In the same way, the numbers 12, 48, and 64 are compared to one another. 

Therefore, since joined superparticulars ought to form other multiples when combined with multiple ratios, in these cases such an order is not preserved—just as it is not preserved in the **octave-plus-fourth** <note>Original Latin: *diapason diatesaron*; the ratio of 8:3.</note>, as was already made clear in the fifth chapter of the second book. Therefore, the double-octave-plus-fourth is not received into the order of consonances. And indeed, musicians have also omitted the **double-octave-plus-fifth** <note>Original Latin: *bisdiapason diapente*.</note> because of the very great distance of the extreme sounds, as...
