<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical intervals and their mathematical ratios, specifically concluding the argument about the double-octave-plus-fourth and defining the boundaries of what constitutes a consonance in the Pythagorean tradition.</meta>

...was said about the distance. But what they handed down concerning the **octave-plus-fifth** <note>original: *diapasondiapente*; an interval of a twelfth</note> and the **octave-plus-fourth** <note>original: *diapasondiatessaron*; an interval of an eleventh</note>, they agreed should also be understood about these: because the **double octave** <note>original: *bisdiapason*</note> is nothing other than a doubled **octave** <note>original: *diapason*</note>, and a **quadruple ratio** <note>4:1</note> is nothing other than a doubled **double ratio** <note>2:1</note>. But this alone is the difference between the **octave-plus-fourth** and the **double-octave-plus-fourth**: that the **octave-plus-fourth** consists in a **multiple superpartient** <term>multiple superpartient</term><note>A complex ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number multiple times plus more than one fractional part (e.g., 8:3).</note> [ratio]. The **double-octave-plus-fourth**, however, [consists] in a **multiple superparticular** <term>multiple superparticular</term><note>A ratio where the larger number contains the smaller multiple times plus exactly one more part (e.g., 16:3).</note> [ratio], as anyone can gather from the aforementioned reasons.

Therefore, there are only three consonances in the **multiple** genus <term>multiple genus</term><note>Ratios where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller, like 2:1 or 3:1.</note> which musicians discuss: namely, the **octave**, the **octave-plus-fifth**, and the **double octave**. And [there are] three in the **superparticular** genus <term>superparticular genus</term><note>Ratios where the larger number contains the smaller once plus one part, like 3:2 or 4:3.</note>: namely, the **fourth** <note>original: *diatessaron*</note>, the **fifth** <note>original: *diapente*</note>, and the **tone** <note>original: *tonus*</note>. Although the **tone** is not itself a consonance <note>A "consonance" was traditionally defined as a pleasant blending of two notes; a tone was considered a "building block" rather than a harmony in itself.</note>, but a member of consonances, nevertheless because it consists in the **sesquioctave ratio** <term>sesquioctave</term><note>The 9:8 ratio, which defines the distance of a whole step.</note> and is most aptly applied to the composition of consonances, we count it among the consonances themselves. 

Thus, the sounds of the **double octave** consonance are furthest apart at their extremes, since they depart from each other by a quadruple dimension of interval. And indeed, musicians have established this as the greatest [consonance]. They prove the **fourth** to be the smallest of all the harmonies, because the higher sound exceeds the lower by a third part of that lower sound itself <note>Referring to the 4:3 ratio.</note>. This, therefore, is the limit of the harmonies: that they cannot be extended beyond the quadruple [ratio], nor compressed within the third part.

# On the order of consonances. Chapter 5.

<margin>
easy to understand
</margin>

<margin>
The first harmony is the **octave** <note>original: *diapason*</note> because it is better known than the others and is arranged in a **double ratio** <note>2:1 ratio</note>. The **octave-plus-fifth** <note>original: *diapason diapente*; an interval of a twelfth</note> is second because it is in a **triple ratio** <note>3:1 ratio</note>. The **double octave** <note>original: *bisdiapason*</note> is third because it is in a **quadruple ratio** <note>4:1 ratio</note>. **Multiple ratios** <term>multiple ratio</term><note>A ratio where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller, such as 2:1, 3:1, etc.</note> are prior to **superparticular** <term>superparticular ratio</term><note>A ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number once, plus one part of the smaller, such as 3:2 or 4:3.</note> ones. Next is the **fifth** <note>original: *diapente*</note> which arises from the **sesquialter** <note>3:2 ratio</note>, and then the **fourth** <note>original: *diatessaron*</note> from the **sesquitertian** <note>4:3 ratio</note>.
</margin>

Now let us express the order of those consonances according to the
Pythagoreans <note>Followers of Pythagoras, who believed that the universe and music were governed by mathematical proportions.</note>. For that consonance is to be established
first in the order whose property of sweetness an open
sense perceives. For as each thing is through
itself, so it is also perceived by the sense in the
harmonic faculty, as Boethius <note>An influential 6th-century philosopher whose "De institutione musica" was the standard music theory textbook for a thousand years.</note> says. Therefore, if that
consonance which consists in a **double ratio** <note>2:1</note>
is more known than all other consonances, there is no
doubt that the **octave** <note>original: *diapason*</note> harmony is the
first of all. And it deservedly excels because it leads in our recognition.
The others, which maintain the order of multiplicity,
because **multiple ratios** not
undeservedly transcend **superparticular** relationships,
according to the Pythagoreans, they ought
to follow in a natural order. This results in the **octave** <note>original: *diapason*</note>
indeed being first in the double ratio.
The **octave-plus-fifth** <note>original: *diapason diapente*</note> is second in the
triple ratio. The **double octave** <note>original: *bisdiapason*</note>, moreover, in the
quadruple ratio is third. For this reason, let us
arrange the natural numbers starting from unity
up to four in this way:
1, 2, 3, 4. For the number two compared to unity
makes a **double ratio**, yielding the **octave** <note>original: *diapason*</note>
consonance, which is the greatest and most famous for its simplicity— <unclear>notissima</unclear>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical derivation of musical intervals from the previous page's discussion of Boethius. ...the **number three** <note>original: *ternarius*</note>, when calculated against the same unity, demonstrates a **triple proportion** <note>3:1 ratio</note>, sounding the **octave-plus-fifth** <note>original: *diapason diapente*; an interval of a twelfth</note>. Furthermore, the **number four** <note>original: *quaternarius*</note>, compared to that same unity, creates a **quadruple ratio** <note>4:1 ratio</note>, bringing forth the **double octave** <note>original: *bisdiapason*; an interval of two octaves</note> symphony. And indeed, because these **multiple ratios** <term>multiple ratio</term><note>A ratio where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller, such as 2:1, 3:1, or 4:1.</note> look back toward unity—which is the first—and follow it in a direct order, the **Pythagoreans** <note>Followers of Pythagoras who believed that numbers were the fundamental reality of the universe and music.</note> established that the multiples should precede, and the **superparticular ratios** <term>superparticular ratio</term><note>A ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number plus one part of that number, such as 3:2 or 4:3.</note> should follow.

There remains, therefore, the proportion of three to two,
<margin>the first superparticular</margin>
which is the **sesquialter** <note>3:2 ratio</note>, constituting the **fifth** <note>original: *diapente*</note>. This is indeed the first superparticular ratio occurring in the natural order of numbers. Furthermore, the number four compared to three leads to the **sesquitertian** <note>4:3 ratio</note>, proving the consonance of the **fourth** <note>original: *diatessaron*</note>, which is the second superparticular ratio. Likewise, the number four compared to two creates a **double ratio** <note>2:1 ratio</note>, just as the number two does in relation to unity. And because these first two superparticular ratios divide that double ratio, they placed the superparticulars—which lead to the fifth and the fourth—after the multiple relations. <insert>However, **Eubulides** and **Hippasus** <note>Hippasus of Metapontum was an early Pythagorean philosopher credited with discovering the mathematical ratios of musical intervals.</note> established a different order of consonances, saying that the increases in multiplicity correspond to the decreases in superparticularity, so that there cannot be a double without a half, nor a triple without a third part. They place the double first, namely the **octave** <note>original: *diapason*</note>, and second the sesquialter.</insert>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical intervals and their mathematical ratios. The OCR begins by completing the description of the sesquitertian ratio (4:3) as the "second" superparticular, before introducing the specific organizational system of Nicomachus of Gerasa, a Pythagorean mathematician.</meta>

<margin>
Another order of consonances according to Nicomachus
</margin>

...nd <note>completing "second" from the previous page</note> as if in a contrary division to the **double** <note>2:1 ratio</note>, which makes the **fifth** <note>original: *diapente*</note>. Third is the **triple** <note>3:1 ratio</note>, which is formed by the mixture of the double and the **sesquialter** <note>3:2 ratio</note>. Therefore, this is the **octave-plus-fifth** <note>original: *diapason diapente*</note>. Fourth is the **sesquitertian** <note>4:3 ratio</note>, as if in a contrary division of the triple, containing the **fourth** <note>original: *diatessaron*</note>. Fifth is the **quadruple** <note>4:1 ratio</note>, that is, the **double octave** <note>original: *bisdiapason*</note>, created from the joining of the triple and the sesquitertian ratios.

Nicomachus, however, established the order of consonances in a different manner. For just as unity <note>the number one</note> holds the principle of increase and decrease in proportions, so too he claims that the **octave** <note>original: *diapason*</note> is the principle of the remaining consonances, and that they can be established from it in a contrary division. For let a series of natural numbers be arranged, beginning from unity, in this way:

->**i. ii. iii. iiii. v.**<-

For the number two is the double of unity; its contrary part—which is its half—is unity itself. The number three is the triple of unity; its contrary part is the third. The number four is the quadruple of unity; its contrary part is the fourth. Thus, the principle of increasing and decreasing is found in simple unity. For this reason, the **octave** <note>original: *diapason*</note>, which is the double ratio, will be in the highest place as the principle. The others, however, will be in a contrary division, once the octave is set aside. For the **sesquialter** <term>sesquialter</term><note>The 3:2 ratio, which produces the musical interval of a fifth.</note> will be opposed to the triple division; the **sesquitertian** <term>sesquitertian</term><note>The 4:3 ratio, which produces the musical interval of a fourth.</note> on the other hand, will be opposed to the quadruple. This is proved by the same argument: for the same number is the first sesquialter which is also the first triple, if it is compared to unity.

It demonstrates how ratios like the fifth and fourth are geometrically and numerically related to the primary units of triple and quadruple proportions.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the mathematical proof for the hierarchical ordering of musical consonances. It completes the logic introduced by Nicomachus of Gerasa regarding how superparticular ratios (like the fifth and fourth) are "opposites" to multiple ratios (like the octave-plus-fifth and double octave), eventually concluding that the octave is the primary principle of harmony.</meta>

For **three** <note>the number 3, or *ternarius*</note> compared to **two** <note>the number 2, or *binarius*</note> is the first **sesquialter** <note>the 3:2 ratio, which creates the musical fifth</note>. Yet the same number, when compared to **unity** <note>the number 1</note>, is the first **triple** <note>the 3:1 ratio</note>. Furthermore, it is triple in relation to the difference it creates between itself and two <note>The difference between 3 and 2 is 1; 3 is the triple of 1</note>. Since, therefore, by this reasoning the sesquialter is opposed to the triple, the consonance of the **fifth** <note>original: *diapente*</note> is reasonably thought to be opposed to the **octave-plus-fifth** <note>original: *diapason diapente*</note> symphony.

Likewise, the **quadruple** <note>the 4:1 ratio</note> has a contrary division to the **sesquitertian** <note>the 4:3 ratio, which creates the musical fourth</note>. For that which is the first quadruple compared to unity—namely, **four**—is itself the first sesquitertian if it is compared to **three**. It is also made quadruple of the difference which it has between itself and three <note>The difference between 4 and 3 is 1; 4 is the quadruple of 1</note>. From this it follows that the sesquitertian proportion, which is the **fourth** <note>original: *diatessaron*</note>, is divided in a contrary manner to the quadruple proportion, which is the **double octave** <note>original: *bisdiapason*</note>.

The **double** <note>the 2:1 ratio</note>, however—because it has no opposite proportion according to this reasoning of **Nicomachus**, nor does any number smaller than the sesquialter exist with which **two** (which is the first double) could be joined in a **superparticular** <note>a ratio where the larger number is the smaller plus one unit, like 3:2 or 4:3</note> proportion—it exceeds such a form of contrary proportion. And for that reason, according to Nicomachus, the **octave** <note>original: *diapason*</note> holds the principle of the consonances. And although these consonances relate to one another in this way, he finally concludes with the **Pythagoreans** that it is better for all **multiple** consonances <note>ratios like 2:1, 3:1, 4:1</note> to precede, and for the **superparticulars** <note>ratios like 3:2 and 4:3</note> to follow.

<summary>This page explains Nicomachus's mathematical theory that ratios are "opposed" based on their differences, leading to the conclusion that the octave (2:1) is the fundamental principle of all music because it lacks a contrary partner.</summary>
<keywords>Nicomachus, Pythagoras, octave, diapason, fifth, diapente, fourth, diatessaron, sesquialter, sesquitertian, ratios, musical theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the theoretical proof of musical ratios, moving from the philosophical distinctions of Nicomachus of Gerasa to a specific mathematical demonstration of why the octave must belong to the "multiple" genus and why the fifth, fourth, and tone belong to the "superparticular" genus.</meta>

# In what genus the consonance of the **octave** <note>original: *diapason*</note> consists
## Chapter 6 <insert>easy to understand</insert>

Since it has been shown by more extensive explanations that the consonance of the **octave** <note>original: *diapason*</note> is established within the **multiple** <note>a mathematical category where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller, like 2:1 or 3:1</note> genus of inequality—specifically in the **double** <note>the 2:1 ratio</note> proportion—and that the **fifth** <note>original: *diapente*</note>, the **fourth** <note>original: *diatessaron*</note>, and the **tone** <note>original: *tonus*</note> are in the **superparticular** <note>a ratio where the numerator is the denominator plus one, such as 3/2 or 4/3</note> genus—namely in the **sesquialter** <note>the 3:2 ratio</note>, **sesquitertia** <note>the 4:3 ratio</note>, and **sesquioctave** <note>the 9:8 ratio</note>—it remains to prove by stronger arguments that the octave itself can in no way be placed in any other genus of inequality than the multiple. Furthermore, it must be proven that the fifth, the fourth, and the tone cannot consist in any genus other than the superparticular.

And first, this one thing is indeed worth knowing: that if we subtract from any superparticular ratio a smaller, contiguous superparticular ratio, then that which remains is less than half of the ratio that was subtracted. For example, in the case of the **sesquialter** <note>3:2</note> and the **sesquitertia** <note>4:3</note>: since the sesquialter is greater, let us take away the sesquitertia from the sesquialter. There remains only the **sesquioctave** <note>9:8</note> ratio. If this is doubled, it does not complete the consonance of the **fourth** <note>original: *diatessaron*</note>—namely the sesquitertia ratio—but rather it is less by one **minor semitone**. Because if the doubled sesquioctave does not complete the sesquitertia, then a single sesquioctave does not contain half of that sesquitertia.

<summary>This page introduces a mathematical proof to categorize musical intervals, arguing that the octave belongs to multiple ratios while the fifth, fourth, and tone belong to superparticular ratios. It specifically demonstrates that a whole tone (9:8) is less than half of a fourth (4:3).</summary>
<keywords>octave, fifth, fourth, whole tone, sesquialter, sesquitertia, sesquioctave, musical mathematics, ratio theory, semitone</keywords>

--- Page 146 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration of musical ratios. Having established that the octave, fifth, fourth, and tone belong to specific mathematical classes (multiple and superparticular), the author now uses a numerical example (the numbers 6, 8, and 9) to prove how these intervals relate to one another and why a tone is not exactly half of a fourth.</meta>

Therefore, if we subtract the **fourth** <note>original: *diatessaron*</note> from the **fifth** <note>original: *diapente*</note>, there remains a **tone** <note>original: *tonus*</note> which does not fill half of the **fourth** itself. This is because, as has often been mentioned, the **fourth** consists of two tones and a semitone <note>a "half-step" that is actually slightly less than half of a whole tone in this mathematical system</note>. For the sake of clarity, let us present the **sesquialter** <note>the 3:2 ratio, which forms the musical fifth</note> in a way that distinguishes the **sesquitertia** <note>the 4:3 ratio, which forms the fourth</note> and the **sesquioctave** <note>the 9:8 ratio, which forms the whole tone</note> in this manner:

->**Sesquialter** <note>3:2</note> proportion<-
->**Sesquitertia** <note>4:3</note> ratio &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **Sesquioctave** <note>9:8</note><-
->6 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 8 &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; 9<-
->**Fourth** <note>original: *diatessaron*</note> &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp; **Tone** <note>original: *tonus*</note><-
->Consonance of the **Fifth** <note>original: *diapente*</note><-

For you see that 9 compared to 6 has a **sesquialter** <note>3:2</note> relationship and forms the consonance of the **fifth** <note>original: *diapente*</note>. Eight compared to six, however, is a **sesquitertia** <note>4:3</note> and therefore a **fourth** <note>original: *diatessaron*</note>. Nine compared to eight is a **sesquioctave** <note>9:8</note>, and from this comes the **tone**. Therefore, if you take away the smaller number—namely six <note>original: *senarius*</note>—of which the **sesquitertia** is the middle term (that is, eight <note>original: *octonarius*</note>), there remain the numbers eight and nine. When these are compared to each other, they produce the **sesquioctave** and the **tone**. This [tone] does not fill exactly half of the consonance of the **fourth**. Furthermore, if we subtract the **sesquiquarta** <note>the 5:4 ratio, which in later music defines a major third</note> from the **sesquitertia** <note>4:3</note>, that which remains does not fill half of the **sesquiquarta**. This same principle holds true in the other ratios.

<summary>This page uses the numerical sequence 6-8-9 to demonstrate that a musical fifth (6:9) is composed of a fourth (6:8) and a whole tone (8:9). It mathematically proves that a tone is not a simple "half" of a larger interval, but a specific ratio (9:8) that leaves a remainder.</summary>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical proof regarding musical intervals. Having previously demonstrated the ratios of the fifth, fourth, and tone using the numbers 6, 8, and 9, the author now turns to the octave (diapason). He aims to prove that the octave belongs to the 'multiple' class of ratios (specifically 2:1) rather than the 'superparticular' class (ratios like 3:2 or 4:3) by analyzing its component parts.</meta>

Now, indeed, we must approach the consonance of the **octave** <term>diapason</term> <note>original: *diapason*; the interval of an eighth</note>. For if the **octave** <term>diapason</term> were not established in the multiple class of inequality <note>ratios where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller, like 2:1 or 3:1</note>, it would necessarily be placed in the **superparticular** <term>superparticulari</term> <note>ratios where the larger number contains the smaller number once plus one part of it, such as 3:2 or 4:3</note> class. Therefore, let us suppose the **octave** <term>diapason</term> exists in a superparticular relationship. Let us now take away from it the continuous consonance, namely the **fifth** <term>diapente</term> <note>original: *diapente*</note>. What remains is the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: *diatessaron*</note>, which, when doubled, exceeds the consonance of the **fifth** <term>diapente</term> by a **tone** <term>tonus</term> and a **semitone** <term>semitonium</term> <note>a "half-step"</note>.

But if the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> itself, in its simple form, did not fill half of the **fifth** <term>diapente</term>, then when doubled it would not reach the whole **fifth** <term>diapente</term>, and thus the consonance of the **octave** <term>diapason</term> could be placed in the superparticular class. But since the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> is more than half of the **fifth** <term>diapente</term>, if it is doubled, it exceeds the **fifth** <term>diapente</term>. For since the **fifth** <term>diapente</term> consists of three **tones** <term>tonus</term> and a **semitone** <term>semitonium</term>, while the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> is formed of two **tones** <term>tonus</term> and a **semitone** <term>semitonium</term>; if that **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> is doubled, there will be four **tones** <term>tonus</term> and two **semitones** <term>semitonium</term>. 

And indeed, the **fifth** <term>diapente</term> is exceeded by those four **tones** <term>tonus</term> and two **semitones** <term>semitonium</term> by one **tone** <term>tonus</term> and one **semitone** <term>semitonium</term>. Therefore, it cannot happen that the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term>, when taken twice, does not reach the consonance of the **fifth** <term>diapente</term>. For this reason, the consonance of the **octave** <term>diapason</term> can by no means be included in the superparticular class.

<summary>The author uses a logical proof to show that an octave (diapason) cannot be a superparticular ratio by demonstrating that its internal components—the fifth and the fourth—do not fit the mathematical requirements of that class. He specifically points out that a fourth is more than half of a fifth, which prevents the octave from being categorized alongside intervals like the fifth (3:2) or fourth (4:3).</summary>

# In which class the **fourth** and **fifth**
# and the **tone** consist. Chapter 7. <gloss>easy to understand</gloss>

¶ Although it has been made clear enough from the preceding pages that the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: *diatessaron*; the interval of a fourth, mathematically 4:3</note> and the **fifth** <term>diapente</term> <note>original: *diapente*; the interval of a fifth, mathematically 3:2</note> as well as the **tone** <term>tonus</term> <note>original: *tonus*; a whole step, mathematically 9:8</note> consist in the **superparticular** <term>superparticulari</term> class <note>original: *superparticulari*; a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller number plus exactly one part of it, such as 3/2 or 4/3</note>, this fact still remains to be proven with clearer arguments. And first, indeed, we will presuppose this true conclusion: that if from any **superparticular** ratio we take away the smaller **superparticular** ratio that is continuous with it, that which remains is less than half of the ratio that was withdrawn, as was declared in the preceding chapter. Therefore, if anyone should deny that these consonances belong to the **superparticular** class, they would have to admit they belong to the **multiple** <term>multiplici</term> class <note>original: *multiplici*; a ratio where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller, like 2:1 or 3:1</note>. For any observer will be able to gather for himself from the previous chapters why they cannot be placed in the **superpartient** <term>superpartienti</term> class <note>original: *superpartienti*; a ratio where the larger number contains the smaller plus more than one part, like 5:3</note> or in other composite classes.

Let us therefore presuppose—even though it is impossible—that they are to be established in the **multiple** class. Since the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> is a smaller consonance and the **fifth** <term>diapente</term> is a larger one, let the **fourth** be in a **double** <term>dupla</term> ratio <note>2:1</note> and the **fifth** in a **triple** <term>tripla</term> ratio <note>3:1</note>. It is indeed likely that if the **fourth** is established in the **double** ratio, the **fifth**—which is continuous to it—is in the ratio which is continuous to the **double**, namely <note>original: *f.*; abbreviation for *scilicet*</note> the **triple**.

The **tone** <term>tonus</term> <note>original: *tonus*; a whole step</note>, however, which in musical relations follows the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term>, since it is indeed smaller, must be placed in a ratio that is smaller than the **double** <term>dupla</term> <note>original: *dupla*; the 2:1 ratio of an octave</note>. But such a ratio is not found in the **multiple** <term>multiplici</term> class. For the **double** ratio is the first and the smallest of all **multiples**. It is necessary, therefore, that the **tone** itself be placed in the **superparticular** <term>superparticulari</term> class.

Let the **tone**, therefore, be in the first **superparticular** ratio, that is, in the **sesquialtera** <term>sexqualtera</term> <note>original: *sexqualtera*; the 3:2 ratio, which we now know as the perfect fifth</note>. But since the remaining **tone** should not fulfill half of the **fourth** that was retracted from the **fifth**—as is clear through the previously established conclusion—two **sesquialtera** ratios now exceed the **double** ratio, as was more broadly explained in the last part of the third book. Therefore, the **fourth**, the **fifth**, and the **tone** cannot be established in any class other than the **superparticular**.

...the **tone** <term>tonus</term> <note>original: *tonus*; the interval of a whole step</note> in a **double** <term>dupla</term> [2:1] proportion, the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: *diatessaron*; the interval of a perfect fourth</note> indeed in a **triple** <term>tripla</term> [3:1], and the **fifth** <term>diapente</term> <note>original: *diapente*; the interval of a perfect fifth</note> in a **quadruple** <term>quadrupla</term> [4:1]. But since a **fifth** consists of a **fourth** and a **tone**, a **quadruple** proportion would, in this way, be made from a **double** and a **triple**—which indeed cannot happen. For a **double** and a **triple** joined together produce a **sextuple** <term>sexcupla</term> [6:1] proportion, as is clear in these numbers: 2, 4, 12. For the number 4 shows a **double** proportion to 2, while 12 shows a **triple** to 4. Likewise, 12 shows a **sextuple** proportion to 2.

Likewise, let us suppose the **fourth** is in a **triple** ratio and the **fifth** in a **quadruple**. If we now subtract the **triple** from the **quadruple**, that which remains becomes a **sesquitertia** <term>sexquitertia</term> <note>original: *sexquitertia*; a 4:3 ratio, where the larger number contains the smaller plus one-third of it</note>. Again, if we take away the **fourth** from the **fifth**, the **tone** remains, which according to this reasoning would consist of a **sesquitertia** proportion. But three **sesquitertia** proportions are less than one **triple**. Therefore, three **tones** would not fill one **fourth**, which is completely false; for three **tones** exceed a **fourth** by one **major semitone**, that is, an **apothome** <term>apothome</term> <note>original: *apothome*; in Pythagorean tuning, this is the "leftover" part of a tone after a minor semitone is removed; it is slightly larger than a standard semitone</note>. 

Thus, the **fourth** can in no way be placed in the **multiple** <term>multiplici</term> <note>The class of ratios where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller, such as 2:1, 3:1, etc.</note> class; for this reason, neither will the **fifth** hold a **multiple** proportion. Because if we place the **fifth** in the **multiple** class—since the **fourth** is smaller and adjacent to it—we could in no way place it [the fourth] in the first **multiple**, that is, in the **double** proportion.

...since there was then no place or ratio smaller than the **double** <term>dupla</term> [2:1] proportion to which the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: *diatessaron*; the interval of a perfect fourth</note> could be conveniently adapted. In that case, the **fourth**, which is smaller than the **fifth**, could not be placed in any multiple ratio at all, because the **double** proportion is the smallest that occurs in the **multiple class**.

Indeed, it is widely established from the aforementioned reasons that the **fourth**, the **fifth**, and the **tone** cannot be placed in the **multiple class**; therefore, they rightly consist in the **superparticular class** <term>superparticulari</term> <note>A category of ratios where the larger number contains the smaller plus one unit (e.g., 3:2, 4:3, 5:4).</note>. This is because, as has been touched upon many times, all musical consonances are explained through the **multiple** and the **superparticular** classes. 

It must furthermore be noted that if the **fifth** and the **fourth** are placed in the **superparticular class**, they must necessarily have the greatest superparticular proportions. For among the superparticulars, the **sesquialtera** [3:2] and the **sesquitertia** [4:3] are the greatest...

<meta>This page continues the logical proof from the previous discussion regarding the mathematical ratios of musical intervals. **Third.** We prove this conclusion in the following way: 
for if the **fifth** <term>diapente</term> <note>original: *diapente*; the interval of a perfect fifth.</note> and the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: *diatessaron*; the interval of a perfect fourth.</note> consisted in smaller proportions of the **superparticular class** <term>generis superparticularis</term> <note>A category of ratios where the larger number is equal to the smaller number plus one part of it (e.g., 3:2, 4:3, 5:4). In music theory, these are considered the most consonant ratios after the simple multiples.</note> than the **sesquialtera** <term>sexquialtera</term> <note>A 3:2 ratio.</note> and the **sesquitertia** <term>sexquitertia</term> <note>A 4:3 ratio.</note>, 
there is no doubt that—just as any other two **superparticular** proportions joined together (other than the **sesquialtera** and **sesquitertia**) would not produce a single **double** <term>dupla</term> <note>A 2:1 ratio.</note> ratio— 
so the **fifth** and the **fourth** would not complete the **octave** <term>diapason</term> <note>original: *diapason*; the interval of an octave.</note>, 
which, as has been shown, consists in the **double** proportion. <margin>A decorative pen flourish or bracket-like "nota" mark appears here in the margin.</margin> Indeed, the **double** proportion is composed of the **sesquialtera** and the **sesquitertia**. 
The **octave** is therefore joined together from the **fourth** and the **fifth**. 
Wherefore, if the **octave** is established in the **double** proportion, it is necessary for the **fifth** and the **fourth** to be placed in the **sesquialtera** and the **sesquitertia**; 
for otherwise, when joined together, they could not complete the **octave** consonance, which consists in the **double** proportion. 
For any two successive proportions of the other **superparticulars** can in no way complete the **double** proportion, 
as is clear in these numbers: 3, 4, 5, 6. For the number **four** to the number **three** <term>quaternarius ad ternariū</term> makes a **sesquitertia** proportion, 
while the number **five** to the number **four** <term>quinarius ad quaternariū</term> makes a **sesquiquarta** <term>sexquiquarta</term> <note>A 5:4 ratio.</note>. 
But the **sesquitertia** and **sesquiquarta** joined together do not complete the **double**, 
because the number **five** to the number **three** is not a **double** ratio <note>In ratio multiplication, (4/3) × (5/4) = 5/3. This is approximately a major sixth, not a 2:1 octave.</note>.

...established in the **double** proportion <term>proportio dupla</term> <note>original: *proportione*; here referring back to the 2:1 ratio of the octave.</note>. Similarly, a **sesquiquarta** <term>sesquiquarta</term> <note>original: *sesquiquarta*; a ratio of 5:4.</note> joined with a **sesquiquinta** <term>sesquiquinta</term> <note>original: *sesquiquinta*; a ratio of 6:5.</note> falls short of the total sum of a **double** proportion. 

For the **five** <note>original: *quinarius*.</note> compared to the **four** <note>original: *quaternarium*.</note> holds a **sesquiquarta** ratio, while the **six** <note>original: *senarius*.</note> compared to the **five** holds a **sesquiquinta**. Yet that same **six** compared to the **four** does not fulfill the **double** proportion, but rather creates a **sesquialtera** <term>sesquialtera</term> <note>A 3:2 ratio (as 6:4 reduces to 3:2). In music, this is a perfect fifth.</note>.

The same judgment holds for all other continuous **superparticulars** <term>superparticularibus</term> <note>A class of ratios (n+1):n. The author is exploring what happens when you multiply these ratios together, such as (5/4 × 6/5) or (6/5 × 7/6).</note>. However, it should be noted that whenever any two continuous **superparticular** ratios are joined together and fall short of the perfection of the **double**, the **larger term** <note>The highest number in the calculation.</note> falls short of being a **double** of the **smallest term** by as many units as that pair is distant from the first two **superparticulars** that fail to complete the **double**. 

This is demonstrated by these numbers: **3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10**.

The **sesquialtera** <note>3:2 ratio.</note> is not subject to this rule, because when it is coupled with the **sesquitertia** <note>4:3 ratio.</note>—which is continuous to it—it produces the **double** proportion, as has been explained more extensively elsewhere.

<margin>
What the introduced kinds signify.
<note>original: *quid significet Idutis genua*; likely a scribal corruption of *inductis generibus*.</note>
</margin>

But this new sequence begins from the **sesquitertia** <note>4:3 ratio.</note>. Thus, the **sesquiquarta** and the **sesquitertia** joined together do not complete a **double**. Indeed, in the **larger term**, namely **five** <note>original: *.f. qnario*; the letter 'f' often stands for 'scilicet' or 'namely'.</note>, one unit is missing for it to maintain a **double** proportion with the **smaller term**, which is **three**. 

Truly, when the **sesquiquarta** and the **sesquiquinta** are joined together—which follow in the natural order of numbers—the **larger term**, namely **six**, lacks **two** <note>original: *binarius*.</note> units to be the **double** of the **four**, which is the **smaller term**.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical demonstration from page 66, concluding the observation that as ratios move higher in the number series, the resulting combined interval falls further and further short of the perfect 2:1 octave. ...they fall short of a full **double** <term>proportio dupla</term> <note>The 2:1 ratio, which creates the musical interval of an octave.</note> by that same unit. But when the **sesquiquarta** <term>sesquiquarta</term> <note>The ratio 5:4.</note> and **sesquiquinta** <term>sesquiquinta</term> <note>The ratio 6:5.</note> depart from the **sesquitertia** <term>sesquitertia</term> <note>The ratio 4:3.</note> at the outer limit by two <note>Referring to the sequence 4, 5, 6; the difference between 4 and 6 is two.</note>, they fall short of the double by that same two units <note>The double of 4 is 8; the number 6 is 2 units away from 8.</note>. And for that reason, the ratios of the **sesquitertia** and **sesquiquarta** are larger than those of the **sesquiquarta** and **sesquiquinta**, because among **superparticular** <term>superparticular</term> <note>A ratio where the numerator is the denominator plus one (e.g., 3:2, 4:3). In these ratios, the smaller the numbers used, the "larger" the musical interval produced.</note> ratios, a smaller ratio is always found in larger numbers, and a larger ratio in smaller numbers.

Now we must also return to the consonances of the **diapente** <term>diapente</term> <note>The musical interval of a perfect fifth.</note> and the **diatessaron** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>The musical interval of a perfect fourth.</note>. For since the **diapente** consists of a **sesquialtera** <term>sesquialtera</term> <note>The ratio 3:2.</note> ratio and the **diatessaron** consists of a **sesquitertia** ratio, and since the **sesquialtera** ratio is larger while the **sesquitertia** is smaller—and among these consonances the **diapente** is similarly larger while the **diatessaron** is smaller—it is concluded that the larger consonance should be matched to the larger ratio, and the smaller consonance to the smaller ratio.

Therefore, the **diapente** must be placed in the **sesquialtera** ratio, and the **diatessaron** in the **sesquitertia** ratio. For when we subtract a **diatessaron** from a **diapente**, the space that remains is called a **tone** <term>tonus</term> <note>A whole step in music.</note>. In the same way, if we take away a **sesquitertia** ratio from a **sesquialtera**, a **sesquioctava** <term>sesquioctava</term> <note>The ratio 9:8.</note> ratio remains. From this, it follows that the **tone** must be established in the **sesquioctava** ratio.

<summary>This page explains the mathematical principle that smaller numbers create larger musical intervals and proves that a musical "tone" is defined by the 9:8 ratio, found by subtracting a Fourth from a Fifth.</summary>

# Chapter 8: In which genus the octave-plus-fifth and the double octave consist. <gloss>Very easy to understand</gloss>

Since it has been demonstrated that the consonance of the **diapason** <term>diapason</term> <note>The musical interval of an octave, mathematically expressed by the 2:1 ratio.</note> consists in the **double** <term>dupla</term> ratio, the **diapente** <term>diapente</term> <note>The interval of a perfect fifth, expressed by the 3:2 ratio.</note> in the **sesquialtera**, the **diatessaron** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>The interval of a perfect fourth, expressed by the 4:3 ratio.</note> in the **sesquitertia**, and the **tone** in the **sesquioctave** <term>sesquioctava</term> <note>The whole step, expressed by the 9:8 ratio.</note> ratio: Now indeed it must be noted that just as the **double** and **sesquialtera** ratios joined together produce a **triple** ratio (as is held at the end of the third [book] <note>Likely referring to the third book of Boethius’s *De institutione musica*, or a similar foundational mathematical text.</note>), so too the **diapason** and the **diapente** joined together produce another consonance which retains its name from both terms. Therefore, the consonance of the **diapason-plus-diapente** <note>The interval of a twelfth (an octave plus a fifth).</note> is necessarily placed in the **triple** ratio. 

If, however, we add a **sesquitertia** to the **triple** ratio, it immediately becomes a **quadruple** ratio. Whence, if a **diatessaron** symphony is added to the **diapason-plus-diapente** consonance, a **bisdiapason** <term>bisdiapason</term> <note>The double octave, or the interval of a fifteenth (4:1 ratio).</note> consonance is produced, which for this reason can in no way be placed in any ratio other than the **quadruple**. 

Therefore, the **diapason**, the **diapason-plus-diapente**, and the **bisdiapason** belong to the **multiple** <term>multiplici</term> <note>The "Multiple" genus of inequality in Pythagorean mathematics, where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller (e.g., 2:1, 3:1, 4:1).</note> genus of inequality. But the **diapente**, the **diatessaron**, and the **tone** belong to the **superparticular** <term>superparticulari</term> <note>The "Superparticular" genus, where the larger number contains the smaller number plus one more unit (e.g., 3:2, 4:3, 9:8).</note> genus. Since, therefore, the [investigation] of the consonances of the present consideration...

It categorizes intervals into the "multiple" and "superparticular" mathematical genera.</summary>

<meta>This page completes the word "ratio" from the previous page's discussion on the double octave. It moves from specific interval definitions into a broader Boethian and Pythagorean philosophy of sound, motion, and the limits of musical consonance based on mathematical simplicity.</meta>

...ratio emerged. And since consonance itself is a fixed mixture of two voices according to **Boethius** <note>original: "Boetium"; Boethius (c. 480–524 AD) was the most influential musical authority of the Middle Ages.</note>. **Sound** <term>sonus</term>, however, is a falling of a modulated voice produced in one tension; it is indeed the smallest particle of melody. Moreover, every sound consists of a **strike** <term>pulsus</term> <note>referring to the physical impact or vibration that creates sound.</note>. Finally, every strike occurs from **motion** <term>motu</term>, as was mentioned before in the first [chapter] of the second [book].

And since of motions some are equal and others are unequal: of the unequal motions, some are greatly unequal, others less so, and others moderately unequal. For from the equality of motions, an equality of sounds is born. From moderate inequality, which is also more manifest, the closest and simplest **proportions** <term>proportiones</term> arise—namely, **multiples** <term>multiplices</term> and **superparticulars** <term>superparticulares</term> <note>These are the two "purest" classes of ratios in Pythagorean math. Multiples are whole numbers like 2:1 or 3:1. Superparticulars are ratios where the top number is exactly one greater than the bottom, like 3:2 or 4:3.</note>. These are the **double**, **triple**, **quadruple**, **sesquialtera**, **sesquitertia**, and **sesquioctave** <note>original: "dupla tripla quadrupla sexqualtera & sexquitertia atq; sexqoctaua"; these correspond to the octave, twelfth, double octave, perfect fifth, perfect fourth, and the whole tone.</note>.

From unequal proportions that are manifold, or not so clear, or entirely distant from one another, **dissonances** emerge; for no harmony of sounds is produced. Hence, for this reason, the **Pythagoreans** <term>pythagorici</term> <note>Followers of Pythagoras, who defined music through strict mathematical ratios.</note> excluded consonances beyond the **double octave** <term>bisdiapason</term>, which is the greatest distance at the extremity of sounds in a **quadruple** interval. And although it was said above in the fourth chapter, by the same rule the **double octave-plus-fifth** <term>bisdiapason diapente</term> and the **double**...

...to preserve the **octave-plus-fourth** <term>diapason diatessaron</term> <note>original: "diapason diatessaron"; an interval of an 11th.</note> more than the **octave-plus-fifth** <term>diapason diapente</term> and the **octave-plus-fourth** seem to have. But since, when we depart from the **quadruple** <term>quadruplum</term> interval of voices—which, as mentioned before, is seen as the maximum in harmonies—that very excess brings a certain **dissonance** <term>dissonantia</term>. And the further sounds extend themselves beyond the **double-octave** <term>bisdiapason</term> <note>original: "bisdiapason"; a range of two octaves, or a 15th.</note>, the more such a distance of sounds seems to mix dissonance for the ears.

**Boethius** <note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 477–524 AD), the primary authority on music theory for the Middle Ages.</note> and other musicians, therefore, in investigating **consonances** <term>consonantie</term>, decisively fixed the quadruple space of sounds as a sufficiently distant limit, beyond which musical consonances ought by no means to be expanded.

For a **tone** <term>tonus</term> is made of two sounds and one **interval** <term>interuallum</term>. A **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term>, however, consists of four sounds and three intervals. A **fifth** <term>diapente</term>, moreover, contains five sounds and four intervals. The **octave** <term>diapason</term>, therefore, since it is formed from a fourth and a fifth, has eight sounds and seven intervals. The **octave-plus-fifth** <term>diapasondiapente</term>, having twelve sounds, comprises eleven intervals. Finally, the **double-octave** <term>bisdiapason</term> retains fifteen...

**sounds** <note>original: "fonos"; a Greek-derived term for sounds or tones.</note>. It possesses fourteen **intervals** <term>intervalla</term>. 

Thus, each **consonance** <term>consonantia</term> <note>A combination of sounds that results in a pleasant, unified effect on the ear.</note> always has one fewer interval than the number of its **voices** <term>voces</term> <note>In this context, "voices" refers to the individual pitches or notes that define the interval.</note>. And it should be noted that the **fifth** <term>diapente</term> and the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term>, when considered separately, exceed the **octave** <term>diapason</term>. For the fifth is completed by five sounds, while the fourth is completed by four. When these are added together, they become nine; however, the octave is filled with only eight sounds. 

Therefore, when we speak of **composite consonances** <note>Intervals formed by stacking two or more simpler intervals.</note>, it must be understood that the simple intervals themselves are joined together in such a way that the last sound of the first consonance is the same as the first sound of the following second consonance within that composition. The following fifth book shows the capacity and nature of these consonances.

The Greeks assert that **Mercury** <note>The Roman name for Hermes, the messenger god often credited in myth with inventing the lyre from a tortoise shell.</note> was the inventor of this **four-stringed instrument** <term>quadricordum</term>. However, this is not at all to be believed, because there is no doubt that **Jubal** <note>A descendant of Cain mentioned in Genesis 4:21 as "the father of all who play stringed instruments and pipes."</note> sought out this **tetrachord** <term>tetracordum</term> <note>A scale or instrument consisting of four notes.</note> first, before the Great Flood. Later, the art of singing was gradually directed and increased in many ways by several people. For **Coroebus**, the son of Atys, who was the king of the **Lydians**, added a fifth string. Then **Hyagnis** the Phrygian added a sixth. Finally, **Terpander** of Lesbos added a seventh, led by the order of the **seven planets**. As appears from the aforementioned chapter, these planets complete the music of the world. 

Likewise, because there are only seven sounds that nourish all of music—each of which is separated from the others by its distinctness, for each one rejects similarity to the others and they are entirely different from one another—for this reason, **Virgil** <note>The Roman epic poet Publius Vergilius Maro (70–19 BC).</note> says in his sixth book: "seven distinctions of voices" <note>original: "septem discrimina vocum"; a quote from the Aeneid, Book VI, line 646, referring to the notes of the lyre.</note>, that is, seven different voices, in the image of the seven...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical theory and the development of scales, moving from the history of the four-stringed lyre to the cosmic and physical origins of the seven-note system. ...of the planets, which complete the **mundane music** <term>musica mundana</term> <note>The "Music of the Spheres," an ancient philosophical concept where the movements of celestial bodies create a divine, mathematical harmony.</note> and the seven days of the week, which, led through many rotations, fill the whole body of the year. In the same way, these seven sounds, unfolded many times, perfect **harmonic and instrumental music** <term>musicen armonicam atque instrumentalem</term> through their low and high pitches.

Of these voices, the lowest is called **Hypate** <note>original: "Hypate"; while the Greek word literally means "highest," in music it refers to the "highest" string in terms of rank or physical position on the lyre, which produced the *lowest* pitch.</note>, as if it were "greater" and "more honorable." For this reason, they also call **Jupiter** "hypatos"—that is, the supreme. Likewise, a consul is called by the same name because of the excellence of his dignity. The specific quality of this Hypate is attributed to **Saturn** <note>In the ancient cosmic order, Saturn was the slowest-moving planet and was therefore associated with the deepest, lowest musical note.</note> because of its slowness of motion and the gravity of its sound.

The second note is indeed called **Parhypate** <note>original: "parhypate"; meaning "next to the hypate."</note>, as if it were fixed next to the Hypate. The third is **Lycanos** <note>original: "lycanos"; the string played by the index finger.</note>, because the finger we call the **index** is called *lycanos* by the Greeks. Among them, the name *lycanos* is derived from "licking" <note>original: "ligendo"; a traditional medieval explanation suggesting the index finger is the one used to taste or lick food.</note>; and since, when singing, the index finger—which they called *lycanos*—reached for this string, which was the third from the Hypate, the string itself retained the name Lycanos.

The fourth is called **Mese** <note>original: "mese"; meaning "middle."</note>, because between the first and seventh notes, the fourth is always the middle. The fifth is **Paramese** <note>original: "paramese"; meaning "beside the middle."</note>, as if it were placed next to the middle. The seventh is called **Nete** <note>original: "nete"; from the Greek *neate*, meaning "lowest." In the physical layout of the lyre, this was the string furthest from the player's body, producing the *highest* pitch.</note>, that is, the "lower." Between this Nete and the Paramese is the sixth note, which is called **Paranete** <note>original: "paranete"; meaning "next to the nete."</note>, as if it were positioned next to the Nete. However, since the Paramese is the third from the Nete, with the same term...

...called **Trite** <note>original: "Trite"; literally "the third."</note>, that is, the third, the description of which is made in this manner:

| | |
|----------|----------|
| **Hypate** <note>The lowest note.</note> | 1st |
| **Parhypate** <note>Next to the lowest.</note> | 2nd |
| **Lycanos** <note>The "indicator" note.</note> | 3rd |

| **Two conjunct tetrachords** <term>tetracorda coniuncta</term> | |

These seven strings indeed seem more essential and more necessary because, as was said before, they are the only ones that differ from one another, through which all melody is directed. This is the opinion of the **Lord Reconciler** <note>original: "dominus reconciliator"; a reference to the medieval scholar Pietro d'Abano (c. 1250–1316), known as the "Conciliator," who wrote famous commentaries on Aristotle.</note> regarding the third musical problem of **Aristotle**. For when we ascend above these seven, we soon encounter a sound similar to the first, lower one. 

And if we proceed further, a sound similar to the second string occurs; and in this way, the replication of the seven different sounds proceeds. For the eighth is similar to the first, the ninth to the second, the tenth to the third, the eleventh to the fourth, the twelfth to the fifth, the thirteenth to the sixth, and the fourteenth to the seventh. The fifteenth replicates the eighth, which also carries the likeness of the first. Therefore, since there are only seven distinct sounds...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the numerical properties of musical intervals, transitioning from the seven-stringed system mentioned on the previous page to the completion of the eight-note octave system. It explains the transition from the "Heptachord" (seven strings) to the "Octochord" (eight strings) and the resulting change in nomenclature for the strings.</meta>

...en. The eighth is the first which takes on the likeness of the first note. For every sound brings with it the likeness of its own octave <term>diapason</term> <note>original: "octaui"</note>. From this, it follows that the **octave consonance** <term>diapason consonantia</term>—which creates such a joining of the outermost sounds that they seem to be one and the same sound—is completed from eight sounds, according to the **Pythagoreans** and **Ptolemy** <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century mathematician and music theorist.</note>. 

For to the seven previously described strings, **Lycaon of Samos** <note>A figure in Greek musical legend credited with adding the eighth string to the lyre.</note> added an eighth string so that he might complete the full octave consonance, which consists of an **eight-stringed system** <term>octocordo</term>. He fitted this new string between the **Paramese**—which used to be called the **Trite**—and the **Paranete**. Thus, this new string itself became the third from the highest note <note>original: "nete"</note>. The string that was formerly called **Trite** lost that name and was thereafter called only **Paramese** <note>meaning "next to the middle"</note> because it was situated after the middle string. Meanwhile, the string placed between it and the **Paranete** deservedly took the name of **Trite** <note>literally "the third"</note>, as it was now the third note from the top. This is the description of that eight-stringed system:

| **Hypate** <note>The lowest note.</note> | 1st |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Parhypate** <note>Next to the lowest.</note> | 2nd |
| **Lycanos** <note>The "indicator" note.</note> | 3rd |
| **Mese** <note>The "middle" note.</note> | 4th |
| **Paramese** <note>Next to the middle.</note> | 5th |
| **Trite** <note>The "third" note from the top.</note> | 6th |
| **Paranete** <note>Next to the highest note.</note> | 7th |
| **Nete** <note>The highest note.</note> | 8th |

->Two disjunct tetrachords<-

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of ancient Greek musical systems, specifically explaining how four-note groups (tetrachords) are linked together. It moves from describing the seven-stringed heptachord, where groups share a note, to the eight-stringed octochord, where groups are separated, and lists historical musicians who expanded the system further.</meta>

For in the **heptachord** <term>eptacordo</term> <note>A seven-stringed musical scale or instrument.</note> there are two joined **tetrachords** <term>tetracorda</term> <note>Groups of four notes.</note>. Indeed, **conjunction** <term>sinaphe</term> <note>original: "Sinaphe"</note>—which is what the Latins call a "joining"—occurs in the heptachord whenever a single shared boundary continues and connects two tetrachords. And certainly, in the heptachord arranged above, the **middle note** <term>mese</term> <note>original: "mese"</note>, which is the center, is common to these two tetrachords. For it is the last string of the first tetrachord and the first string of the second.

Therefore, for this reason, the heptachord is called **conjoined** <note>original: "cimineinon," a corruption of the Greek "synemmenon."</note>. However, the **octochord** <term>octocordū</term> <note>An eight-stringed scale.</note> is called **disjoined** <note>original: "diezeumenon"</note>. For in the octochord there are two separate tetrachords: the first four strings fill the first tetrachord, and the other four complete the second.

In the octochord, then, there is **disjunction** <term>diezeusis</term>, which occurs whenever two tetrachords are separated by the interval or interposition of a whole tone or a semitone.

Therefore, since in the octochord the **middle note** <note>original: "mese"</note>—which took its name from its central location—is not strictly in the middle, but rather two "middles" are found, **Theophrastus of Pieria** <note>original: "Theophrastus periothes," a 6th-century BC musician.</note> added one string to the lower, deeper section. Because this was placed above the lowest string <term>hypate</term>, it was named the **next-to-the-lowest** <term>hyparhypate</term> <note>original: "hyparipate"</note>. **Histiaeus of Colophon** <note>original: "Estiachus melophonius," a musician mentioned by Boethius.</note> fitted a tenth string into the lower section. **Timotheus of Miletus** <note>A famous and controversial Greek musician known for increasing the number of strings on the lyre.</note> added an eleventh string, which, when it was added above the lowest <term>hypate</term> and the next-to-the-lowest <term>parhypate</term>, was...

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the ancient Greek musical scale. Having previously described the "disjunct" (separated) tetrachord, the text now transitions to the "joined" (synemmenon) tetrachord, explaining how it solves the problem of harsh dissonances. It then describes the expansion of the system into the "highest" (hyperboleon) tetrachord and the final addition of the "lowest note" (proslambanomenos) to ensure the Middle string (Mese) is mathematically centered.</meta>

...joined, that is, **synemmenon** <term>synemmenon</term> <note>original: "sinemena." In Greek music theory, this refers to a "conjunct" tetrachord, where two four-note groups share a common note to bridge an interval.</note> in this manner: the **Middle** <term>mese</term>, the **Third-joined** <term>trite synemmenon</term>, the **Next-to-the-last-joined** <term>paranete synemmenon</term>, and the **Last-joined** <term>nete synemmenon</term>. Indeed, this **joined four-note group** <term>synemmenon tetracordum</term> is added and linked with the string of the **Middle** <term>mese</term> for the purpose of tempering the **tritone** <term>trittonus</term> <note>An interval of three whole tones, historically known as the "Devil in Music" because its harsh, unstable sound was considered difficult to sing and unpleasant to hear.</note>, whose dissonant roughness nature abhors. For this four-note group would be superfluous if the tritone occurring between the **next-to-the-lowest of the middle** <term>parhypate meson</term> <note>original: "parapathemeson," likely a variation of parhypate meson.</note> and the **next-to-the-middle** <term>paramese</term> could be tempered in any other way. 

Therefore, the philosophers retained only the first three **four-note groups** <term>tetrachorda</term> in the **eleven-stringed scale** <term>endecacordo</term>—namely, the **lowest** <term>hypaton</term>, the **middle** <term>meson</term>, and the **disjunct** <term>diezeugmenon</term>—within the arrangement of the **monochord** <term>monocordo</term> <note>The monochord was a scientific instrument with a single string and a movable bridge, used by ancient and medieval theorists to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals.</note>. 

But since the **Middle** <term>mese</term>, which takes its name from its "middle place," was not actually in the center in the arrangement of the eleven-stringed scale, one other four-note group was added above the **last-disjunct** <term>nete diezeugmenon</term>. Because it surpassed the other strings in sharpness <note>The Latin "acumine" refers to high pitch.</note>, it was called the **hyperboleon** <term>hyperboleon</term>, meaning the "upper added" group. And indeed, it is joined with the **last-disjunct** string in this manner: **Last-disjunct** <term>nete diezeugmenon</term>, **Third-highest** <term>trite hyperboleon</term>, **Next-to-the-last-highest** <term>paranete hyperboleon</term>, and **Last-highest** <term>nete hyperboleon</term>. 

Thus there are four **joined four-note groups** <term>tetracorda coniuncta</term> within fourteen strings. Because the **Middle** <term>mese</term> was still not yet in the center among these, a single string called the **added note** <term>proslambanomenos</term> <note>original: "proslambanomenos," meaning "the acquired" or "added" note in Greek.</note> was added at the lower part, as if it were "acquired" or "apposed," for the reason that it was the last one to be invented.

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the Greek musical scale, specifically addressing the "Highest" tetrachord and explaining why the "Joined" (synemmenon) tetrachord is treated as an optional addition to the standard fifteen-string monochord. It also provides Latin translations for Greek musical terms attributed to the scholar Albinus.</meta>

...of the **Highest** <term>hyperboleon</term>, of which the middle—namely the **Joined** <term>synemmenon</term>—is not included in the sequence of the fifteen strings of the philosophical **monochord** <term>monocordum</term> <note>A single-stringed instrument used to mathematically demonstrate musical intervals.</note>. For, as has been stated before, it introduces a redundancy; while it was sought only to temper the **tritone** <term>trittonum</term> <note>An interval of three whole tones, famously known as the 'devil in music' for its perceived instability.</note>, it was not intended to extinguish it. This is because, without the tritone, the different species of the **fifth** <term>diapente</term> and the **octave** <term>diapason</term> could not be fully understood or distinguished. Thus, it is added to the string of the **middle** <term>mese</term> at will, and removed at will.

Albinus <note>Likely referring to Albinus, a contemporary of Boethius, known for his work on music theory.</note> translated the strings of these five tetrachords into the Latin language. He called the **Lowest** <term>Hypatas</term> "Principal"; the **Middle** <term>Mesas</term> "Middle"; the **Joined** <term>Sinemenas</term> "Joined"; the **Disjoined** <term>Diezeugmenas</term> "Disjoined"; and the **Highest** <term>Hyperboleas</term> "Excellent."

Moreover, it must be considered here that every **tetrachord** <term>tetracordum</term> sounds the consonance of a **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term>. Indeed, "tetrachord" is named from *tetra*, which means "four," and *corda*, meaning "string." Thus, a tetrachord is called a joining of four strings.

Likewise, the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> is named from *dia*, which means "of" or "from," and *tessara*, meaning "four"—as if it were a consonance made from four sounds. Regarding this, it should also be noted that *dia* written with the Latin vowel "i" <note>The author is making a linguistic distinction between the preposition 'dia' (through/from) and 'di/dyo' (two).</note> is the same as the preposition "of" (*de*). But when written with the Greek "y" [upsilon], it signifies "two."

<meta>This page follows the definition of a tetrachord (a four-stringed scale segment) from the previous page. It begins a new chapter categorizing the three "genera" or types of musical scales used in ancient Greek theory, specifically focusing on the Diatonic genus.</meta>

## On the Types of Tetrachords, Chapter 2

Among the philosophers, there were three types of melodies which were distinguished by the essence of their **tetrachords** <term>tetracordum</term> <note>A series of four notes spanning the interval of a fourth.</note>. We know that the word *melos* <note>original: "melos"</note> is derived from the sweetness of honey <note>original: "mellis"</note>; likewise, **melody** <term>melodia</term> is, as it were, a sweet song. The first genus was called the **diatonic** <term>dyattonicum</term> <note>From the Greek 'diatonos,' meaning 'stretched through' or 'natural.'</note>. In this genus, each tetrachord proceeds by a minor **semitone** <term>semitonium</term>, a **tone** <term>tonus</term>, and another **tone**, as seen here:

->Tetrachord in the diatonic genus<-
->Lowest of the lowest<- <note>original: "Hypatehypato"</note>
->semitone<-
->Next to the lowest of the lowest<- <note>original: "Paripatehypaton"</note>
->tone<-
->Indicator of the lowest<- <note>original: "Lycanos hypaton"</note>
->tone<-
->Lowest of the middle<- <note>original: "Hypatemeson"</note>
->Consonance of a fourth<- <note>original: "Diatessaron consonantia"</note>

In this diatonic genus, the tetrachord always consists of three intervals. The first interval, which lies between the first and second strings, is a minor semitone. The second, which occurs between the second and third, is a tone. The third also... <note>The sentence continues onto the following page.</note>

<summary>This page introduces the three genera of music, providing the etymological roots of 'melody' and detailing the structure of the Diatonic tetrachord, which consists of a semitone followed by two whole tones.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the diatonic genus, explaining why it is considered the most "natural" of the three musical systems and detailing its structural reliance on the interval of the fourth (diatessaron).</meta>

...is because between the third and fourth string there is a full space of a tone. <insert>For I believe it is called **diatonic** <term>dyatonicon</term></insert> <note>From the Greek *diatonos*, meaning "stretched through" or "natural."</note> as if it were "two-toned," because it naturally possesses two tones within its two intervals. Because this genus is somewhat harsher and more natural than the others, it was retained by the philosophers, and the discipline of music deals with it almost exclusively. 

For in all singing, this mode exists naturally: that in every grouping of four notes, the consonance of a **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"; a stable musical interval spanning four notes.</note> should always be produced, which consists of two distinct tones and one semitone. This arrangement fits conveniently in this diatonic genus, but not at all in the **chromatic** <term>cromatico</term> or **enharmonic** <term>enarmonico</term> <note>The other two ancient Greek melodic systems; the chromatic used half-steps and the enharmonic used even smaller "quarter-tones."</note> genera. 

<insert>Thus, in the diatonic genus, every</insert> **tetrachord** <term>tetracordum</term> <note>A scale segment of four notes.</note> begins from a semitone and proceeds through two tones, since a semitone is softer than a tone. <insert>For each</insert> thing in nature ought to begin with the sweeter elements and afterwards proceed through the harsher and rougher ones. <insert>Because if the tetrachord began with a tone,</insert> the "conjoined" tetrachord <term>sinemenon</term> <note>original: "sinemenon"; a specific part of the Greek scale system used to modulate or change pitch.</note> would have been added and invented in vain, tied to the **middle string** <term>meses</term> <note>original: "meses"; the central, tuning-reference note of the scale.</note> to avoid the **tritone** <term>trittoni</term> <note>An unstable and harsh interval of three whole tones, often avoided in early music theory.</note>, as the diligent reader can discover for himself. 

Therefore, since we naturally produce a fourth when singing within four notes, and since the fourth can be considered in three ways...

<summary>This page explains why the diatonic genus is the foundation of music theory, noting that its structure (semitone-tone-tone) is the most natural and avoids the harsh tritone interval.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the diatonic genus from page 74. While the previous page established why the diatonic system is considered natural, this page defines the mathematical and melodic structure of the "fourth" (diatessaron) and its three variations.</meta>

...in the **diatonic genus** <term>dyattonico</term>. Therefore, there is said to be a threefold mode of singing. 

For the first mode of singing occurs when, among four sounds, we modulate the first **species of the fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"; an interval spanning four notes. A "species" refers to a specific arrangement of tones and semitones within that interval.</note>, specifically by an **ascending** <term>intensiue</term> <note>In period music theory, *intensio* refers to the tightening of a string to raise the pitch; hence, moving upward in a scale.</note> movement through a semitone and two tones. And this is called the first species of the fourth. 

The second mode of singing, however, is when in four sounds we produce, while ascending, two tones and one **minor semitone** <term>semitonium minus</term> <note>In the Pythagorean tuning system used here, a semitone is slightly less than half a whole tone.</note>, from which the second species of the fourth is made. 

But the third mode of singing occurs in song when we produce, ascending, one tone, one semitone, and then another tone; and this is called, within these **tetrachords** <term>tetracordis</term> <note>A scale segment of four notes, the building block of the ancient Greek and medieval musical systems.</note>, the third species of the fourth. 

Truly, when one proceeds to the higher **strings** <note>original: "cordas"; the author uses the physical strings of an instrument to represent different pitches.</note>, we discern that in every grouping of four sounds, nothing else is being produced than those same three aforementioned species of the fourth, which are repeated in the order described above. For they differ from one another only by the location of the **semitone** <term>semitonii</term>. 

For the first species of the fourth has the semitone first, then two tones. The second, however, has two tones first, and then the semitone. The third, indeed, holds a tone first, then the semitone, and finally another tone. For the order of this process is preserved in the arrangement of the tetrachords. But when...

<summary>This page explains the three "species" or patterns of the musical interval of a fourth (diatessaron) used in the diatonic genus. It demonstrates that every four-note sequence in this system is simply a variation of where the semitone is placed relative to the whole tones.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the tetrachord species. While the previous page introduced the three patterns of tones and semitones that form a "fourth," this page explains how the addition of a low "added note" (proslambanomenos) organizes these patterns into a formal system used by both ancient philosophers and medieval Latin musicians.</meta>

Later, an **added note** <term>proslambanomenos</term> <note>original: "proslambanomenos"; literally the "extra-assumed" note. It was added to the bottom of the Greek musical system to complete a full two-octave range.</note> was added to the lower part, a whole tone away from the **principal of the principals** <term>hypate hypaton</term> <note>original: "hypatehypaton"; the lowest note of the original Greek system, roughly corresponding to the "B" below the bass clef.</note>. This was done so that its placement would not be without purpose: it completes an **octave** <term>diapason</term> <note>original: "diapason"; the interval spanning eight notes.</note> consonance with the **middle note** <term>mese</term>, which sits in the center of the fifteen-string system. It also forms a **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"; an interval of four notes.</note> with the **index note of the principals** <term>lycanos hypaton</term>; this is the specific variety of the fourth made of a tone, a semitone, and a tone. 

Although this was shown earlier as the third species, now—taking its starting point from the **added note** <term>proslambanomenos</term>—it is called the **first species** of the fourth. The **second species**, however, is that which is formed from a semitone and two tones. The **third species** is formed from two tones and a semitone. 

The philosophers maintained this specific order on the **monochord** <term>monochordon</term> <note>original: "monocordo"; a scientific instrument with a single string and a movable bridge, used to teach the mathematical ratios of music.</note> after the annexation of the **added note**. Latin musicians also honor this arrangement in their introductions to music and in the composition of the tones for **plainchant** <term>cantus planus</term> <note>original: "cantus plani"; the traditional, monophonic sacred music of the Western Church.</note>. 

Therefore, the consonance of the fourth has only three species because it consists of only four notes. For every consonance always has one fewer species than the number of its **notes** <note>original: "voces"; literally "voices," referring here to the individual pitches within the interval.</note>. When one proceeds beyond the third, the first species immediately presents itself again. Since it is similar to the first, it is considered the first; for that which is similar is not said to be different. The others are repeated in that same order. Indeed, the progression of these three species continues for as long as the notes [can reach]...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the intervals within the musical system. It specifically transitions from the theoretical structure of the diatessaron (the fourth) to its practical application in the "Hand" (the Guidonian system) and introduces the different musical genera, starting with the diatonic and chromatic.</meta>

Since, therefore, there are three different species of the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"; the musical interval spanning four notes.</note> found within six strings, as has been demonstrated, their succession and repetition are indicated by the natural arrangement of the strings in order.

The Latin musicians, establishing an introduction to music called the **Hand** <term>manus</term> <note>original: "manus"; referring to the "Guidonian Hand," a mnemonic device where each joint of the hand represents a note, used to teach singers how to visualize the musical scale.</note>, determined that only six different syllables—namely **ut**, **re**, **mi**, **fa**, **sol**, and **la**—fixed to specific positions on the strings, would bring forth those three species of the fourth. They were, however, to be repeated as often as necessity required. Through this repetition, the three species of the fourth are taken up again, as has been stated several times; however, these will be treated later in a separate section.

The **diatonic genus** <term>Dyattonicū</term> <note>original: "Dyattonicū"; the primary musical system used in Western chant, characterized by steps of whole tones and natural semitones.</note>, because it joins **tetrachords** <term>tetracorda</term> <note>original: "tetracorda"; a grouping of four notes.</note> so perfectly through the interval of the fourth—moving by a **semitone**, then a **tone**, and another **tone** across three distinct and suitably proportioned intervals—has been retained and observed by musicians. For all **harmony** <term>armonia</term> <note>original: "armonia"; in this context, it refers to the orderly, mathematical structure of the musical system rather than "chords" in the modern sense.</note> finds its foundation in this genus. Indeed, everything in the course of this treatise will be built upon it, as if on a solid foundation.

However, another genus by which the tetrachords were divided among philosophers was called **chromatic** <term>cromaticum</term> <note>original: "cromaticum"; a musical system that uses smaller, "colored" intervals to create different melodic qualities.</note>. For **chroma** <note>original: "Croma"</note> in Greek means **color** <note>original: "color"</note> in Latin. The name "chromatic" is derived from this, as if it produces the tetrachord through a different "shading" or string tension.

<summary>This page explains how the six syllables (ut-la) of the Guidonian Hand are used to navigate the species of the fourth and introduces the Diatonic and Chromatic genera.</summary>
