<meta>This page follows the introduction of the musical genera, transitioning from the Diatonic genus to a detailed technical description of the Chromatic genus. It explains how the intervals of the tetrachord are rearranged to create a different "color" of harmony.</meta>

For just as surfaces, when changed, transition into another color, so too did this kind of singing differ from the others, as if it were a "changed color" <note>The author is explaining the **chromatic genus**. The word "chromatic" comes from the Greek *chroma*, meaning color, suggesting that this system "colors" or embellishes the simpler diatonic scale.</note>. In this genus, the **tetrachord** <term>thetracordum</term> <note>original: "thetracordum"; a grouping of four notes that forms the building block of the ancient musical system.</note> proceeded by a **minor semitone**, a **major semitone**, and three semitones combined—one of which is major, namely the **apotome** <term>apothome</term> <note>original: "apothome"; in Pythagorean tuning, this is the "major" semitone, which is slightly larger than the "minor" semitone (limma).</note>, and two minor ones, which together make up a **minor third** <term>semidyttonum</term> <note>original: "semidyttonum"; literally a "half-ditone." In this context, it refers to the interval of a minor third, which consists of a whole tone plus a minor semitone.</note>.

And so, every tetrachord in this genus corresponded to the consonance of the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"; the interval of a perfect fourth.</note>, but not with the same distribution of tension as in the **diatonic genus** <term>dyattonico genere</term> <note>original: "dyattonico genere"; the standard scale of whole tones and natural semitones.</note>. To take an example that can be applied to the other tetrachords, we should look at the first tetrachord: between the **lowest note of the first group** <term>hypatehypaton</term> <note>original: "hypatehypaton"; the lowest note in the Greek system, corresponding to a modern B.</note> and the **second note** <term>parhypatehypaton</term> <note>original: "parhypatehypaton"; the note C.</note>, they established a minor semitone. Between that second note and the **third note** <term>lycanofhypaton</term> <note>original: "lycanofhypaton"; the 'index-finger' note of the lowest group, here tuned to C-sharp.</note>, they produced a major semitone, that is, the apotome.

But between this third note and the **lowest note of the middle group** <term>hypatemeson</term> <note>original: "hypatemeson"; the note E.</note>, there are two minor semitones and one major—specifically the apotome—which constitutes a minor third. This is called "semi-" because it sounds "imperfect," and a **ditone** <term>dyttonus</term> <note>original: "dyttonus"; a major third, or "two tones."</note> is so named as if it were two "imperfect" tones; for a minor third consists of one whole tone and one minor semitone. Since a major semitone and a minor semitone together make a full tone, a semitone remains <note>The author is breaking down the math: a minor third (1.5 tones) is made of one full tone plus one-half tone.</note>. The knowledge of this genus is made clear by this tetrachord.

Since indeed the **major semitone** <term>semitonium maius</term> <note>In the Pythagorean system used here, the major semitone (apotome) is slightly larger than the minor semitone (limma), and was often considered too dissonant for melodic use on its own.</note> is least of all contained within the harmonies because of its dissonance, and the **minor third** <term>semidyttonus</term> <note>original: "semidyttonus"; an interval spanning three notes of the scale.</note> leads to an inconsistency in the study of consonances when it occurs across two following strings with no middle note placed between them—because a minor third cannot in any way be produced in a single smallest interval between two strings, but cannot be held as anything less than two intervals across three strings, as anyone paying attention to the consonances can conclude for themselves—subsequent generations proved this very genus to be infamous for its excessive "softness" <note>Ancient music theorists often characterized the chromatic genus as "soft" or "sweet," sometimes even "effeminate," in contrast to the "manly" and stable diatonic genus.</note>, abandoning it entirely.

This allowed for incredibly subtle melodic shading, though it was notoriously difficult to sing and tune.</note>. As [will be explained] more broadly...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the three ancient Greek musical genera—the diatonic, chromatic, and enharmonic. While the previous page focused on the mathematical structure of the enharmonic tetrachord, this page explains why the chromatic and enharmonic styles eventually fell out of favor, leaving the diatonic genus as the foundation of Western music.</meta>

...was added in order <note>The author is referring to the "proslambanomenos" or "added note" at the bottom of the scale.</note> so that it might participate in the varied **tension** <note>original: "intensionem"; in ancient music theory, this refers to the tuning or tightening of strings to specific pitches.</note> of the different **genera** <term>genera</term> <note>The three types of scales in ancient Greek music: Diatonic, Chromatic, and Enharmonic.</note>. But after the rejection of two of these genera, in the **diatonic genus** <term>genus dyattonicum</term> <note>The most natural-sounding scale, consisting of whole tones and semitones, which forms the basis of modern major and minor scales.</note>—which alone was approved and retained by the philosophers—the **added note** <term>proslambanomenos</term> <note>original: "proslambanomenos"; the lowest note of the Greek "Greater Perfect System," added below the lowest tetrachord to complete a two-octave range.</note> itself was counted along with the other strings in the formation of the **consonances** <term>consonantia</term> <note>Musical intervals that sound stable and pleasant together, such as the fourth, fifth, and octave.</note>.

Truly, since in consonances nothing smaller than a **minor semitone** <note>original: "semitonio minore"; roughly a modern half-step, though calculated differently in Pythagorean tuning.</note> can be performed, as will be explained in what follows, the **quarter-tone** <term>diesis</term> <note>In the enharmonic genus, this is a very small interval, roughly half of a semitone.</note> is therefore very difficult to express because of its tiny size. And since a **major third** <term>ditone</term> <note>original: "dyttonus"; literally "two tones." In this ancient tuning, a major third was formed by two whole tones.</note> cannot naturally be contained within a single smallest interval between two adjacent strings—because it is actually completed by the space between three consecutive strings—this **enharmonic genus** <term>genus enarmonicum</term> fell out of use because of its extreme difficulty. 

In each of the genera, therefore, every **tetrachord** preserved the consonance of the **perfect fourth** <term>diatessaron</term>, though with different tunings as has been declared. But the **chromatic** and **enharmonic** genera were rejected by musicians because of the difficulty they held in their performance. The **diatonic genus**, however, was retained and preserved with the highest veneration by the **Platonists**, the **Pythagoreans**, and other musicians, because of its most sweet and well-fitted suitability, which is naturally arranged.

<summary>This page explains the historical transition from three musical genera to one, noting that the enharmonic and chromatic systems were abandoned due to their difficulty, while the diatonic system was favored by philosophers for its natural harmony.</summary>

# On the Intervals of the Fifteen Strings. Chapter 3.

The intervals of the fifteen strings arranged above must now be distinguished. For the **Added Note** <term>proslambanomenos</term> <note>original: "Proslambanomenos"; the lowest note of the ancient Greek scale, added to complete the two-octave system.</note> is the first and is lower in pitch than all the others. 

The second is the **lowest of the low** <term>hypate hypaton</term> <note>original: "hypatehypaton"; though the name means "highest," it refers to the highest physical position on the lyre's frame, which produced the lowest-sounding pitch.</note>, which is interpreted as the "lowest of the lowest notes." This name belonged to it by right before the addition of the **Added Note** <term>proslambanomenos</term>, when it was then the first and lowest string. But now, even though it is higher by a **whole tone** <note>original: "tono"; a standard musical step.</note> than the first note, it has retained the name originally assigned to it. 

The third is the **next-to-the-lowest of the low** <term>parhypate hypaton</term> <note>original: "parhypatehypaton"; the prefix "par-" signifies the note adjacent to the "hypate."</note>, which is stretched from the **lowest of the low** <term>hypate hypaton</term> by only a **minor semitone** <note>original: "minore semitonio"; a Pythagorean half-step.</note>. 

The fourth is the **index finger of the low** <term>lycanos hypaton</term> <note>original: "lycanos hypaton"; named for the finger (the *lycanos* or "licking" finger) used to pluck it.</note>, which is a whole tone distant from the **next-to-the-lowest of the low** <term>parhypate hypaton</term>. This note also forms the consonance of a **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: "diatessarõ"; a fundamental musical interval spanning four notes.</note> of the **diatonic genus** <term>dyattonici generis</term> <note>The standard musical scale based on whole tones and semitones.</note> with the **Added Note** <term>proslambanomenos</term>. 

The fifth string is the **lowest of the middle** <term>hypate meson</term> <note>original: "hypatemeson"; the start of the second tetrachord in the Greek system.</note>, interpreted as the "lowest of the middle notes." It is distant from the preceding note nearest to it in pitch by a whole tone, and it forms the consonance of a **fifth** <term>diapente</term> <note>original: "diapentes"; a stable musical interval spanning five notes.</note> with the first string. 

The sixth is the **next-to-the-lowest of the middle** <term>parhypate meson</term> <note>original: "parhypatemeson"</note>, distant from the **lowest of the middle** <term>hypate meson</term> by only a minor semitone. 

The seventh is the **index finger of the middle** <term>lycanos meson</term> <note>original: "lycanosmeson"</note>, distant in tension from the sixth string by a whole tone. 

The eighth string is the **middle note** <term>mese</term> <note>original: "mese"; the central and most important note of the Greek scale, serving as a pitch reference for all others.</note>, which is called "middle" and is a whole tone away from the seventh string closest to it. It holds a **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> with the fifth string, a **fifth** <term>diapente</term> <note>original: "diapenten"</note> with the fourth string, and with the first string, namely...

--- Page 181 ---
<meta>This page continues the catalog of the fifteen strings of the Greater Perfect System. Having described the lowest four strings on the previous page, the author now explains the functional relationship between the Added Note and the Middle string, before introducing the ninth string (paramese) and the mathematical challenges of the tritone interval.</meta>

The **Added Note** <term>proslambanomenos</term> <note>original: "proslabanomenos"; the lowest note of the scale, which completes the full two-octave range.</note> completes the **octave** <term>diapason</term> consonance <note>original: "consonantia"; a harmonious sounding-together of notes.</note>. The **Middle string** <term>mese</term>, sitting in the central position, rules over the other strings that follow it, and they refer back to it as if to a principle and the foundation of their relationship. 

The ninth string is the **next-to-the-middle** <term>paramese</term> <note>original: "paramese"; named for its position adjacent to the "mese" or middle string.</note>, which is distant from the **Middle string** <term>mese</term> by a **whole tone** <note>original: "tono"</note>. Although this string, when combined with the two preceding whole tones, completes a **tritone** <term>tritonus</term> <note>original: "trittonũ"; an interval of three whole tones, famously difficult to sing and known for its "harshness" or dissonance.</note> that is harsh in its severity, it was nevertheless retained by the philosophers and fixed in this location. They did this so that this ninth string might complete one of the seven perfect species of the **octave** <term>diapason</term> with the second string—namely, the **lowest of the low** <term>hypate hypaton</term>—which is proven to correspond to it in the eighth position <note>In ancient theory, the interval from the 2nd string to the 9th string forms an octave (an 8-note span).</note>. 

This octave itself is filled by five whole tones and two **semitones** <term>semitonia</term>, as was more clearly explained in the fourth book of the fourth section. The octave is perfect between the second and ninth strings even when that discordant tritone occurs; for within those eight strings, five whole tones and two **minor semitones** <note>original: "minora semitonia"; half-steps.</note> are arranged, by which the symphony of the octave is completed. 

If this **next-to-the-middle** <term>paramese</term> string were distant from the **Middle string** <term>mese</term> by only a minor semitone, a **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> consonance would be created among those four strings that [currently] contain the tritone. Naturally, a fourth should always be preferred in a group of four strings according to the **diatonic genus** <term>dyattonicum</term> <note>The standard musical scale consisting of whole steps and half steps.</note> by which all song is ruled and proceeds. However, the nature of things so exercised the skill of the philosophers that they applied a long and exquisite investigation to find a suitable way by which the terrible harshness of that tritone could be avoided in melody, while still allowing the perfect species of the octave itself to be conceived.

<summary>This page discusses the ninth string (paramese) and its relationship to the middle string, explaining how ancient music theorists managed the "harsh" tritone interval to maintain a perfect octave system.</summary>
<keywords>octave, diapason, paramese, mese, tritone, hypate hypaton, diatonic genus, musical consonance, Greek music theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of the fifteen strings of the Greater Perfect System. Having established the relationship between the middle string and the next-to-middle string (paramese) on the previous page, the author now addresses the "wretched" tritone interval and explains why the "Joined" tetrachord was introduced to maintain the integrity of the musical system.</meta>

For this reason, they established that the **Joined Tetrachord** <term>tetrachordum synemmenon</term> <note>original: "tetracordū illud sinemenō"; a set of four notes "joined" or overlapping with the standard scale to provide a melodic alternative that avoids harsh intervals.</note> should be connected to the **Middle string** <term>mese</term>. Because this tetrachord began with a **semitone** <note>original: "semitonio"; a half-step, the smallest interval in this system.</note>, just like the other tetrachords, it bypassed the third following **whole tone** <note>original: "tonũ"</note> that was established between the **Middle string** <term>mese</term> and the **Next-to-the-middle** <term>paramese</term>. Consequently, those four strings which originally expressed a **tritone** <term>tritonus</term> <note>original: "trittonus"; a notoriously dissonant interval of three whole tones.</note> were afterwards made to produce the consonance of a **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term>.

However, since the removal and elimination of that tritone would destroy the second perfect species of the **octave** <term>diapason</term> consonance which we have already mentioned—because in the aforementioned eight strings, only four whole tones would then be intercepted along with three **minor semitones** <note>original: "semitonia miora"</note>, which when joined together do not fulfill an octave—the philosophers decreed that the tritone itself should remain. They preferred to have a constant struggle with that most wretched tritone rather than to remove one of the seven species of the octave from the total count. 

Here, that **Joined Tetrachord** <term>tetrachordum synemmenon</term> was removed at the appropriate time, and when it was necessary, it was brought back in a joined state with the said **Middle string** <term>mese</term>, as was stated in the first chapter.

The tenth string is the **third of the disjunct strings** <term>trite diezeugmenon</term>, distant from the **next-to-the-middle** <term>paramese</term> by only a minor semitone. It is interpreted as "the third of the disjunct strings," holding the third position counting from the **Middle string** <term>mese</term>. 

The eleventh string is the **next-to-the-last of the disjunct strings** <term>paranete diezeugmenon</term>, distant in pitch from the tenth string by a whole tone. 

The twelfth is the **last of the disjunct strings** <term>nete diezeugmenon</term>, distant from the eleventh (which is its neighbor) by a whole tone; and to the **Middle string** <term>mese</term>, which is central...

...completing the consonance of a **fifth** <term>diapente</term> <note>original: "diapētes"; an interval spanning five notes of the scale.</note>. This is, as it were, a certain model of the celestial order and its divisions.

For the **lowest of the middle** <term>hypate meson</term> is attributed to **Saturn**.

The **last of the divided** <term>nete diezeugmenon</term> to the **Earth**.

> And nature brings it about that the extremes on one side sound low, while on the other side they sound high; wherefore that highest course of the starry heaven, whose rotation is swifter, is moved with a sharp and excited sound.

The lunar voice, however, which is the lowest, [is moved] with the deepest sound. For the Earth...

## On the division of strings by tones and consonances according to proportions. Chapter 4.

I desire <note>original: "Cupior," likely meaning "I am eager that..."</note> that the division of the fifteen strings <note>The standard range of the Greek Greater Perfect System.</note> appear complete, provided we first judge them according to the dictates of arithmetic. Therefore, if we establish that the division should be described through the measurement of the string, or in numbers and proportions, a greater space of string and a larger quantity of number will produce deeper sounds. If, however, the length of the string were...

<meta>This page continues the discussion from Chapter 4 regarding the mathematical relationship between string length and musical pitch. The previous page ended mid-sentence, stating that if the length of the string were more contracted...</meta>

...[it will be] more contracted, since in that case a smaller plurality exists in the numbers, it will generate higher-pitched <note>original: "acutiores"</note> sounds. For from this comparison, in as much as each string is either longer or represented by a larger quantity of numbers, and another is more contracted or marked by fewer numbers, by that much is a deeper <term>gravior</term> or higher <term>acutior</term> sound discovered. 

Nor should it disturb the reader's mind that when tightening <note>original: "intendentes," referring to increasing the tension of a string to raise pitch</note> the strings, musicians have often marked the spaces of the proportions by a larger number, but when loosening <note>original: "remittentes," decreasing tension to lower pitch</note> them, by a smaller number. In that context, tension satisfies the high pitch and loosening satisfies the depth; for in that instance, they were only marking the spaces of the proportions, not concerning themselves with the specific quality of the high or low pitches. And for that reason, they would tighten toward a high pitch using larger numbers and often loosen toward depth with smaller numbers. 

Here, however, where we measure the physical spaces and sounds of the strings, it is necessary to follow the nature of things: to give larger numbers to the greater length of the strings (from which depth <term>gravitas</term> arises) and shorter numbers to the smaller length (from which the high pitch <term>acumen</term> of the voice is born). 

Therefore, since the instrument known as the **monochord** <term>monocordum</term> <note>A scientific instrument with a single string and a movable bridge, used to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of musical intervals.</note> can rationally be divided by a single string, we shall establish that single string as the one to be divided; within it, we will place those fifteen "voices of the philosophers" <note>The fifteen notes of the Greater Perfect System of ancient Greek music theory.</note> according to arithmetic proportions <term>arytmeticis proportionibus</term>. Indeed, what is produced by many strings in a monochord instrument would sound perfectly in one single string, if the physical touch and...

<meta>This page continues the technical demonstration of the monochord's division, moving from the general theory of pitch and string length discussed on the previous page to the specific mathematical ratios used to plot the fifteen notes of the Greater Perfect System.</meta>

Once the pins <note>original: "clauicule," likely referring to tuning pins or the bridge markers on the monochord</note> are struck, let us here arrange the division of those fifteen sounds proportionally on a single string. 

Let there be a string of nine equal measures <term>passuum</term>, and where the first measure begins at the deeper <term>graviorem</term> part, let the <term>Proslambanomenos</term> <note>Greek for "the added note," the lowest note in the ancient Greek musical system</note> be established, which is the first note. Where indeed that first measure is finished, the second note—namely the <term>Hypate Hypaton</term> <note>Greek for "the principal of the principals"; though "hypate" means highest, it refers to the highest physical string on the lyre, which produced the lowest pitch</note>—should be placed. This note stands a whole tone away from the first, because a string of nine measures contains within itself a string of eight measures plus one-eighth part of it, in a **sesquioctave** <term>sesquioctava</term> <note>A 9:8 ratio</note> proportion; from this ratio, as was shown in Book Four, a whole tone results. 

For the **Proslambanomenos**, since it is established in the first and deeper position, emits a deeper sound, from which all other sounds are born, just as all numbers are born from the unity of a single number. 

Once these two notes have been established, since there does not exist a whole movement between the second and third notes, but rather a minor semitone, it is necessary to create the fourth note before the third. This is because a non-integer musical movement <note>meaning an interval like a semitone that cannot be expressed as a simple whole-number ratio like 2:1 or 3:2</note> measures the string by no certain dimension; therefore, no major or minor semitone can be created by itself. 

For this reason, beginning on the first, deeper note, divide the whole string into four equal measures. Wherever the first measure ends, there you ought to fix the fourth note, that is, the <term>Lychanos Hypaton</term> <note>Greek for "the index-finger note of the principals"</note>, which will respond to the first note in a **diatessaron** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>the interval of a perfect fourth</note> consonance in a **sesquitertial** <term>sesquitertia</term> <note>a 4:3 ratio</note> proportion. For a string of four measures contains within itself a string of three measures and a third part besides. 

Now let the compass be placed on this fourth nu-

Through the eighth note <note>original: "voce," meaning the pitch or the point on the string representing that pitch</note>, let us divide the entire string into eight equal measures <term>passibus</term>. Then, with the compass fixed on that same fourth note, let it be turned back toward the deeper <note>original: "gutorẽ," likely a scribal abbreviation for "graviorem" (lower/deeper)</note> part; where that ninth measure rests, let the third note—namely the **Parhypate Hypaton** <note>The third note of the scale, literally "next to the principal of the principals"</note>—be placed. It stands at a distance of a whole tone from the fourth note, which is the **Lichanos Hypaton** <note>The fourth note; "Lichanos" refers to the index finger used to play it</note>. 

That space which occurs between the second note and this newly created third note is a **minor semitone** <term>semitonium minus</term> and is fixed. Indeed, those minor semitones that fall and remain in the natural order—whether after, before, or between two whole tones—are called "fixed." However, those that are typically created by dividing a whole tone for a specific time and then removed are called "movable" <note>This refers to the "chromatic" or "enharmonic" tunings where certain notes shifted, unlike the "diatonic" notes which remained constant</note>. 

Therefore, every semitone—whether major or minor, movable or fixed—because it is in no way able to divide the string into equal parts (as has been said), must be generated through certain circuits of whole musical movements <note>The author is explaining that you cannot simply find a semitone by halving a whole tone's physical distance; it must be derived through the ratios of larger intervals like the fourth or fifth</note>.

Again, let the compass be placed on the first note, dividing the entire string into three equal parts. Where the first measure then marks the limit, let the fifth note—namely the **Hypate Meson** <note>The fifth note of the scale, meaning "the principal of the middle [tetrachord]"</note>—be fixed. This creates the consonance of a **fifth** <term>diapente</term> <note>A perfect fifth, a 3:2 ratio</note> in relation to the first note in a **sesquialtera** <term>sesquialtera</term> proportion. 

For a string of three measures contains within itself a string of two measures plus one-half of it <note>The mathematical definition of the 3:2 ratio</note>. This fifth note makes a whole tone in relation to the fourth. Then, let the compass be placed on the third note, measuring the entire remaining string in four equal parts; where the first [measure] ends...

<meta>This page continues the geometric instructions for dividing the monochord string to find the notes of the scale. Having previously established the first four notes, the author now describes the construction of the fifth through eighth notes (Parhypate Meson, Lichanos Meson, and Mese) using mathematical proportions like the 4:3 fourth and the 2:1 octave.</meta>

...the step [of the compass] having been made, let the sixth note, namely the **Parhypate Meson** <note>The "next to the principal" note of the middle tetrachord</note>, be placed. This note maintains a **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> consonance toward the third note and a **minor semitone** <term>semitonium minus</term> toward the fifth note next to it; this [semitone] could not be obtained except by this or a similar method. 

If indeed the compass is placed on the fourth note, and the entire remainder of the string is measured out into four most equal steps, then where the previous step ended, let the seventh note, namely the **Lichanos Meson** <note>The "index finger" note of the middle tetrachord</note>, be placed. It provides a **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> consonance toward that same fourth note in a **sesquitertial ratio** <term>proportione sesquitertia</term> <note>The 4:3 ratio, the mathematical basis for the musical fourth</note>, and a whole tone toward the sixth note next to it in a **sesquioctave ratio** <term>proportione sesquioctava</term> <note>The 9:8 ratio, the mathematical basis for the whole tone</note>.

After these steps, return to the very first note of the string, where the fixed compass should divide the entire string into two most equal steps. Where the first step marks its boundary, you should establish the eighth note, namely the **Mese** <note>literally "the Middle," this was the central note of the ancient Greek musical system</note>. It is called the "middle" and forms a true, **equal-sounding** <term>equisonam</term> **octave** <term>diapason</term> consonance with the first note in a **double ratio** <term>dupla proportione</term> <note>The 2:1 ratio, where the string length is halved to produce a note an octave higher</note>; for a string length of two steps contains the string length of one step twice within itself. 

Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century mathematician and music theorist</note> calls this octave consonance "equal-sounding," as well as the **double-octave** <term>bisdiapason</term> which is doubled from it. As was discussed earlier in the fifth [chapter] of the second [book] <note>Likely referring to Boethius’s *Fundamentals of Music*, which transmits Ptolemy's theories</note>, he says that "equal-sounding" notes are those which, when struck together, produce a sound that is somehow a single, simple tone made from two. Those called "consonances," however, lead to a sound that is composite and mixed, even if it is pleasant, such as the **fifth** <term>diapente</term> and the **octave-plus-fifth** <term>diapason-plus-diapente</term>. For who does not most easily perceive this with their own ears? &c.

<summary>This page describes the geometric and mathematical derivation of the notes Parhypate Meson, Lichanos Meson, and Mese on the monochord. It explains the distinction between "equal-sounding" intervals like the octave and "consonant" intervals like the fifth, citing the authority of Ptolemy.</summary>
<keywords>monochord, Parhypate Meson, Lichanos Meson, Mese, diatessaron, diapason, Ptolemy, musical ratios, sesquitertia, sesquioctava, consonance</keywords>

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the octave (diapason). Having just identified the Mese as the eighth note that completes the octave, the author now explains why this interval sounds like a single, unified tone despite the difference in pitch.</meta>

...in the **octave** <term>diapason</term>, the two extreme sounds ring as one and the same **equal-sounding** <term>equisonam</term> tone. Indeed, that difference which you understand in the parts of the string, you can perceive when the two extreme sounds of the **octave** are struck together.

And just as on the **monochord** <term>monocordo</term> <note>A single-stringed instrument used to demonstrate musical ratios</note> or in this division of the string, there is no difference between a string of one measure and a string of two measures except that one is longer and the other shorter—yet it is the same thing doubled—so too the extreme voices of the **octave** differ in nothing except that the low voice emits a lower sound and the high voice a higher sound. 

For it is the property of equality that it always remains equal, even if the divided part changes its place; for even if two parts of a divided continuum are separated in location, as long as their quantity is not changed, they are always equal. Therefore, the extreme voices of the **octave** are equal and similar, as was touched upon in the first chapter, because every connection of eight voices <note>the sequence of eight notes that forms the scale</note> creates a similarity of the extremes. 

Rightly, therefore, **Ptolemy** <note>original: "Ptholemeus"; refers to Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century scholar whose work "Harmonics" defined Greek music theory</note> called the **octave** "equal-sounding," while he called the others "consonances" <term>consonantias</term>, which are known to differ greatly from this equality. For those consonances of the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> or **fifth** <term>diapente</term> do not divide the string through the middle, nor do they fall into the **double ratio** <note>the 2:1 mathematical ratio that defines the octave</note>, as was shown in the seventh [part of the] fourth [chapter].

<summary>This page explains the unique status of the octave (diapason) as an "equal-sounding" interval, arguing that its 2:1 mathematical ratio makes the two notes sound like the same pitch at different registers. It contrasts this perfect equality with other consonances like the fourth and fifth.</summary>

...which, when joined together, reach this union of **equality**. These things are said concerning the **equality of the octave** <note>original: "diapason equalitate"</note> so that it may be known to many that this is the interval which, because of its most perfect measurement, easily joins and creates voices on the **monochord** <term>monocordo</term> <note>A one-stringed instrument used by medieval scholars to demonstrate the mathematical ratios of music</note> and on this tangible string. It does so, however, in such a way that the **octave** <term>diapason</term> itself is always constituted from all its tones and semitones. Although this is usually done in many and various ways, this is nevertheless the superior measurement of the string: first to form and proportionally join eight voices, and then, if necessary, to progress indefinitely by octaves.

Remember therefore, reader, that the string divided through the middle (as described above) created the first **octave** for you. Now, however, if you wish to have its second species and the ninth voice, place your compass on the second voice, which is called the **hypate hypaton** <term>hypatehypaton</term> <note>The lowest note of the classical Greek musical system; in medieval theory, often associated with the note B or A</note>, dividing the whole string into two most equal steps. ### Chapter 5: Concerning the Division of Tones by Their Own Semitones.

Having obtained the measurement of the whole tones and of the natural or fixed **semitones** <term>semitonia</term> <note>A semitone is the smallest interval in this musical system, roughly half of a whole tone</note> and consonances on a single string, with arithmetic proportions determining them, it must now be shown by what method each individual whole tone established in the aforementioned measurement of the string ought to and can be divided into two unequal semitones. For there are the **minor semitone** <term>semitonium minus</term> and the **major semitone** <term>semitonium maius</term>, which are the members that, when joined together, complete a whole tone.

There are also, for this reason, smaller diminished parts <note>original: "diminutiōes pticule"</note> which divide the semitones themselves and which, when later joined together, complete them; these we exclude and omit because of the excessively [small] portion which they occupy, as was touched upon in the third part of the fourth [chapter]. Therefore, since the semitones appear as the larger and primary divisions in the division of the whole tones, we shall distinguish them by dividing each individual tone in the measurement of the string.

Therefore, since according to the schools of musicians, larger parts cannot be divided into equal smaller parts in a **superparticular** <note>A "superparticular" ratio is one where the numerator is exactly one greater than the denominator (like 3:2 or 9:8). In Pythagorean tuning, such ratios cannot be divided into two equal rational parts.</note> ratio or in other classes [of ratios], a whole tone—which consists of a **9:8 ratio** <term>sesquioctava</term> <note>original: "sesquioctava"; the mathematical ratio of a whole tone where the string length is divided into 9 parts vs 8 parts</note>—is divided into two unequal semitones. Wherefore, let the compass be placed on the third voice arranged and measured above, dividing the whole string into eight most equal steps...

<summary>This page introduces the mathematical necessity of dividing whole tones into unequal major and minor semitones. It explains that because the whole tone is based on a 9:8 ratio, it cannot be split into two perfectly equal halves.</summary>

<keywords>semitone, whole tone, sesquioctava, musical proportions, monochord, arithmetic, interval division</keywords>

<margin>
The major semitone is discordant by itself.
The minor semitone is concordant.

...which, returning in that third voice, is turned back toward the lower part. And where that ninth step has placed its boundary, let there be the division of the minor and major **semitone** <term>semitonium</term> of the first **whole tone** <term>tonus</term> <note>A whole tone is a musical interval consisting of two semitones; in Pythagorean tuning, these semitones are not equal in size</note> sitting between the **proslambanomenos** <note>The lowest note of the ancient Greek musical system, roughly equivalent to a modern 'A'</note> and the **hypate hypaton** <note>The second fixed note of the scale, equivalent to a modern 'B'</note>. Since this small voice <note>A "voice" or "vocula" here refers to a specific musical pitch or note</note> is removed at will and replaced at will, because it does not naturally fit in the arrangement of the **consonances** <term>consonantiae</term> <note>The primary, pleasing intervals like the octave or fifth</note>, it has obtained no suitable name. Therefore, we shall call it a **little key** <term>clavicula</term> <note>original: "clauicula"; likely referring to a movable marker or a specific accidental like B-flat</note>, naming others similar to it with comparable terms.

Therefore, this first whole tone is divided into two unequal parts. By a correct measurement having been calculated, the **minor semitone** <term>semitonium minus</term> sounds in the smaller part, and in the larger part sounds the **apothome** <term>apothome</term> <note>A Greek term meaning "the remainder" or "cutting off," used to describe the major semitone in Pythagorean tuning</note> or **major semitone** <term>semitonium maius</term>, which is discordant by itself and suited for nothing. The minor semitone, on the contrary, is the beauty and sweetness of **harmony** <term>armonia</term>; these semitones, when joined together, always complete a whole tone.

Therefore, that ninth step—formed from the immovable, fixed, and natural semitone which occurs between the second and third voice, and from the larger part (namely, the major semitone of the divided first whole tone)—has created a whole tone. Thus, it must not be denied that that fixed and immovable semitone is the minor one, which, when the larger part of the tone is added to it, completes the whole tone.

Two things are therefore inferred from these: namely, that a whole tone is divided by a major and minor semitone, just like the divisible **sesquioctave proportion** <term>proportio sesquioctava</term> <note>The mathematical ratio 9:8, which defines a whole tone in the Pythagorean system</note>, and that those minor semitones are primary, to which the larger ones are joined.

Some call these "fictitious voices" <note>original: "fictas uoces"; in medieval music, these are notes outside the standard hand-chart or "gamut," what we today call accidentals</note>, while others commonly call them **flats** <note>original: "bmoles"; literally "soft Bs," the origin of the flat symbol (♭)</note>. 

However, some call them **semitones** <term>semitonia</term> more truly and more properly, since they do nothing else but divide individual whole tones into two unequal parts. If you have measured these correctly and do not find one part to be larger than the other (or conversely, one smaller), you should consider that monochord to have been incorrectly partitioned. 

Now the careful reader might ask why these little keys—which are rightly called semitones since they produce sounds—are not included in the arrangement of the fifteen voices <note>The "Greater Perfect System" of ancient Greek music, which consisted of fifteen primary notes</note> mentioned above, and why they are not called by their own suitable names or brought forth in the natural sounding of the **consonances** <term>consonantiae</term> <note>Harmonious intervals like the octave, fifth, and fourth</note>. 

That would indeed be too intricate and superfluous, especially since a human naturally possesses a free and unimpeded voice. For nature itself dictates that in the singing of melodies in the **diatonic genus** <term>genus diatonicum</term> <note>The standard arrangement of whole tones and semitones that forms the basis of Western scales</note>, nothing smaller than a **minor semitone** <term>semitonium minus</term> can be produced by the human voice. Nature has established that only the minor semitone itself should be sung, rejecting the **apothome** <term>apothome</term> or **major semitone** <term>semitonium maius</term> because of the excessive dissonance it creates when singing. Therefore, the **minor semitone** <term>semitonium minus</term>, which on account of its sweetness is fitted into consonances to be sung by the **human voice** <note>original: "hūana uoce"</note>, has been retained by musicians with the highest veneration. It is established in the natural arrangement of voices either before, after, or between two whole tones. 

With nature as a guide, this semitone then completes a perfect **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>A musical interval spanning four notes of the scale</note> in each of the four-voice arrangements; this consonance must always be maintained within four sounds, as was sufficiently stated in the second chapter. Minor semitones are added and removed at will.
</margin>

But in harmonic composition, these are not at all necessary because of their very small proportion, as was previously stated. For they are "movable" semitones because they are added at will and can be removed at will. Although they do not occur in harmonic arrangement except in the tempering of the **tritone** <term>tritonus</term> <note>The "augmented fourth," an interval of three whole tones famously known as the "Devil in Music" for its perceived harshness</note>, which was touched upon more broadly in the first chapter. 

**Nor, if he had been able to escape the harshness of the tritone itself in any other way**, would the musician have fixed that little key between the **mese** <note>The "middle" note in the Greek system, roughly equivalent to A</note> and the **paramese** <note>The "next-to-middle" note, equivalent to B. <meta>This page continues the technical discussion of dividing the whole tone into major and minor semitones. It concludes the argument that the minor semitone must precede the major semitone in the division of a tone to maintain musical harmony and the integrity of the tetrachord system.</meta>

...to proceed in that dimension and persist in the lower part of the tone; this is necessary for at least three reasons. 

For first, as an argument taken from the **tritone** <term>tritonus</term> itself: if one proceeds from the note **Mese** <term>mese</term> <note>The central "middle" note in the ancient Greek musical system used by medieval theorists</note> to the **Trite Synemmenon** <term>trite synemmenon</term> <note>The fourth note of the "disjunct" tetrachord, often used to avoid the harsh tritone interval</note> where the whole tone is cut to temper the tritone, [resulting in] a **major semitone** <term>semitonium maius</term> or **apothome** <term>apothome</term> <note>original: "apothome"; the larger, mathematically derived portion of a whole tone, which sounds slightly "off" to the ear if used as a melodic step</note>, will the latest error not be worse than the first? Truly, an apothome joined with two whole tones sounds more dissonant than a melody of three whole tones succeeding one another. 

Furthermore, if the **minor semitone** <term>semitonium minus</term> did not come first in this division of the whole tone, the **synemmenon tetrachord** <term>tetrachordum synemmenon</term> <note>A specific grouping of four notes—specifically B-flat, C, D, and E—used to soften the scale</note> would either not have been discovered at all, or it would begin from a most terrible discord, which could in no way be tolerated. Indeed, it was fitting for it to proceed by a minor semitone, then a whole tone, and another whole tone, just as the other tetrachords proceed. 

Lastly, if in this kind of division of the tone the minor semitone did not come first, who does not see that where a minor semitone is naturally fixed, two minor semitones would succeed each other? The first, of course, would be the "movable" one from the divided preceding tone, and the second would be this fixed one. These are not only difficult to pronounce <note>The author uses "pronounce" to mean "sing" or "execute" the pitch</note>, but what is worse, when joined together they do not complete a whole tone. 

For example, if you place the compass on the second measurement of the string or the **monochord** <term>monochordum</term> voice, traversing the whole string in nine equal steps, the first step divides the whole tone between the third and fourth voice into a major and minor semitone, and if you proceed further in this way...

<summary>This page provides three mathematical and aesthetic arguments for why a whole tone must be divided starting with a minor semitone, specifically to avoid harsh dissonances like the tritone and to maintain the structure of the synemmenon tetrachord.</summary>

### Chapter Six: Concerning the arrangement of sounds through Latin letters and strings

<margin>
Latin musicians, in order to pass down sounds in imitation of the Greeks—by whom those fifteen strings were invented and measured—arranged seven Latin letters to be repeated up to fifteen. Where the Greeks have the **lowest note** <term>proslambanomene</term> <note>original: "proslambanomene"; the lowest note of the Greek Greater Perfect System.</note>, they placed **A**; where the Greeks have the **principal of the principals** <term>hypatehypato</term> <note>original: "hypatehypato"; the second-lowest note in the Greek system.</note>, there they placed **b**, and so on. Later, prudent musicians added one **four-note scale** <term>tetrachord</term> <note>original: "tetracordum"; a series of four notes with specific intervals.</note> to those fifteen strings so that the first four letters were doubled. Furthermore, they represented the first seven notes, which they called **low** <term>graves</term>, with capital letters like **A B C D**; others with a smaller form like **a b c d**, and these they called **high** <term>acutas</term>; the three added thereafter, which they called **highest** <term>superacutas</term>, they distinguished by certain little strokes like <term>aa</term> <term>bb</term> <term>cc</term> <term>dd</term>.
</margin>

->[Circular library stamp: National Central Library of Florence]<-

Latin musicians, following in the footsteps of the Greek elders, constructed the division of sounds in the same order as those fifteen strings were invented and measured by the elders themselves. For they fixed seven letters of the alphabet on the monochord, repeating them and resuming from the first so that there would be fifteen, arranged as follows for the voices: The first letter, namely **A**, they situated in the lower place where the **lowest note** <term>proslambanomenos</term> <note>original: "proslambanomenos"</note> was; the second, namely **b**, where the **principal of the principals** <term>hypatehypaton</term> <note>original: "hypatehypatõ"</note> was; the third, namely **c**, on the third string; the fourth, namely **d**, on the fourth string; the fifth letter, **E**, on the fifth string; the sixth, **F**, on the sixth string; and the seventh, **G**, on the seventh string. Likewise, they placed the first on the eighth string, **A**; the second on the ninth, **b**; the third on the tenth, **C**; the fourth on the eleventh, **d**; the fifth on the twelfth, **E**; the sixth on the thirteenth, **F**; and the seventh on the fourteenth, **G**. Then they placed the first again on the fifteenth, **A**. Afterward, prudent musicians [added] to these fifteen letters a **four-note scale** <term>tetrachord</term> <note>original: "tetracordũ"</note> described with letters in a higher place with its own letter...

<insert>
<margin>+</margin> ...so that they might have a ten-fold account in these letters, they added one formed with double strokes. And so that a **disjunct tetrachord** <term>tetracordum disiunctum</term> might be made (afterward, in the lower part, the letter **Gamma** <term>Γ gama</term> <note>The Greek letter G, used by medieval theorists to represent the note G below low A.</note> was added by the **Blessed Gregory** <note>Referring to Pope Gregory the Great, though the letter Gamma was historically added to the system by later theorists like Guido of Arezzo.</note>, since we received music from the Greeks). Thus all letters ought to be written in the **introductory guide** <term>introductorio</term> which **Guido of Arezzo** <note>original: "guido aretinus"; a medieval music theorist (c. 991–1033) who developed the musical staff and solmization.</note> constructed by coordinating the syllables he invented. They pronounced these sounds or chants as the Greeks and Hebrews do, which is praised by Guido and easily shown to be done... the order of the voices of the semitones which they had arranged on their strings to avoid the **tritone** <term>tritono</term> <note>An interval of three whole tones, famously dissonant.</note> becoming a **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"; the interval of a perfect fourth.</note>.
</insert>

<summary>This page describes the transition from the Greek musical system to the Latin letter-based system (A through G) on the monochord. It explains the hierarchy of low, high, and highest notes and credits historical figures like Pope Gregory and Guido of Arezzo with the development of Western musical notation.</summary>

<keywords>monochord, tetrachord, Guido of Arezzo, musical notation, Greek music theory, Latin alphabet, Gamma, diapason, semitone, pitch hierarchy</keywords>

--- Page 201 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Latin musical notation system, which was adapted from earlier Greek models. It specifically details how the letter names for notes were written to indicate different octaves.</meta>

...which had been tripled, they bound together, noting that many men reach the same limit of high pitch through vocal **modulation** <term>modulatione</term> <note>original: "modulatōe"; the inflection or melodic movement of the voice in a scale.</note>. They demonstrated this **four-note scale** <term>tetrachord</term> <note>original: "tetracordū"; a series of four notes that formed the building block of ancient musical scales.</note> with these letters: **A B C D**. Therefore, in this order, the first four letters of the alphabet were tripled, while the remaining three were left with only a double impression.

Because the first seven letters were positioned on the lower strings and produced lower sounds, musicians drew them with a larger form <note>meaning they used capital letters.</note>. Those letters used in the following sequence on the higher strings were marked with a smaller script to distinguish them from the others. Finally, the last ones added at the end—since they surpassed those seven higher strings in pitch—were marked with certain doubled strokes <note>original: "tractulis geminatis"; likely referring to prime marks or doubled letters used to indicate the highest octave.</note> to distinguish them from the letters fixed on the lower and higher strings. Here is the description of them all:

->**A B C D E F G**<-
->**a b c d e f**<-
->**g' α' β' ε' δ'**<-
<note>The list shows the transition from capital letters (low) to lowercase (high) to lowercase with marks and Greek letters (super-high). The Greek letters alpha, beta, epsilon, and delta are used here as symbols for the highest pitches.</note>

Thus, they called the first seven **low** <term>graves</term>, the next ones **high** <term>acutas</term>, and the last four **super-high** <term>supacutas</term>.

<margin>
Before the arrangement for pronouncing sounds with the aforementioned letters, they expressed them using certain **ciphers** <term>zyphris</term> or characters that were extremely difficult to utter, which have since fallen out of use.
</margin>

Indeed, before this arrangement of letters was invented, the origins, properties, lows, and highs of sounds were expressed with certain very difficult **ciphers** <term>ciphris</term> or characters. This method of performance, because of the laborious difficulty of its delivery, was cast aside and abandoned by musicians themselves, and for that reason, it has passed away.

<summary>This page explains the medieval system of using Latin letters (A-G) to represent musical notes, detailing how capital, lowercase, and marked letters indicate different octaves. It also notes that this system replaced an earlier, much more difficult method of notation involving complex ciphers.</summary>
<keywords>musical notation, tetrachord, graves, acutas, supacutas, ciphers, monochord, pitch, Latin letters</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical notation using letters of the alphabet to denote pitch, specifically moving from the physical measurement of strings (the monochord) to the practical application of singing and historical precedents from authorities like Guido of Arezzo.</meta>

...from the practice about which we have indeed treated more broadly, concerning the more difficult method of singing, in the fifth book of **The Flower of Music** 
<margin>
Another work by Franchinus titled **The Flower of Music**.
</margin>
which we wrote as a guide directed to the Illustrious Marquis; and about this, Johannes Carthusiensis <note>Johannes de Mantua, a 15th-century Carthusian monk and music theorist.</note> has revealed many things extensively in his own work. Therefore, the skill of the early musicians established the easiest way and method of the seven letters to be pronounced and observed. For both the Greeks and the Hebrews were accustomed to setting forth the first seven letters of their alphabets, arranged in this order, with no difficulty in the dictation of melodies <term>modulis</term> <note>original: "mōulis"; refers to the measurement or melodic structure of a song.</note>. These we clearly proved with correct **melodic movement** <term>modulatione</term> in the first part of the aforementioned fifth book of *The Flower of Music*. 

Therefore, the melodic movement established by letters was easy, as that most famous musician **Guido of Arezzo** <note>The 11th-century monk credited with inventing the staff notation we use today.</note> testifies, writing thus in a certain letter: 
"We have proven this best with the letters of the notes alone, by which nothing is easier for learning a song, provided it is practiced for at least three months." 
<note>original: "Solis notaꝝ lrīs optīꝫ pbauimꝫ q̄bꝫ ad diſcēdū cātū nihil ē facilius ſi freq̄tate fuerit ſaltē tribꝫ mēſibꝫ." This is a famous claim by Guido regarding the efficiency of his pedagogical system.</note> 

Indeed, the same order of **whole tones** and **semitones** is preserved in these letters as was measured on the strings above. For between **A** and **B** they placed a whole tone; between **B** and **C** a **minor semitone** <note>In early music theory, a semitone (half-step) was not always equal; the 'minor' or 'limma' was the smaller variety found in Pythagorean tuning.</note>; between **C** and **D** a whole tone; between **D** and **E** a whole tone; between **E** and **F** a minor semitone; between **F** and **G** a whole tone; between **G** and **A** a whole tone; and between **a** and **b** a whole tone. And there, they sometimes divided the whole tone into two unequal semitones to avoid the **tritone** <note>original: "trittōnū"; an interval of three whole tones (like F to B) that was considered harsh and unstable—the "devil in music"—and was often corrected by flattening the B.</note>, as was clearly shown above. Likewise, they had a minor semitone between **b** and **c**, and in the rest they also kept the same order and progression which was set in the strings arranged above.

<summary>Franchinus explains the historical and practical benefits of using a seven-letter notation system (A-G), citing the efficiency of Guido of Arezzo's methods and detailing the specific intervals of whole tones and semitones between notes.</summary>
<keywords>Franchinus Gaffurius, Guido of Arezzo, music theory, whole tones, semitones, tritone, notation, Flower of Music, Johannes Carthusiensis</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of musical notation, moving from the historical use of the seven-letter alphabet to a specific system of mapping vowels to pitches, allowing any written text to be converted into a melody.</meta>

<margin>
Note.

It is a marvel that if we sing that which we read and which delights us, it will provide us with an even greater delight.
</margin>

Philosophers are proven to observe that here, in these very letters, a method exists such that in every combination of four letters, the consonance of a **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"; the musical interval of a perfect fourth, which was considered one of the fundamental mathematical harmonies in medieval music theory.</note> always happens to occur. For this reason, we by no means doubt that everything which is said can be written, and whatever is written can be reduced to song. Therefore, since that which we read delights us, if we sing that very same thing, it will add a greater delight, because the discipline of **music** <term>musices</term> <note>original: "musices"; a Latinized Greek term for the art and science of music.</note> is naturally delightful, as was shown in the first part of the first book by many authorities and examples. 

For every word in grammar and common speech—indeed, what is more, every syllable—is completed by at least one **vowel** <term>uocali</term>, and cannot be held without a vowel. There are, indeed, five vowels by which every syllable and word is thoroughly distinguished. To demonstrate that everything spoken or written can be sung, we subjoin to each of these arranged letters <note>These letters (A, B, C, etc.) represent the "gamut," the standard range of musical pitches used in the Middle Ages and Renaissance.</note> those pitches or strings <term>cordas</term> that sound. And since there are only five vowels, yet many sounds or letters—namely eighteen—we shall repeat those vowels several times, so that each letter or pitch contains those five in this manner:

**A B C D E F G a b c d e f g Γ A B E**

| A | B | C | D | E | F | G | a | b | c | d | e | f | g | Γ | A | B | E |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| a | e | i | o | u | a | e | i | o | u | a | e | i | o | u | a | e | i |
| o | u | a | e | i | o | u | a | e | i | o | u | a | e | i | o | u | a |
| e | i | o | u | a | e | i | o | u | a | e | i | o | u | a | e | i | o |
| u | a | e | i | o | u | a | e | i | o | u | a | e | i | o | u | a | e |
| i | o | u | a | e | i | o | u | a | e | i | o | u | a | e | i | o | u |

<summary>This page describes a method for turning any written or spoken text into music by assigning vowels (a, e, i, o, u) to specific musical pitches across an eighteen-note range. The author argues that singing a text increases the listener's delight and highlights the mathematical perfection of musical intervals like the perfect fourth within this system.</summary>

->**♮, e, d, a** <note>These characters are shown with double strokes above them, indicating they belong to the "superacute" or highest octave of the medieval scale.</note><-

Indeed, this letter surpassed the eighteenth letter nearest to it by a whole tone in height.

<margin>Blessed Gregory added the letter Γ (Gamma); many say it was Guido.</margin>

For that letter [Gamma] is surpassed in height by a tone by the letter which was previously the first [the letter A]. 

But since the beginning and origins of musical consonance, as well as the division of the whole string or **monochord** <term>monocordum</term> <note>original: "monocordí"; a single-stringed instrument used by theorists to measure musical intervals mathematically.</note>, were originally established in the first letter of the Latin alphabet—namely **A**, which had been placed in the lower register of the "philosophical strings"—this same order has always been celebrated up to these times. Thus, in that very **A**, which resounded as the **proslambanomenos** <term>proslambanomenos</term> <note>original: "proslambanomenos"; a Greek term meaning "the acquired note," the lowest note in the ancient Greek Greater Perfect System.</note>, the beginning of the types of consonances was taken. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the musical scale's development, specifically explaining how the Greek letter Gamma (representing the low G) integrates into the system of twenty notes and introduces the story of how Guido d'Arezzo derived the six solmization syllables from a liturgical hymn.</meta>

...ma <note>original: "ma", the second syllable of "Gamma".</note> indeed sounds in Greek, but it indicates the letter **G** in Latin. Therefore, with these matters thus arranged, there were twenty **notes** <term>cordis</term> <note>original: "cordis", literally "strings," referring here to the distinct pitches of the medieval musical scale.</note> distinguished by seven letters, which marked the seven differences of pitches. When musicians had practiced this method of singing for a long time, the nature of reality prompted them toward a skillful investigation. Through long practice, they searched for a way to more easily reveal, using fewer steps, those things which were occurring and were very difficult to perceive and understand through many complex means. And so, as many write, while the monk **Guido** <note>Guido d'Arezzo (c. 991–1033), the monk credited with inventing the musical staff and the "do-re-mi" system.</note> was passing slowly through the cloister of the monastery, the voices of the monks singing the hymn of **Saint John the Baptist** <note>Saint John was the patron saint of singers; the hymn mentioned is "Ut queant laxis."</note> reached his ears—namely:
*So that your servants may, with loosened voices, resound...* <note>original: "ut queant laxis resonare fibris"; the first line of the hymn to St. John.</note> etc.
As they were singing, he was led in a certain way by a divine spirit to approach the melody. Through varied and manifold examination, he weighed the fact that the first six syllables of the six phrases of the first verse of that same hymn differed from one another in height and depth of pitch. He found that each syllable was separated from its neighbor by either a whole tone or a minor semitone by natural arrangement. He judged that the species of the **fourth** <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"; a musical interval spanning four notes.</note> corresponded in four sounds to each other in the correct order. For those six syllables are:
**ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la**
which the aforementioned Guido afterwards [applied] to all harmony for...

<summary>This page describes how Guido d'Arezzo observed the rising pitches in the "Hymn to Saint John the Baptist" to create the six-note solmization system (ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la). It explains his goal of simplifying musical instruction, moving away from complex systems to a more intuitive method of perceiving intervals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of Guido d'Arezzo's hexachord system, picking up from the discovery that the syllables of the hymn to St. John the Baptist correspond to the rising pitches of a musical scale.</meta>

...decided it should be brought forth, for they consist of all the grammatical vowels. Since there are only five vowels, yet there are six syllables, the first vowel is repeated in these syllables—namely, in the last one which is called **la**. Thus, just as letters were previously expressed in melodic voices, those who came after established these six syllables for use. For we should not judge the pronunciation of syllables to be more difficult than that of letters, since a syllable in melodic pronunciation <note>the act of singing a note to a specific syllable</note> retains the same short and long duration as a vowel or letter. That which is demonstrated in letters and vowels regarding height <note>original: "acuminis", referring to high pitch</note> and depth <note>original: "gravitatis", referring to low pitch</note> is also expressed in syllables.

For between this first syllable <note>Ut</note> and the second <note>Re</note> there is a **tone** <term>tonus</term> <note>a whole step in music</note>, just as there is between the first note—the Greek letter <note>Gamma, the low G</note> added to the system—and the second note <note>A</note>. Likewise, between the second syllable and the third <note>Mi</note>, there is a full tone, just as between **A** and **B**. 

But between the third and fourth syllable <note>Fa</note>, there is a **minor semitone** <term>semitonium minus</term> <note>a half step, the smallest interval in this system</note>, just as between **B** and **C**. Indeed, the fourth syllable leads to the fifth <note>Sol</note> by a tone; likewise, the fifth to the sixth <note>La</note> by a tone. 

And therefore, he <note>Guido d'Arezzo</note> established that whatever might be singable should be described and expressed by the voices with these six syllables. And so that what we are discussing may shine more clearly, let us subjoin the arrangement of the first verse of that hymn, formed with those same syllables. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the musical system attributed to Guido d'Arezzo, specifically focusing on the placement of semitones and the naming of notes using syllables.</meta>

<margin>
A <term>semitone|The smallest interval in medieval music, roughly half of a whole tone.</term> is rather placed between *mi* and *fa* because they correspond to each other with a certain softness and weak sweetness, which is always fixed as one and the same everywhere.
</margin>

Therefore, <note>Guido d'Arezzo (c. 991–1033), a monk and music theorist who invented the staff notation and the solmization system we still use today (do-re-mi).</note> **Guido** himself, perceiving very well and conveniently that the **minor semitone** is naturally placed between *mi* and *fa*—which correspond to each other with a certain softness and weak sweetness—established that this minor semitone is indeed always fixed as one and the same, whether it be established in the lower or in the higher strings <note>In this context, "strings" refers to pitches or positions on the monochord.</note>. In a wonderful order, he established that *mi* and *fa* should always be preferred where a natural semitone occurred. Indeed, in the previously mentioned arrangement of letters, a minor semitone always occurred between **B and C** and between **E and F**, and sometimes between **A and B-flat** <note>original: "a & b. f."</note> where a <term>tritone|An unstable, dissonant interval of three whole tones (like F to B-natural), often called "the devil in music."</term> was being tempered. It occurred naturally so that, although it arose in diverse strings and places, it was always one and the same.

Therefore, all minor semitones occurring naturally in chants are more easily and aptly brought forth with a single pronunciation <note>referring to the use of the same syllables *mi* and *fa* for any semitone, regardless of the actual notes.</note>. Since they are diverse, they are always similar, as has been said, nor do they differ from each other in being larger or smaller. For this reason, he coupled these syllables with the letters first established in this order: In the first, lower Greek letter, he established the first syllable, as here: **Γ-ut** <note>Gamma-ut: the lowest note of the medieval system.</note>. In the second letter, the second syllable, in this way: **A-re**. In the third letter, the third syllable, as here: **B-mi**. 

And note that in this kind of introductory arrangement, wherever this syllable *mi* happens in the natural order on the letter **B**, then that letter is written as a "square" <note>B-quadratum (♮), the origin of the "natural" and "sharp" signs.</note> to distinguish it from the "round" **B** <note>b-rotundum (♭), the origin of the "flat" sign.</note>, which is shaped when we couple the syllable *fa* to that same letter B...

For the gathering of all these six syllables is called a **deduction** <note>original: "deductio." A **deduction** is perfected and completed by these six syllables, and from them, three species of <term>diatessaron|A perfect fourth; an interval spanning four notes of the scale.</term> are fulfilled, as was touched upon above in the second chapter.

In the fourth Latin letter [D], which holds the fifth place in the scale, he placed the fifth syllable, namely *sol*. Likewise, he placed the second syllable, namely *re*, which relates to the first syllable (*ut*) recently fixed in the preceding chapter as if to a foundation, in this way: <insert>D-sol-re</insert>.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the medieval gamut (the scale), specifically focusing on the pitch B. It explains the distinction between B-natural (square B) and B-flat (round B), which were necessary to avoid the dissonant tritone interval.</meta>

The third syllable of the fourth **deduction** <note>original: "deductio"; the sequence of six notes—ut, re, mi, fa, sol, la—used to navigate the scale.</note> is found at a distance from the ninth string—its nearest neighbor—by a whole tone in height; it is represented in this way: **B-mi**. But between this tenth and ninth string, the letter **round b** <note>original: "b littera rotunda"; our modern flat symbol (♭) derives from this "rounded" shape.</note> is placed with the fourth syllable of the fourth deduction, namely *fa*. This note is a **minor semitone** <note>original: "minor semitonio"</note> distant in height from the ninth string, and is surpassed by the tenth string by a **major semitone**, that is, an **apothome** <term>apothome|In Pythagorean music theory, the remainder of a whole tone after a minor semitone has been subtracted. It is slightly larger than a standard semitone.</term>. For this syllable—namely the *fa* of the third deduction—divides the whole tone drawn between the ninth and tenth strings into a minor and major semitone, thereby softening the **tritone** <note>original: "trittonum moliendo"; by using B-flat instead of B-natural, the harsh four-note interval between F and B is avoided.</note>.

It is called **square b** <note>original: "b quadra"; the ancestor of our modern natural (♮) and sharp (♯) symbols.</note> from its shape, and **round b**. Because this round b governs the syllable it holds—namely *fa*—and establishes a sweeter sound by a minor semitone relative to its lower neighbor, it has received the name **soft b** <note>original: "b mollis"; the root of the French word "bémol" and German "Moll".</note>. The other one, conversely, demonstrates the syllable it contains—namely *mi*—as harder and sharper, producing a whole tone relative to the lower pitch near it; thus, by the opposite quality of the other, it deservedly earned the name **hard b** <note>original: "b dura"; the root of the German word for major, "Dur".</note>. 

Therefore, although these two letters share one and the same name, and even though they introduce a different shape, quality, and sound, we shall treat them in this numbered arrangement of strings as a single string producing, as it were, a single sound. Therefore, in the third...

<summary>This page explains the dual nature of the note B in the medieval system, distinguishing between "hard B" (B-natural) and "soft B" (B-flat). It describes how B-flat is used to divide a whole tone into semitones to avoid the dissonant tritone interval.</summary>
<keywords>B-flat, B-natural, soft B, hard B, apothome, minor semitone, tritone, hexachord, gamut, medieval music theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical notation and theory, moving from the basic structure of pitches to the practical application of the Guidonian Hand and solmization.</meta>

In this arrangement of letters and syllables, everyone can understand for themselves that the same sequence of tones and semitones is arranged as we have shown to exist in the fifteen strings of the lyre <note>original: "quindecim prophoris chordis"; a reference to the classical Greek Greater Perfect System of 15 pitches described by Boethius</note>. It is also evident that there are seven "deductions" <note>original: "deductiones"; these are hexachords, or six-note scales, which were the building blocks of medieval melody</note> by which this entire order is woven together. By some, it is called the **Hand** <note>the Guidonian Hand, a mnemonic tool where each joint of the hand represents a specific pitch</note>; hence it is that it is woven upon the left hand.

Indeed, since this constitution of letters and syllables is the first and easiest introduction to the discipline of music, we have rightly thought it should be called an "Introductory." In this, if you wish to establish more strings in the lower part—that is, in the deeper register—you can descend by repeating both the letters and the syllables in the same order, fixing <term>G-sol-re-ut</term> <note>a compound name for the pitch G, combining its letter name with its positions in different hexachords</note> where the <term>Gamma-ut</term> <note>the lowest note of the medieval system, written as the Greek letter Gamma</note> is placed; below which you will substitute **F-fa-ut**, then **E-la-mi** for it, and thus you will repeat the others by joining them in sequence.

Likewise, in the higher part, you can add as many letters and syllables as you wish by placing **E-la-mi** in the highest extreme where **E-la** is pronounced, and above it **F-fa-ut**, then **G-sol-re-ut**, then **A-la-mi-re**, and the rest in the same order.

Furthermore, it must be noted that although these syllables are uttered in the introduction of a song, they are not actually noted down nor written. Instead, their names and locations are distinguished by certain full square notes <note>the 'punctum' or 'virga' used in chant notation</note> as well as oblique ones in <term>plainchant</term> <note>original: "cantus planus"; traditional, unmeasured church music like Gregorian chant</note>, and by various notes and figures in <term>measured music</term> <note>original: "cantus mensurabilis"; music where the shape of the note dictates its specific rhythmic duration</note>. This happens because if the syllables themselves were [placed] above...

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the Guidonian system, transitioning from the expansion of the pitch range to the theoretical classification of hexachords—the six-note scales used to teach singing. The author, Franchinus Gaffurius, cites his own previous works and the authority of Marchetto da Padova.</meta>

If one were to write out letters and syllables just like the texts of antiphons <note>original: "antiphōas"; a short chant sung as a refrain</note> and other pieces to be delivered and sung, it would introduce the greatest confusion, and they would be difficult to distinguish from the syllables of the antiphons themselves—especially when the chant descends to the very line where the antiphons are written. Likewise, syllables are printed and noted with greater difficulty than musical notes.

There are, therefore, three **properties** <term>proprietas</term> <note>a "property" defines which type of hexachord is being used based on the position of the semitone</note> of these **deductions** <term>deductiones</term> <note>hexachords, or six-note scales</note>: namely, **Natural**, **Square B** <note>original: "b quadra" or ♮; also called "Hard B"</note>, and **Soft B** <note>original: "b mollis" or ♭</note>. I have treated these extensively in the fifth book of *The Flower of Music* <note>original: "floris musices"; referring to Gaffurius's *Theoricum opus musice discipline* (1480) or a related draft</note>, chapter eight, and in the *Book of Dialogues* which I addressed to Carlo Pallavicino, Bishop of Lodi, near the end of the third dialogue.

<margin>
Another work by Franchinus, i.e., the *Book of Dialogues*.
</margin>

Now, regarding these, this must be briefly understood: every "deduction" whose beginning and head—namely the syllable **ut**—is fixed on the letter **C**, is said to be of the **Natural** property. Indeed, every deduction having its beginning on **G** is said to be of the property of **Hard B** <note>original: "b duralis"</note> or **Square B**. Likewise, every deduction having its foundation and beginning on **F** is said to be of the property of **Soft B** <note>original: "b mollaris"</note> or **Round B**.

For this reason, according to **Marchetto** <note>Marchetto da Padova, a highly influential 14th-century music theorist</note>, a property in song is the derivation of several voices from one and the same beginning. By others, however, it is defined thus: a property is the essential or accidental acceptance of voices established in a low or high order. For we know that notes are assigned for syllables, and syllables for voices <note>original: "uocibus"; here meaning the actual musical pitches or sounds</note>; because voices themselves cannot be written down, but are instead committed to memory so they do not perish. From this...
