### On the species of the fourth and the fifth. Chapter 7.

The consonance of the fourth <term>diatessaron</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"; the interval between the first and fourth notes of a scale</note> is made of four sounds constituting two tones and one minor semitone, as was said before in the second chapter.
<margin>On the fourth</margin>
Indeed, it is named from *dia*, which means "of," and *tessaron*, "four," as if it were made from four voices. For "world music" <term>musica mundana</term> <note>a concept from the philosopher Boethius describing the mathematical harmony of the universe, the planets, and the seasons</note> is usually compared to this interval because it is completed from the four elements <note>Earth, Air, Fire, and Water</note>. For the parts of the number four <term>quaternarius</term> <note>original: "quaternarii"</note>, compared to one another, lead to the other consonances and, joined together, they fulfill the number ten <note>1+2+3+4=10; this reflects the Pythagorean "Tetractys," where the first four numbers represent the foundation of all reality and musical harmony</note>, by which the whole multitude of numbers increases. Let us therefore arrange this number four, naturally beginning from unity, in this way: 1, 2, 3, 4. For the number two <term>binarius</term> in relation to unity <term>unitatem</term> leads to the double proportion, and therefore the consonance of the octave <term>diapason</term> <note>original: "diapason"; a 2:1 ratio</note>; the number three <term>ternarius</term> in relation to the number two is a "one-and-a-half" proportion <note>original: "sexqualtera" or sesquialtera; a 3:2 ratio</note> and the consonance of the fifth <term>diapente</term> <note>original: "diapente"</note>; the number four <term>quaternarius</term> in relation to the number three is a "one-and-a-third" proportion <note>original: "sexqtertia" or sesquitertia; a 4:3 ratio</note>, here the consonance of the fourth <term>diatessaron</term>. Likewise, four in relation to two is a double proportion, and therefore the octave. Again, four in relation to unity is a double-double or quadruple proportion from

...which makes the symphony of the double octave <term>bis diapason</term> <note>original: "bis diapason siphonia"; an interval of two octaves, which the Pythagoreans linked to the number four through the ratio 4:1</note>. This page introduces the third species and explores how these intervals can be subdivided.</meta>

For between *re* and *mi* is one interval of a whole tone; between *mi* and *fa* is the second, namely a minor semitone <note>original: "semitonium minoris"; in medieval theory, the semitone was not exactly half a tone, but a "minor" or "smaller" interval</note>; and between *fa* and *sol* is the third, by which a whole tone is completed. 

Sometimes it consists of two intervals, as when saying *re-mi-sol* or *re-fa-sol*. For between *re* and *mi* is the first interval, which makes a whole tone, but between *mi* and *sol* is the second, which sounds as a <term>semidittone</term> <note>original: "semidittonus"; what we now call a minor third</note>, which is to say a whole tone and a semitone. For it is called "semidittone" from *semi*, which means half, and *tone*—as if it were an imperfect <term>dittone</term> <note>original: "dittonus"; a major third, literally "two tones"</note>, which is two tones joined together but not full. For a "dittone" is named from *dia*, which means "two," and *tone*. 

Likewise, between *re* and *fa* is one interval of a semidittone, and between *fa* and *sol* is one interval of a full whole tone. Therefore, however many intervals the fourth consists of, it must contain two whole tones and one minor semitone. Again, it may consist of only one interval <note>A melodic leap</note>, such as when *re-sol* is voiced; and let what is said here be understood as applying to this first species.

<summary>This page defines the third species of the fourth (ut-re-mi-fa) and explains how the interval of a fourth is always composed of two whole tones and one semitone, regardless of how the internal notes are skipped or grouped.</summary>

<meta>Shelfmark: Magl. A.6.36. This page continues the discussion of musical intervals, moving from the species of the fourth (diatessaron) to the species of the fifth (diapente). It establishes a rule for the relationship between the number of notes in a consonance and the number of "species" (variations of tone/semitone patterns) it contains.</meta>

Therefore, regarding the other species of the fourth <term>diatessaron</term> <note>the interval between the first and fourth notes of a scale</note>: for two things seem to be proven from these. First, that every consonance—whether a fourth, a fifth <term>diapente</term>, or an octave <term>diapason</term>—has one fewer species than it has voices <note>original: "uoces"; referring here to the number of distinct pitches/notes within the interval</note>. Second, that every consonance can have at most as many intervals as it has species, or one fewer interval than it has voices. This occurs when the consonance includes all its voices, about which the diligent researcher can seek a true judgment for himself. 

But concerning these species of the fourth, more has been treated above in the second chapter. The fifth <term>diapente</term> <note>the interval of a perfect fifth</note>, however, is a consonance made of five sounds containing three whole tones and one minor semitone. For it is named from *dia*, which means "from" <note>original: "de"</note>, and *penta*, meaning "five"—as if it were a consonance mixed from five voices. Indeed, it consists in the number three <term>ternario</term>, which represents a perfect division in musical matters; for in that number there exists a beginning, a middle, and an end in an equal arrangement. 

Indeed, the fifth is formed from a fourth and an added whole tone. Since it has only five voices, it must necessarily retain four species. For the first species of the fifth is formed from the first species of the fourth and a whole tone added in the higher <note>original: "acutiori"</note> part, proceeding by these syllables: **re, mi, fa, sol, la** ascending <note>original: "intensiue"</note>. It begins, indeed, in D-sol-re, where the fourth string of the philosophers used to reside, and it ends in A-la-mi-re, where the <term>mese</term> <note>the central note of the Ancient Greek Greater Perfect System, used as a reference point for other intervals</note> was placed. Nor should it be surprising that the first species of the consonance of the fifth...

<summary>This page establishes a general rule that musical intervals have one fewer "species" (variations) than they have notes, then introduces the perfect fifth (diapente). It defines the fifth as a combination of a fourth plus a whole tone, specifically describing the first species (re-mi-fa-sol-la).</summary>
<keywords>diatessaron, diapente, species of the fifth, hexachords, D-sol-re, A-la-mi-re, mese, music theory, intervals</keywords>

--- Page 221 ---
<meta>Shelfmark: Magl. A.6.36. This page continues the detailed technical explanation of how musical intervals are constructed. Having previously defined the "fifth" (diapente) as a combination of a "fourth" (diatessaron) and a whole tone, the author now addresses the specific starting pitches (the "species") for these intervals within the medieval gamut, specifically why the first species must begin on the note D.</meta>

<margin>
Why the first species of the <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapetes", a scribal error for "diapente"</note> begins in D-sol-re.
</margin>

The interval is not conceived as beginning in A-re <note>the lowest note of the medieval musical scale</note>, where the first species of the <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"</note> takes its origin, even though the first species of the fifth itself is formed from that first species of the fourth plus a whole tone. This occurs for many reasons. 

First, if the first species of the fifth drew its beginning from A-re, the second species following it could not be found as a perfect species, because it would be composed of two whole tones and two semitones—namely, from B-mi to F-fa-ut <note>This describes a "diminished fifth," which was considered imperfect or dissonant in medieval theory</note>. Indeed, it would lack one major semitone of its total perfection, which requires three whole tones and one minor semitone. 

Second, its third species, which would occur from C-fa-ut to G-sol-re-ut, proceeding through the syllables *ut, re, mi, fa, sol*, would [incorrectly] be the fourth species. 

Third, the fourth species, which would occur from D-sol-re to A-la-mi-re, proceeding through the syllables *re, mi, fa, sol, la*, would [incorrectly] be the first. 

Fourth, if the first species of the fifth began in A-re, then one could not provide the first species of the fourth above it, which is required to be added to that first species of the fifth to establish the first rule of <term>plainchant</term> <note>original: "cantus plani"; traditional unison church music</note>—that is, the first <term>tone</term> or first <term>mode</term> <note>The Dorian mode, which is built from a fifth (D to A) and a fourth (A to D)</note>. 

Fifth, one could not have the first species of the fourth in the introductory order *below* the first species of the fifth, which is required by the second rule of plainchant or the second tone <note>The Hypodorian mode, which places the fourth below the fifth</note>. 

Therefore, the first species of the fifth has its beginning and foundation in D-sol-re and rises to A-la-mi-re; for wherever there is a similar arrangement of syllables—namely, *re, mi, fa, sol, la*—it is always the first species of the fifth, as seen here.

<summary>This page explains why the first species of the fifth must begin on the note D (D-sol-re) rather than A (A-re), arguing that starting on A would create dissonant intervals and disrupt the mathematical structure of the musical modes.</summary>

<keywords>fifth, diapente, fourth, diatessaron, species, musical modes, Dorian mode, solmization, D-sol-re, plainchant, medieval music theory</keywords>

The second species of the <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapentes"</note> is formed from the second species of the <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"</note> and a whole tone. It begins indeed in E-la-mi, where the fifth string of the philosophers was situated, and it ends by rising to high B-mi, which is proven to occupy the string [called] <term>paramese</term> <note>In ancient Greek music theory, the *paramese* was the note just above the middle string of the lyre.</note>.

When, however, there is a progression toward the further notes of the introductory scale <note>The "introductorium" refers to the hand-chart or basic scale used to teach beginners.</note>, beginning in the high **a-la-mi-re**, soon a proper species of the <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapẽtes"</note> presents itself. Indeed, the remaining species that follow do not succeed one another in a naturally disposed order because of many inconveniences that would follow—as was stated above—if the first species of the fifth were to begin in **A-re**, where the first string of the philosophers is located <note>In ancient Greek theory, the lowest note was Proslambanomenos, corresponding to the medieval A-re.</note>.

Therefore, the repetition of these four species resumes in **d-la-sol-re**, and there its first species takes its beginning, which is raised from the high **a-la-mi-re** up into the "acute" register where the fifteenth string of the philosophers—that is, the <term>nete hyperbolaion</term> <note>original: "netehypboleo"; the highest note of the Greater Perfect System in ancient Greek music theory.</note>—held its seat. --- Page 225 ---
<meta>Shelfmark: Magl. A.6.36. This page continues the discussion from page 101 regarding the structure of musical intervals. The previous page ended by discussing how the "acute" or high-pitched register begins at d-la-sol-re. This page explains the internal composition of the fifth (diapente) and how it can be broken down into various combinations of tones and semitones before introducing the octave (diapason).</meta>

...it possesses more intervals, and this is when it includes all its pitches, as here: **re**, **mi**, **fa**, **sol**, **la**. For between **re** and **mi** is one interval of a <term>tone</term> <note>original: "toni"; a whole step</note>. Likewise, between **mi** and **fa** is the second, namely the interval of a <term>minor semitone</term> <note>original: "semitonii minoris"; a half step</note>. Between **fa** and **sol** is the third, by which a tone is completed; but between **sol** and **la** is the fourth and last, which makes a tone.

Likewise, the <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"</note> can consist of three intervals, as by saying **re**, **fa**, **sol**, **la**. For between **re** and **fa** is the interval of a <term>semiditone</term> <note>original: "semiditonii"; a minor third, or a step-and-a-half</note> and the first interval; between **fa** and **sol** is the second, namely a tone; likewise between **sol** and **la** is the third, namely the interval of a tone. The fifth can equally be conceived in another way through three intervals, as for example by saying **re**, **mi**, **sol**, **la**. Likewise, there is another way by saying **re**, **fa**, **sol**, **la** <note>The author is demonstrating that the internal steps can be grouped differently while still spanning the same total distance.</note>.

Likewise, it is proven to be completed by two intervals, as by saying **re**, **sol**, **la** through the spaces of a <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"</note> and a tone. Likewise by saying **re**, **mi**, **la** through the spaces of a tone and a fourth. Furthermore, it is shown to consist of one single interval when **re-la** is spoken. 

Wherefore it should be noted that although the consonance of the fifth may consist of one, or two, or three intervals <note>meaning leaps rather than a continuous scale</note>, nevertheless there always lie three pitches between the two extremes. All of these, working together, always contain three tones and one minor semitone, from which the fifth itself is completed. For if it were otherwise, these extreme pitches could in no way correspond to each other as a fifth. That which was exemplified regarding the first species of the fifth is also to be understood without any doubt regarding the other species.

### On the species of the octave. Chapter 8.

<summary>This page analyzes the mathematical and melodic composition of the "diapente" (musical fifth), demonstrating how it is always composed of three tones and one semitone regardless of how the internal notes are grouped. It concludes by introducing the next topic: the species of the "diapason" (octave).</summary>
<keywords>diapente, fifth, intervals, solmization, re mi fa sol la, semiditone, diatessaron, tone, semitone, musical theory</keywords>

--- Page 226 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical breakdown of musical intervals, specifically moving from the fourth and fifth (discussed on the previous page) to the octave. The text transitions from the mathematical composition of the octave to its philosophical and cosmological implications before categorizing its "species" or modes.</meta>

The consonance of the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "Diapason"</note> is made of eight sounds, consisting of five <term>tones</term> <note>whole steps</note> and two <term>minor semitones</term> <note>half steps</note>. It is so named from *dia*, which means "from," and *pason*, "all," as it is a consonance consisting of all sounds. Indeed, as was shown in the first chapter, all sounds in the whole discipline of music are seven; when the first is repeated, this perfect symphony of the octave is completed. We have clearly treated the perfection and properties of this interval in the previous volumes.

For it is proven that the whole body of the world is moved by this harmony, as was touched upon in the second chapter of the first book; likewise, the whole human body is regulated by it, as can be considered in the third chapter of the first book. It is proven that all harmony entirely diverges from the consonance of the octave. Since the octave itself is made of a <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"</note> and a <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapente"</note>, and these have various species, the various species of the octave are seen to be joined in various ways.

For there are seven differing species of the octave. The first of these is formed from the first species of the fourth—stretching from **A-re** to **D-sol-re**—and from the first species of the fifth, held between **D-sol-re** and **A-la-mi-re**. Therefore, the first species of the octave proceeds from **A-re** to **A-la-mi-re**, that is, from the <term>proslambanomenos</term> <note>The lowest note of the Greek musical system, corresponding to A2</note> to the <term>mese</term> <note>The "middle" note of the Greek system, corresponding to A3</note>.

The second species of the octave is formed from the second species of the fourth, falling between **B-mi** and **E-la-mi** in the <term>grave</term> <note>low-pitched</note> register, and from the second species of the fifth, formed from **E-la-mi** grave to **B-mi** <term>acute</term> <note>high-pitched</note>. Therefore, the second species of the octave proceeds from **B-mi** to **B-mi**, that is, from the <term>hypatehypaton</term> <note>the "lowest of the low" notes in the Greek system, B2</note> to the <term>paramese</term> <note>the note "next to the middle," B3</note>.

The third species of the octave is formed from the third species of the fourth, made between **C-fa-ut** and **F-fa-ut** in the grave register, and from the third species of the fifth...

<summary>This page defines the octave (diapason) as the perfect consonance encompassing all seven notes of the musical scale and links its harmony to the movement of the cosmos and the human body. It begins a detailed classification of the seven "species" of the octave by combining different versions of the fourth and fifth intervals.</summary>
<keywords>octave, diapason, diatessaron, diapente, musica mundana, musica humana, species of the octave, Greek musical system, proslambanomenos, mese, solmization</keywords>

<note>In medieval music theory, a "species" refers to the specific arrangement of whole tones and semitones (half-steps) within an interval. Because the musical scale has half-steps in specific places (between B-C and E-F), a "fourth" starting on C sounds different than a "fourth" starting on D. These variations were categorized into numbered species.</note>

It demonstrates how different starting pitches result in different patterns of whole and half steps, which defined the character of the musical modes.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical classification of the species of the octave, specifically detailing the fifth, sixth, and seventh arrangements. It maintains the method of defining an octave by combining a specific species of a fifth (diapente) with a specific species of a fourth (diatessaron). It continues to correlate the medieval Guidonian solmization system (ut, re, mi, etc.) with the classical Greek pitch names.</meta>

The **fifth species of the <term>octave</term>** <note>original: "diapason"</note>, moreover, is formed from the **second species of the <term>fifth</term>** <note>original: "diapente"</note> located between low **E-la-mi** and high **B-mi**, or between the <term>hypatameson</term> <note>original: "hypatemeſon"; in the Greek system, the lowest note of the middle tetrachord, corresponding to E</note> and the <term>parameson</term> <note>original: "parameſen"; the pitch B, which serves as the "disjunction" between tetrachords</note>, and from the **second species of the <term>fourth</term>** <note>original: "diateſſarõ"</note> proceeding from high **B-mi** to high **E-la-mi**. Thus, the fifth species of the octave proceeds from low **E** to high **e**, or from the *hypatameson* to the <term>nete diezeugmenon</term> <note>original: "netediezeugmenon"; the highest note of the disjunct tetrachord, corresponding to e</note>.

The **sixth species of the octave**, however, is formed from the **third species of the fifth** which falls from low **F-fa-ut** to **c-sol-fa-ut**, or from the <term>parhypatameson</term> <note>original: "paripatemeſõ"; the note F in the Greek system</note> to the <term>trite diezeugmenon</term> <note>original: "tritediezeugmenõ"; the note c in the Greek system</note>, and from the **third species of the fourth** located between **c-sol-fa-ut** and [high] **F-fa-ut**, or between the *trite diezeugmenon* and the <term>trite hyperbolaion</term> <note>original: "tritehypboleon"; the third note of the highest tetrachord, corresponding to high f</note>. Thus, the sixth species of the octave falls from low **F-fa-ut** to high **F-fa-ut**, or from the *parhypatameson* to the *trite hyperbolaion*.

The **seventh and last species of the octave** is formed from the **fourth species of the fifth** made from low **G-sol-re-ut** to **D-la-sol-re**, and from the **first species of the fourth** led between **D-la-sol-re** and high **G-sol-re-ut**. Therefore, the seventh species of the octave occurs from low **G-sol-re-ut** to high **G-sol-re-ut**, or from the <term>lychanos meson</term> <note>original: "lycanoſmeſon"; the "indicator" note of the middle tetrachord, corresponding to G</note> to the <term>paranete hyperbolaion</term> <note>original: "paranetehypboleon"; the second-to-highest note of the highest tetrachord, corresponding to high g</note>, as is shown by these examples:

### Seventh species of the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "Septima ſpeties diapafon"</note>

<summary>This page defines the fifth and sixth species of the octave (starting on F and G) by identifying their component intervals of the fifth and fourth. It uses Guidonian solmization syllables to identify specific pitches and their functions within the medieval hexachord system.</summary>
<keywords>octave species, diapason, diapente, diatessaron, solmization, hexachord, music theory, F-fa-ut, G-sol-re-ut</keywords>

--- Page 231 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the classification of the seven species of the octave (completed on the previous page) to the broader grouping of these species into the four "maneries" or modes. It defines the "constitution" or tonal system as a combination of an octave and a fourth.</meta>

For it is established that the **octave** <note>original: "diapason"</note> consists of at most seven intervals—specifically when it contains all eight of its notes, as was stated at the end of the fourth book—likewise, it is recognized that it can be completed by six intervals, or five, or four, or three, or two, in various ways. It can even consist of a single interval when the outer notes do not catch any middle note between them <note>The author is explaining that while an octave scale has seven steps, a singer might leap across the entire span in a single "interval" or jump</note>, a matter I leave to the consideration of the diligent reader. For the ancients set forth these seven species of the octave in only four **modes** <note>original: "maneries"; a medieval term for the categories that would become the eight church modes</note>, the first of which they called **Protos**, the second **Deuterus**, the third **Tritus**, and the fourth **Tetrardus**. And indeed, these modes established boundaries for chants beyond which they could not be extended by any heightening or lowering of the voice. For this reason, these modes were called **constitutions** <note>original: "constitutiones"; a term for a complete scale or system of notes</note>, for each "constitution" encompassed an **octave** and a **fourth** <note>original: "diapason ac diatessaron"</note>, which is defined thus:

> A **Constitution** is a full body of melody, as it were, consisting of a joining of harmonies.

Therefore, the **first mode** or first constitution proceeded from **A-re** to **D-la-sol-re**, that is, from the *proslambanomenos* <note>The "added note," the lowest note in the Greek system, corresponding to A</note> to the *paranete diezeugmenon* <note>The "next-to-highest" note of the disjunct tetrachord, corresponding to d</note>. The **second mode** extended from the low **B-mi** to the high **E-la-mi**, or from the *hypate hypaton* <note>The "principal of the principals," corresponding to B</note> to the *nete diezeugmenon* <note>The "last" note of the disjunct tetrachord, corresponding to e</note>. The **third constitution** resided between **C-fa-ut** and the high **F-fa-ut**, or from the *parhypate hypaton* <note>The "next-to-principal" of the principals, corresponding to C</note> [to the *paranete hyperbolaion*].

<summary>This page explains how the seven species of the octave are grouped into four modes (Protos, Deuterus, Tritus, Tetrardus) and defines the "Constitution" as a tonal system spanning an octave and a fourth.</summary>
<keywords>octave, modes, maneries, Protos, Deuterus, Tritus, Tetrardus, Constitution, Greek pitch names, proslambanomenos</keywords>

--- Page 232 ---
<meta>The previous page discussed the first three "constitutions" or modal scales. This page concludes that list with the fourth constitution and then explains how the four ancient "maneries" (modes) were expanded into the system of eight modes (authentic and plagal) used in Gregorian chant. The catchword "sub" at the end of the previous page led into "tritehypboleon" here.</meta>

...and the **trite hyperbolaion** <note>original: "tritehypboleon"; the "third note of the highest tetrachord," corresponding to the modern note F</note>. The **fourth constitution** or **mode** <note>original: "maneries"</note> proceeded from **D-sol-re** to **G-sol-re-ut acute**, or from the **lychanos hypaton** <note>The "index finger note of the lowest tetrachord," corresponding to G</note> to the **paranete hyperbolaion** <note>The "next-to-highest note of the highest tetrachord," corresponding to G an octave higher</note>.

For every **plainchant** <note>original: "cantus planus"</note> was governed by these four rules or modes and was terminated at the fourth lower note where the **conjunction** <note>original: "synaphe"</note> joined the **fourth** <note>original: "diatessaron"</note> and the **octave** <note>original: "diapason"</note>. Indeed, the first mode terminated in **D-sol-re**, the second in **E-la-mi**, the third in **F-fa-ut**, and the fourth in the "grave" or lower **G-sol-re-ut**. Thus, these chants reached an **octave** <term>diapason: the interval of an eighth</term> above their final note, while they dropped a **fourth** <term>diatessaron: the interval of a fourth</term> below that same final.

Therefore, since such a great ascent from the final note, combined with a descent of four notes below that same final, was awkward and difficult for the singer, the foresight of musicians divided these four. They established that the modes should contain only a single **octave** raised up from their final note, constructed from a **fifth** <note>original: "diapente"</note> and a **fourth**, in such a way that the **fifth** is always the lower part while the **fourth** is the higher.

Furthermore, to these first four modes—arranged as a single octave in this order—they joined four others which would possess a **fourth** sinking below the final and a **fifth** extended upward from the final. When all of these are brought together, they are seen to fulfill an eightfold order. For their order stands thus:

**Protus** <note>The "First" mode</note> and its **plagal** <note>original: "subiugalis"; literally "yoked" or subordinate mode</note>.
**Deuterus** <note>The "Second" mode</note> and its **plagal**.
**Tritus** <note>The "Third" mode</note> and its **plagal**.
**Tetrardus** <note>The "Fourth" mode</note> and its **plagal**...

<summary>This page describes the transition from the four ancient Greek-influenced "constitutions" to the medieval system of eight church modes. It explains how the range of a chant was reorganized from an unwieldy combination of an octave and a fourth into more manageable "authentic" and "plagal" scales.</summary>

<keywords>plainchant, musical modes, maneries, Protus, Deuterus, Tritus, Tetrardus, plagal, octave, fourth, fifth, medieval music theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of modal structures. While the previous page focused on the four "constitutions" of chant and their relationship to the final notes, this page transitions to the "seven species of the octave," explaining how they are divided and how the classical modes are derived through the shifting of whole tones and semitones.</meta>

...yoked. But concerning these matters, I think it more appropriate to treat them thoroughly in other volumes which are assigned to **practical music** <note>original: "pratice musice"; the study of how music is actually performed and composed, as opposed to theoretical or mathematical speculation</note>.

Now, regarding the **seven species of the octave** <term>diapason: the interval of an eighth</term>, it should be noted that the first three species arranged above require the **consonance of the fourth** <term>diatessaron: the interval of a fourth</term> to be placed at the lower part in their composition. The remaining four, however, [require] the fourth itself to be raised above the **fifth** <term>diapente: the interval of a fifth</term>, as has been shown by these same reasons and examples.

For the philosophers called these seven species of the octave "modes" <note>original: "modos"</note> from "modulation," establishing them regularly as the boundaries of all chants. For the first constitution of the octave, made from the **proslambanomenos** <note>original: "a proslambanomenos"; the "acquired note," the lowest pitch in the Greek Greater Perfect System, equivalent to a low A</note> to the **mese** <note>original: "ad mesen"; the "middle note," an octave above the lowest note</note>, or from **A-re** to **a-la-mi-re acute** <note>Modern equivalent: from A2 to A3</note>, formed the first mode, which we call the **Hypodorian**. 

If, however, all the notes of this first species of octave are raised by a **whole tone** <note>original: "toni"</note>, the second mode will be formed, namely the **Hypophrygian**. Again, if all the notes of the Hypophrygian are raised by a **semitone** <note>original: "semitonio"</note>, the **Hypolydian** mode will be formed. If all the notes in the Hypolydian are raised by a whole tone, they will make the **Dorian**. When all the notes of the Dorian again receive a raising of a whole tone, they will constitute the **Phrygian**. But if the notes of the Phrygian are raised by a semitone, they will create the **Lydian**. Indeed, if these notes of the Lydian are raised by a whole tone, they will produce the **Mixolydian**. These seven modes [are] the same...

<summary>This page defines the seven "species of the octave" based on the arrangement of fourths and fifths, and details the derivation of the seven classical modes (Hypodorian through Mixolydian) by ascending step-wise through the scale.</summary>
<keywords>octave species, diapason, diatessaron, diapente, Hypodorian, Dorian, Phrygian, Lydian, Mixolydian, music theory, Boethius</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the Greek modal system (the "tonoi" or "modes") as interpreted by medieval theorists. It picks up mid-sentence from the previous page, which began to explain the Hypophrygian mode by shifting the notes of the Hypodorian mode up by a whole tone.</meta>

...[which] obtain the voices and strings which the first **species of the octave** <term>diapason: the interval of an eighth</term> holds—namely, from the **proslambanomenos** <note>original: "proslambanomenos"; the "acquired note," the lowest pitch in the Greek Greater Perfect System, equivalent to a low A</note> to the **mese** <note>original: "mese"; the "middle note," an octave above the lowest note</note>. 

But as the order of the introduction naturally proceeds in the **diatonic genus** <term>diatonic genus: a musical scale that moves primarily by whole tones and semitones</term>, one [mode] exceeds the other in pitch by a whole tone or a semitone. Whence the **proslambanomenos** of the **Hypophrygian** (or **A-re**) surpasses the one that belongs to the **Hypodorian** in pitch by a **whole tone** <note>original: "tono"</note>. Similarly, the **mese** and the **a-la-mi-re** <note>The medieval pitch name for the note A3</note> of that same Hypophrygian surpasses the note in the Hypodorian by that same whole tone. 

From this, the remaining intervening voices and the entire order of the Hypodorian are surpassed by a whole tone by the intervening voices and the entire order of the Hypophrygian. This same order holds for the others, which any diligent student can investigate for themselves. 

Since, therefore, these seven modes—established according to the seven species of the octave—always retain the same voices and strings, differing among themselves only in pitch <note>original: "acumine tantum & grauitate"; literally "sharpness and gravity"</note>, **Ptolemy** <note>original: "Phtolemeus"; refers to Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century mathematician who influentiallly reorganized the Greek system of modes</note> not inappropriately added an eighth mode to them. Musicians call this the **Hypermixolydian**. Its order surpasses the order of the **Mixolydian** in pitch by a whole tone. I have arranged for these to be distinguished figuratively, using the first letters fixed in the introduction, in this manner:

<meta>This page continues the discussion of musical modes and intervals, moving from the etymology of "trope" to the definition of "tone" and the mathematical construction of the octave from the fourth and fifth.</meta>

And indeed these <term>modes</term> <note>The 'modes' are the predecessor to modern musical scales.</note> are called "tropes" because of the "turning" <note>original: "conversio"</note> they undergo as they pass from low pitch to high pitch, creating one from another. For <term>tropos</term> in Greek is called "conversion" in Latin; hence, according to **Boethius**, <note>Boethius (c. 480–524 AD) was the most influential musical authority of the Middle Ages.</note> "Tropes are arrangements in whole systems of pitches, differing in either low or high pitch." We later writers, however, call these modes **tones**, as if they were generated from <term>tones and semitones</term>. Musicians are accustomed to define them thus: A "tone" is a rule which, through the rising and falling of the melody, determines all chants at their conclusion. <note>In medieval music, the "final" note of a chant determines its mode or 'tone'.</note> But I judge that these should be passed over in silence here, since they belong more to <term>practical music</term> than to <term>speculative music</term>. <note>Speculative music was the study of the mathematical and philosophical laws of sound; practical music was the study of performance and composition.</note> A treatise on them—already detailed in the sixth book of *The Flower of Music* and in the fourth discussion of the book of *Discussions*—will be explained more clearly in its proper place in the second part of this work, which will be devoted to practice.

Now, however, we return to the <term>diapason</term> <note>The octave interval.</note> consonance, which, as has been shown, is perfected by the <term>diatessaron</term> <note>The interval of a perfect fourth.</note> and <term>diapente</term> <note>The interval of a perfect fifth.</note> joined together. Yet, this must be noted in these matters: although a fourth placed in a lower position and a fifth joined to it in a higher part may resonate as a perfect octave consonance at the outer pitches, the middle sound—which completes the fourth relative to the lower pitch and begins the fifth toward the high pitch—does not <unclear>render</unclear> to the ears a perfect...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of how the octave (diapason) is divided by a middle pitch to create harmony. It specifically addresses the "harmonic division" where the larger interval (the fifth) is placed at the bottom, explaining why this arrangement sounds more pleasing to the ear than its inverse.</meta>

...it adds to the <term>harmony</term> <note>original: "armoniam"</note> as if the middle tone itself stretches
into the high pitch, where it then completes a <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapenten"; an interval spanning five notes.</note> toward the lower
part and a <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "diatessaron"; an interval spanning four notes.</note> toward the higher part.
For this happens to occur because the <term>fourth</term>,
since it consists of four notes, mixes dissonance to the
ears by itself according to the art of <term>counterpoint</term>. <note>original: "contrapuncti"; the practice of combining independent melodic lines.</note>
The <term>fifth</term>, however, being of five notes, leads
to the best concord. Therefore, when between two
extreme sounds corresponding to the <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason"</note> consonance,
a middle sound is placed, completing a <term>fifth</term>
toward the lower and a <term>fourth</term> toward the
higher, they will add a perfect harmony to the ears when struck
together; and the dissonance of the four
notes which the middle leads to the higher extreme
is not perceived by the ears. This is because the lower
sound sounds a perfect <term>fifth</term> consonance with the middle.
The same lower sound sounds the best <term>octave symphony</term> <note>original: "diapason siphoniam"</note> with the
higher, the sweet quality of which dulls and
blunts the dissonance of those four notes
which the middle leads toward the extreme, so much so that the
discord itself is not perceived by the ears
on account of the tiny portion it was granted
and the weakness of the high pitch; for a high sound
is weaker than a low one. It is otherwise, however, if
the middle fulfills the mixture of four notes
or <term>fourth</term> toward the lower pitch, and the <term>fifth</term>... <note>The text ends mid-sentence; the catchword "dia" suggests the next page begins with "diapente".</note>

<summary>This page explains the psychoacoustics of the harmonic division of an octave, arguing that placing a perfect fifth below a perfect fourth creates a superior harmony because the stronger consonances mask the inherent dissonance of the fourth.</summary>
<keywords>harmony, fifth, fourth, octave, counterpoint, consonance, dissonance, acoustics, pitch perception</keywords>

--- Page 238 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of acoustic theory and harmonic intervals. The previous page ended while explaining why the dissonance of a fourth (diatessaron) is less perceptible in higher registers due to the "weakness" of high pitches and the speed of their vibrations.</meta>

[fifth] toward the higher. For it would be perceived to be more dissonant because the <term>tenor</term> <note>original: "tenor"; the foundation voice in medieval polyphony that "holds" the melody.</note> or low sound, on account of the slowness which it has in itself—as was declared in the first part of the second [book]—retains more time than the high sound, which is produced by faster motions. 

Where there is more time and slowness, discord can be more extensive, and consequently the dissonance is more perceived, as <term>Aristotle</term> says in the 19th <note>original: "zi." (likely shorthand for 19th)</note> problem of his *Musical Problems* <note>original: "pblemate sue musices"; referring to the "Problemata" attributed to Aristotle, a foundational text for medieval science.</note>, to which also concurs the explanation of the Lord <term>Reconciliator</term> <note>original: "reconciliatoris"; referring to Pietro d'Abano (c. 1257–1316), a famous physician and philosopher known as "the Conciliator" for his attempts to resolve contradictions between medicine and philosophy.</note>. Therefore, due to the greater duration of time, the low sound is shown to be slow and ample; its nature becomes manifest here because of its slowness. Although the high sound is rendered fast in a smaller amount of time. 

Therefore, the dissonance of the <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "q̄ttuor uocū"; literally "of four voices," referring to the interval of a fourth (diatessaron), which was often considered a dissonance in certain contrapuntal contexts.</note> is hidden because of the speed of the high sounds. With these three sounds standing thus, if the low sound were raised by a <term>major third</term> <note>original: "dyttonum"; an interval spanning two whole tones.</note> or a <term>minor third</term> <note>original: "seīdyttonum"; an interval spanning a tone and a semitone.</note>, which is called a "third" in <term>counterpoint</term> <note>original: "contrapuncto"; the art of composing multiple melodic lines against each other.</note>, it will effect an <term>imperfect consonance</term> <note>original: "concordātia ipscā" (imperfecta); intervals like thirds and sixths that sound pleasing but were considered less stable than "perfect" intervals like octaves or fifths.</note>: a third to the middle sound, which is called the <term>contratenor</term> <note>original: "contratenor"; the voice part written "against" the tenor.</note>, to which it used to sound a <term>fifth</term> <note>original: "diapētẽ siue qntā"; an interval of five notes.</note> before it took on the tension of that third, and a <term>sixth</term> <note>original: "sextam"</note> to the highest note, to which it previously responded as an <term>octave</term> <note>original: "diapason... id est octauam"</note>. 

For the third and the sixth are species of imperfect consonances in counterpoint. And thus, that lower sound itself, by establishing a third to the middle and a sixth to the highest, by the same reasoning blunts the dissonance of the <term>fourth</term> <note>original: "q̄rtā"</note> between that middle and the higher extreme, extinguishing the discord.

<summary>This page discusses the physics of sound, arguing that lower pitches (tenors) are slower and more likely to reveal dissonance than faster, higher pitches. It applies this logic to explain how "imperfect" intervals like thirds and sixths can be used in counterpoint to mask the perceived dissonance of a fourth.</summary>
<keywords>acoustics, Aristotle, Pietro d'Abano, tenor, contratenor, third, sixth, fourth, counterpoint, consonance</keywords>

O you who have been permitted to attain the cardinal honor,
And me, whom virtue has compelled to revere you:
Receive these gifts, though they be small and hardly worthy 
Of so great a man, in whose name this work rises. 
For no reader will judge these things small 
If you yourself pluck the fruit of my study. 
Come now: embrace both me and the book with a grateful countenance, 
Nor let it be a burden to say: "You shall be my servant."

### To the most dreaded <margin>respected</margin> Prince Alfonso, from Joannes Capaniz of San Cinmonte

> I have read through the verses you sent me, most respected father,
> which were filled with such great weight.
> Not such songs did the Rhodopeian hero <note>original: "rhodopeius heros"; a reference to Orpheus, the legendary Greek musician who hailed from the Rhodope Mountains.</note> sing at home,
> who made the very houses of the underworld stand amazed with his strings.
> Nor such songs did he sing who established the walls of Thebes,
> Amphion, while he played upon his learned lyre. <note>In Greek mythology, Amphion's music was so powerful it moved stones to build the city walls.</note>
> But [these are] like those David sang by day, or such as he sang by night,
> while he sang your praises, O kind Christ.
> Wherefore as a suppliant I pray, while time allows, that you
> write many more things from your learned heart.

---

### Landinus on the *Bucolics*
<note>Cristoforo Landino (1424–1498) was an influential Italian Renaissance humanist. This excerpt likely comes from his commentary on Virgil’s "Bucolics" (pastoral poems).</note>

**Number** is the composition of discrete quantity <note>A mathematical term for things that can be counted as individual units, such as integers, rather than measured as a continuous flow.</note> and is understood as order, consonance, and modulation.

<note>Annotation on musical/mathematical proportions in a more cursive hand</note>
Note: In the <term>superparticular genus</term> <note>original: "superparticulari genere"; a category of mathematical ratios where the larger number contains the smaller number plus one part of it (e.g., 3:2, 4:3).</note> because it is manifold, any proportion can be said to be <term>epipritus</term> <note>original: "epipritus"; the Greek term for a 4:3 ratio, known in music as the interval of a perfect fourth.</note> remaining.

---

### Boethius
<note>Anicius Manlius Severinus Boethius (c. 477–524 AD), whose work "De institutione musica" was the standard music theory textbook for over a thousand years.</note>

The <term>multiplex</term> <note>A ratio where the larger number is a whole multiple of the smaller (e.g., 2:1, 3:1).</note> is the first genus because it begins from unity.
The <term>superparticular</term> is the second because it begins from the number two, namely
[in] genus and species I will provide myself in prose <note>original: "In pedestri"; literally "on foot," referring to unmetered prose writing as opposed to poetry.</note>.

<summary>This page features a celebratory poem comparing the recipient's writing to the divine songs of David and the mythical music of Orpheus, followed by technical definitions of musical proportions from Landino and Boethius.</summary>
<keywords>Orpheus, Amphion, King David, Landino, Boethius, musical proportions, superparticular ratio, discrete quantity, Renaissance humanism</keywords>

### Persius
<note>Aulus Persius Flaccus (34–62 AD) was a Roman satirist. The line quoted here is a variation of the prologue to his Satires.</note>

You might believe [him] to sing a Pegasean song <note>original: "pegaseum melos"; a reference to the Muses and the winged horse Pegasus, whose hoof-strike created the fountain of poetic inspiration.</note>
<gloss>Poliziano <note>Angelo Poliziano (1454–1494), a brilliant Renaissance scholar.</note> says "nectar" and not "song" [melos], because "song" never had its first syllable lengthened. <note>original: "politanius dicit nectar et non melos que numque melos habuit prima productam"; Poliziano is making a technical correction regarding Latin poetic meter.</note></gloss>

### Verses of the Bells
<note>original: "versus Campani"; "Campana" is the Latin word for a large church bell. These traditional verses were frequently inscribed on bells to describe their various functions in medieval life.</note>

<unclear>I praise the true God</unclear>, I put the plague to flight, I gather the clergy.
<unclear>I mourn the deceased</unclear>, I plead to celebrate the Mass.
"Voice" [vox] is <unclear>said to be</unclear> from "calling" [vocando] or from "shouting" [boo]. <note>A classic example of medieval etymology, attempting to find the roots of words in their sounds or actions.</note>

---

A **key** [clavis] is so called because it closes [claudat]; its inventor was Theodorus of Samos <note>An ancient Greek architect and inventor from the 6th century BC.</note> in the north.

A **song** [canticum] is [performed] without sweet poetry, or it is a modulated chant.

**Voice** is the most delicate striking of the air —
<margin>Priscian, Book 1. <note>Priscian was a famous Latin grammarian of the late Roman Empire whose works were foundational to medieval education.</note></margin>
Voice is its own sensation for the ears.
